CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
A
ircraft maintenance is a crucial process aimed at preventing aerial
accidents. This chapter overviews the essential concepts of this process
and delves into moving from scheduled maintenance to Condition-Based
Maintenance ( CBM) and Predictive Maintenance ( PdM) strategies using
Artificial Intelligence ( AI) capabilities and data analysis techniques. Given
the availability of data on aircraft systems’ health degradation, this chapter
primarily focuses on the challenges of estimating their Remaining Useful
Life ( RUL) using Machine Learning ( ML) methods. The adoption of those
data-driven methods for developing PdM solutions is motivated by the following
key factors:
1. The predictive maintenance market is emerging in the aviation industry,
given The European Union A viation Safety Agency ( EASA) integrated
Aircraft Health Monitoring into the regulatory basis for aircraft
maintenance (IMRBPB, 2018);
2. The increasing use of onboard sensors and Aircraft Condition Monitoring
Systems ( ACMSs) facilitates engineers in gathering condition monitoring
data from aircraft systems and components ( Lee et al. , 2023); and,
3. The popularity of developing data-driven models using ML methods
stems from their ability to extract valuable insights from vast amounts
of data without requiring engineers to completely understand the health
monitoring environment ( Berthelsen et al. , 2021).
2.1 Introduction
Deficient maintenance policies have been negatively impacting the profitability
of business sectors that incur significant expenses in machinery maintenance,
especially those with high-availability requirements ( IBM, 2018). One such
sector is commercial air transport, which has recognized the need to transition
from traditional scheduled maintenance to more advanced approaches, thereby
embracing the transformative paradigm named Aircraft Health Management
(AHM) ( Budeanu & Markou , 2022). Commercial aviation aims to enhance
operational efficiency, reduce costs, and improve overall aircraft maintenance
practices by adopting this paradigm that comprises multiple stages, from data
sensing to maintenance decision-making.
In the way of prioritizing the advances in predictive model development,
this chapter is organized as follows. Section 2.2 firstly reviews the evolution
of aircraft maintenance. This review highlights the needs associated with
delving into AHM as an alternative concept of the maintenance of the future
whose promises are based on CBM and PdM. Section 2.3 presents Prognostics
and Health Management ( PHM) as a refined discipline dedicated to assessing
health condition machinery by applying various model development approaches
for CBM and PdM. Finally, in Section 2.4, these approaches, especially
data-driven ones, are adopted to explore the RUL estimation of aircraft systems
— 12 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
by concentrating the efforts on the Aircraft Health Monitoring technique. This
technique focuses on exploring AI model development stages instead of assessing
maintenance decision-making.
2.2 Aircraft health management
Airline operators, including original equipment manufacturerss, aircraft
maintenance, repair and operations, and owner/lessors, all share the ambition
of achieving up to 99% aircraft technical availability. With this vision,
Aircraft Health Management ( AHM) emerges as a unified capability that
monitors aircraft health by analyzing physical parameters such as pressures,
temperatures, vibrations, and others to predict maintenance needs and trigger
efficient and effective maintenance actions. More specifically, AHM focuses
on implementing data-driven maintenance strategies to support technicians in
evaluating the airworthiness of the aircraft or its ability to perform operations
safely. By diligently addressing all maintenance requirements identified through
data-driven evaluations, the aircraft becomes available, enabling it to generate
revenue through flight operations.
Aircraft health management significantly impacts all aviation professionals
involved in the aircraft life cycle, encompassing various individuals, from
designers and stakeholders to maintenance staff and operators. Therefore, its
implementation requires an initial qualification process 1 and ongoing efforts
to maintain that qualification, e.g., incorporating in-service experience and
data-driven revisions.
For a better understanding of the ambitions and the qualification process
of AHM, it is necessary to review some concepts related to the evolution of
aircraft maintenance throughout the creation of different Maintenance Steering
Groups ( MSGs), the approval of Aircraft Maintenance Programs ( AMPs) and
the aircraft maintenance taxonomy.
2.2.1 Evolution of aircraft maintenance
In the early days of aviation, maintenance primarily consisted of corrective
actions without any analytical basis, where equipment used to be repaired after
it broke down or presented failures. At that moment, service maintenance, e.g.,
lubrication or cleaning, was insufficient to prevent catastrophic events.
The formation of airlines created the need to regulate and standardize aircraft
maintenance practices ( McLoughlin, 2018). As a response, a first regulation
effort delegated manufacturers responsible for the maintenance program. This
fact coincides with the entry of Boeing 707 and Douglas DC-8 airliners in the U.S.
in the late 1950s, when their manufacturers used to overhaul every component
at a given time.
This maintenance logic, dependent on manufacturers’ intervention, was
then substituted by a regulated and non-manufacturer-dependent preventive
1Qualification process of AHM: regulations, certifications, and procedures and others.
— 13 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
maintenance approach with the creation of the first Maintenance Steering Group
also known as MSG-1.
2.2.1.1 Maintenance Steering Groups (MSG-1, MSG-2 and MSG-3)
Maintenance Steering Groups ( MSGs) are documents that determine
appropriate times to overhaul aircraft components ( Skybrary, 2018). These
documents have been crafted by leading aircraft manufacturers and industry
experts. Their purpose is to serve as a comprehensive guide for developing
scheduled maintenance operations that meet the stringent standards and
requirements set forth by regulatory authorities, operators, and manufacturers
alike.
Three MSGs standards, MSG-1, MSG-2, and MSG-3, have been published
throughout aviation history. Those standard versions moved from purely
scheduled-based philosophy to process-based and task-based ones ( Budeanu &
Markou, 2022). That transition, illustrated in Figure 2.1, helped to diversify the
following control processes used nowadays.
• Hard Time refers to the interval conferred to the component until
performing maintenance interventions. Typically associated with the
component’s lifetime, this time interval is used to schedule overhauls.
• On Condition does not run the component unit until its failure 2 because
it does repetitive inspections during its lifetime. Condition tests and
analyses determine the need for maintenance interventions.
• Condition Monitoring is reserved for units whose failure does not affect
operational safety because they can run until its failure. Therefore, those
units are monitored during their lifetime but irregularly.
• Zonal Inspection Programs regularly inspect aircraft components
according to different criteria, e.g., corrosion protection. Those programs
emerged as an alternative for condition monitoring.
The first Maintenance Steering Group, also known as MSG-13, was developed
in 1968 by the Air Transport Association ( ATA) Maintenance Steering
Group, in collaboration with The U.S Federal A viation Administration ( F AA)
representatives, airframe manufacturers, airlines, and suppliers. The MSG-1
introduced scheduled maintenance practices, putting them into practice in the
Boeing 747 series.
The MSG-24 introduced condition monitoring with a process-based
maintenance philosophy. This second MSG standard was developed in the 1970s
with the theory that aircraft and their items reach a period when they are
overhauled and restored to new conditions.
In 1978, United Airlines, commissioned by The U.S. Department of Defense,
designed a task-based methodology for designing maintenance programs based
2Component failure: component inability to perform its functional role.
3MSG-1: Maintenance Evaluation and Program Development.
4MSG-2: Airline/Manufacturer Maintenance Program Planning
— 14 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
Figure 2.1 : Scheduled maintenance evolution throughout MSGs standards.
on airline practices. This methodology, which analyzes failure modes at a system
level, was the base of the current MSG-35 standard.
The first MSG-3 standard issue was published in 1980. MSG-3 differentiates
from previous MSGs in three main aspects: 1) described a top-down approach
that focuses on consequences of failures, 2) clarified the differences between
condition monitoring, on condition and hard time control processes, and, 3)
introduced zonal inspection programs related to structural health monitoring
systems.
Until now, this task-based methodology, last updated in 2018 under MSG-3,
has been instrumental in enhancing aircraft reliability—defined as the likelihood
that an item, component, system, or aircraft will fulfill its intended function
without failures over a specified period and under specified conditions ( NASA,
2017). Unfortunately, MSG-4 has been claimed because scheduled maintenance
has proven suboptimal, mainly because aircraft technical availability has been
pursued at any cost.
2.2.1.2 Aircraft reliability and continuing airworthiness
Aircraft technical availability, or where all required maintenance is accomplished
to ensure the aircraft becomes airworthy, relies foremost on aircraft reliability.
Computing this last one is complex because it covers a vast array of metrics,
data gathering, and decisions based on this, but also because the theoretical
reliability of manufacturers is not considered during operations ( Budeanu &
Markou, 2022).
The highest aircraft reliability corresponds to the designed-in reliability.
Manufacturers notify lower reliability because they can not achieve the
designed-in reliability due to production deviations. In practice, lower
reliability values are measured, while increases above the threshold given by
the production-generated reliability should not be expected.
Aircraft maintenance life starts once the first production test flight is
performed, despite the aircraft reliability and Aircraft Maintenance Program
(AMP) gain focus when the aircraft is delivered to its operator. Therefore,
operators based in EU nations and others who follow the EASA regulations
usually aim to accomplish continuing airworthiness—defined as all of the
5MSG-3: Operator/Manufacturer Scheduled Maintenance Development
— 15 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
processes ensuring that, at any time in its operating life, the aircraft complies
with the airworthiness requirements in force and is in a condition for safe
operation ( MAA, 2014). Airlines aim to accomplish those processes for being
reviewed and certified by the continuing airworthiness management organization
(CAMO).
For operators located outside the EU, adherence to their respective
continuing airworthiness regulations is common, e.g., Continuous Airworthiness
Maintenance Program (CAMP) adherence in the case of operators regulated by
The U.S. F AA. Therefore, it is essential to emphasize that AMP approval holds
paramount significance for all operators, regardless of location.
2.2.1.3 Aircraft Maintenance Program
Aircraft Maintenance Programs ( AMPs) based on scheduled maintenance have
demonstrated the need for transformations because airlines aim to reduce the
operational costs,≈9%, dedicated to their maintenance activities ( IATA, 2017).
Figure 2.2 illustrates the organizations approving novel maintenance programs.
When a new aircraft model is designed and produced, Industry Steering
Commite ( ISC) and Maintenance Steering Group ( MSG) are formed. Those
groups, consisting of manufacturers, airlines, and regulation authorities
representatives, repeatedly meet to determine the frequency and the scope
of maintenance inspections. Those determinations are used by Maintenance
Review Boards ( MRBs), composed of regulatory authorities delegations, to
emit final recommendations to manufacturers through writhing a Maintenance
Review Board Report ( MRBR). The manufacturer uses this information to
create a Maintenance Planning Document ( MPD), which the operator follows
in the case of small private aircraft. In the case of airlines, engineers review
in-service data to redact a suited AMP. Finally, MRB approves this program
in terms of airworthiness when airworthiness management organizations return
operators an approved maintenance program.
Figure 2.2 : An overview of organizations endorsing maintenance programs.
Operators and manufacturers use in-service data collection and analysis to
promote changes in maintenance programs. Boeing, e.g., uses in-service airline
maintenance data in ISC sessions to continuously improve customer AMPs
(McLoughlin, 2018).
2.2.1.4 Aircraft Maintenance Taxonomy
Delving into the taxonomy of aircraft maintenance is a highly intricate endeavor
because the maintenance activities of commercial aeroplanes vary extensively
— 16 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
across systems, components, and series. Maintenance must be understood as a
multilayered domain because aircraft elements require a diversified treatment.
Figure 2.3 illustrates a non-exhaustive list of maintenance categories by creating
four layers and four maintenance groups.
The groups, identified by different box colors, refer to the location and
complexity, planning granularity, threshold-based, and nature of the activity
concerning the business strategy. On the other hand, the maintenance layers
refer to the purpose, regularity, planning, and decision support strategy.
Figure 2.3 : Aircraft maintenance: groups and categories.
By looking at the location and the complexity of activity execution during
the aircraft’s operational life, the following main categories emerge:
• Depot Maintenance refers to cases when an aircraft needs significant
repairs and is sent to a depot. This category typically includes heavy
repairs such as part replacements, and lighter tasks, like cleaning and
polishing the aircraft exterior.
• Service Maintenance is conducted regularly, depending on the aircraft’s
needs. This category comprises routine inspections, aircraft systems
repairs, and replacements such as light bulbs and landing gears.
• Line Maintenance responds to the airline’s regular pre and post-flight
inspections. This maintenance is performed by trained technicians who
work for the airline or an outside company. Examples of these operations
are troubleshooting, component replacement, and lubrication.
• Base Maintenance is also performed by airline or outside company
technicians, but the activities are executed after the aircraft is taken out
of service. This category includes, e.g., corrosion repair and replacement
of parts that achieve the end of their lifespan.
The planning granularity or comprehensiveness level encompasses two
strategies: scheduled and non-scheduled. In a scheduled approach, varying
periodic inspection intervals are specified in AMPs (Quantas, 2016). Conversely,
with a non-scheduled approach, aircraft can remain operational, and overhauls
are performed only once a predefined threshold is reached. When such thresholds
are defined, two primary categories emerge: Reliability-Centered Maintenance
and condition-based maintenance ( Budeanu & Markou , 2022)
• Reliability-Centered Maintenance optimizes maintenance programs
by establishing safe minimum levels of equipment upkeep.
— 17 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
• Condition-Based Maintenance employs predictive models to
immediately alert maintenance needs when the equipment’s health has
degraded until an undesired threshold.
Finally, according to the nature of the activity concerning the business
strategy adopted, corrective, preventive, and predictive maintenance strategies
emerge:
• Corrective Maintenance primarily involves repairing the equipment
after it presents failures or breaks down.
• Preventive Maintenance repeatedly conducts tests and overhauls to
restore aircraft reliability.
• Predictive Maintenance employs predictive models to efficiently set
maintenance activity times. This strategy tries to predict the useful
lifetime of aircraft components or systems at a certain time step to indicate
the point in the future where maintenance has to be performed ( Biggio &
Kastanis, 2020).
Because current maintenance practices, mainly scheduled-based, spend
a significant percent of operational costs ( IATA, 2017), Aircraft Health
Management is focusing on promoting the development of decision
support-based strategies. This fact is also influenced by the enormous
potentiality of AI and the increasing incorporation of monitoring sensors in
modern aircraft.
2.2.2 Maintenance of the future
High costs emerge from the fact that airlines mostly perform maintenance
interventions using preventive maintenance strategies and avoid reactive
maintenance ones. While reactive maintenance repairs or substitutes a
component or system only when it fails and can no longer operate, preventive
maintenance intervenes at regular intervals to minimize the probability of
failures ( Yam, 2001). Unfortunately, repeatedly conducting inspections is no
longer cost-effective in guaranteeing aircraft reliability. Thus, stakeholders
adopt more conservative strategies, e.g., service, line, and scheduled checks.
However, despite A and C checks may seem simple because machinery checks are
performed at regular intervals, they can be costly, as empirical data has shown
that over 70% of scheduled maintenance checks do not detect any issues, leading
to unnecessary replacement of components or systems to ensure airworthiness
(MAA, 2014). To reduce high maintenance costs and Aircraft On Ground
(AOG), Condition-Based Maintenance ( CBM) and Predictive Maintenance
(PdM) strategies have emerged, where AOG refers to having serious problems
that obligate preventing an aircraft from flying.
Figure 2.4 illustrates the advantages of CBM and PdM using two criteria:
aircraft availability and maintenance cost. Aircraft availability refers to the
vehicle not being in the hangar due to operational downtime, while maintenance
cost relates to the resources spent. By using scheduled strategies, the probability
— 18 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
of failures is partially reduced and thus AOG. However, overhauls are
performed even when the aircraft does not present failure advents by conducting
unnecessary checks. In fact, ( Airbus, 2018) announced that 90% of aircraft
ground time does not change the aircraft’s condition. Therefore, reducing high
maintenance costs is emergent.
Figure 2.4 : Maintenance strategies: aircraft availability and associated costs.
Using CBM and PdM strategies, operating downtime and costs are considered.
However, both strategies differ in action performed after the determination time.
CBM intervenes immediately after a failure is detected, triggered by crossing
a predetermined threshold, while PdM executes maintenance activities based
on the equipment’s useful lifetime. Thus, PdM inevitably results in lower
maintenance costs as compared to CBM. In fact, PdM promises to enable
airlines to save around $3B per year in maintenance costs ( MAA, 2014).
2.2.2.1 From Aircraft Health Management to Aircraft Health Monitoring
The introduction of Aircraft Health Management ( AHM) as an alternative
concept of aircraft “maintenance of the future” is based on monitoring physical
parameters like pressures, temperatures, vibration, and others in a time
series data to predict the aircraft’s health state and trigger effective and
efficient maintenance actions. This concept, resumed in five verbs (sense,
acquire, transfer, analyze and act), has the challenge of moving from scheduled
maintenance influenced from MSG-3 to CBM and PdM by driving to MSG-4.
The main transformations associated with this way could be summarized in
Figure 2.5, which also illustrates the transition from MSG-1 and MSG-2 to
MSG-3.
Similarly to the process-based to task-based philosophy transition, moving
to a data-driven one also requires deep transformations in qualification
processes, which could imply the development of MSG-4. Furthermore, because
technological advances in zonal inspections and damage tolerances allowed
the MSG-3 advent, technological advances regarding data analysis techniques
are also expected to exploit the benefits of CBM and PdM. Therefore, to
explore the potential of data-driven approaches, Aircraft Health Monitoring
— 19 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
Figure 2.5 : Towards the desired future by looking at the maintenance evolution.
gains importance due to being a technique that limits management exploration
to sense, acquire, transfer and analyze stages.
Condition monitoring plays an essential role in the development of Aircraft
Health Monitoring, mainly in the data acquisition stage. This is because it
adopts a “run-to-failure” philosophy, where aircraft systems or components
are measured under a non-preventive maintenance approach. In other words,
the component/system can remain in service until a functional failure occurs,
allowing technicians to measure the failure degradation trajectory.
To locate aircraft components/systems in early health degradation or
functional failure stages, AHM appeals to measure one or more condition
indicators along their lifetime. Here, a condition indicator refers to the
result produced by an algorithm that combines one or more features of a
component or system to represent its health state ( MAA, 2014). Unfortunately,
gathering condition indicators in functional failure stages has been challenging
for components/systems whose failure can compromise the passenger’s safety,
recurring to digital twins as potential failure simulators and data providers.
2.2.2.2 Digital twins
The aerospace industry has shown keen interest in virtual replicas of physical
entities, achieved by merging data with ML and software analytics to develop
digital models that evolve alongside their real-world counterparts ( Rolls-Royce,
2019). Such virtual replicas are essential in aviation due to the data-rich nature
of aircraft operations. In Aircraft Health Management, academia and start-ups
have needed digital twins to model a more significant number of potential
circumstances than physical aircraft tests would ever allow.
The National Aeronautics and Space Administration - USA ( NASA) was
the first precursor of this technology from the early days of space exploration
(M. Liu, Wang & Liu , 2020). Thanks to the wide acceptance of Commercial
Modular Aero-Propulsion System Simulation ( C-MAPSS) from NASA, the
health degradation of turbofan engines is being extensively and publicly
explored, even using input jet data to simulate their degradation ( Arias Chao et
al., 2021). Besides the twins of aircraft engines, digital twins for rolling bearings
are also publicly famous because aircraft and other high-industrial equipment
comprise rotating machinery.
In product development, manufacturing, monitoring, and support,
— 20 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
Rolls-Royce, General Electric (GE), and Pratt & Whitney are among the most
privately advanced users of digital twin technologies today ( DHL, 2019). While
Rolls-Royce and Pratt & Whitney create virtual copies of pioneering aero engines
such as UltraFan or F119 turbofan, GE makes twins based on operational data
of components 6 or systems 7 (GE, 2022).
2.3 Prognostics and health management
Prognostics and Health Management ( PHM) is an advanced discipline focused
on evaluating the health condition of mechanical and infrastructure assets
(Atamuradov et al. , 2018). When it comes to mechanical assets, its main
objective is to offer valuable insights to technicians, empowering them to make
informed decisions about machine maintenance. As a technology, PHM employs
data analytical techniques, statistics, and physics for solving the following
problems:
• Failure prognostics primarily detects an eventual fault 8, classifying it
according to its specific type and/or estimates its impact on the machine
performance ( Biggio & Kastanis , 2020).
• Failure prognosis forecast how long the monitored unit will be able to
work in the presence of a fault ( Ramezani, Moini & Riahi , 2019).
In the way of addressing the challenges of failure prognostics and prognosis,
PHM focuses on the development of predictive models aligned with the principles
of CBM and PdM. By integrating these predictive models, PHM facilitates a
proactive approach to maintenance, enabling optimized decision-making and
minimizing operational downtimes caused by unforeseen failures. Furthermore,
since PdM can predict the degradation process, prognosis is much more efficient
than prognostics.
2.3.1 Estimations
Prognostics and Health Management allow technicians to take decisive
maintenance actions based on actual machinery states commonly coming from
three estimations ( C. Zhang et al. , 2019). The first estimation, named Health
Indicator ( HI), is the result of one or more condition indicators—a term used
to refer to the result produced by an algorithm that combines one or more
features of a component or system and which is representative of its state
(MAA, 2014). Those condition indicators are accumulated to signal a need
for maintenance action ( MAA, 2014). The second estimation, named health
stage, is usually comprised within a range of HI points and is commonly used in
failure prognostics to separate healthy from faulty moments ( Yaguo et al., 2018).
Finally, the third estimation, named Remaining Useful Life ( RUL), describes the
6Aircraft components: pumps, compressors, turbines, and others.
7Aircraft systems: turbofan engines, break system, cooling system, and others.
8Fault: anomaly identified without impact in the functional output.
— 21 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
time left before the system health falls below a defined Failure Threshold ( Saxena
et al. , 2008).
Figure 2.6 illustrates the relationship between these estimations obtained by
using the model M . Using sensor signals (colored wavy curves), this model
predicts the health condition and the RUL of the aircraft system C at the
i-th time (vertical dotted green line). In this example, this computation
considers a HI (two-colored curve) representing the systems’ degradation stages:
imperceptible (green) and abnormal (red).
Figure 2.6 : Estimation involved in failure prognostics and prognosis.
If the estimated HI crosses the established diagnosis threshold (horizontal
dotted orange line),C enters an abnormal degradation stage, and thus, M should
alert a potential failure. Otherwise, if the estimated HI crosses the established
prognosis threshold (horizontal dotted red line), the aircraft system’s reliability
has achieved the minimum allowed value. Therefore, maintenance staff should
overhaulC given that this theoretical end-of-life time tEoL (vertical dotted black
line) has been achieved. Predictive maintenance strategy computes the RULi at
thei-th time defined as the absolute difference between ti andtEoL to indicate to
maintenance staff how much the aircraft system can continue operating before
achieving its theoretical end of life. Consequently, failure prognosis, which
extends the failure detection problem to a quantitative perspective, promises
to assess over-maintenance and under-maintenance issues not covered by CBM
strategy.
Failure prognostics and prognosis significantly reduce operational downtime
by detecting and locating faults in incipient health degradation stages. Data
acquisition, data analysis, and maintenance decision-making are fundamental
steps for developing those models, as is the case with any maintenance practice.
Data acquired for these purposes can be divided into condition monitoring
and event data. While condition monitoring data includes the measurements
— 22 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
related to the condition indicators of the targeted equipment, event data includes
information about adjustments and operational changes, e.g., installation,
overhaul, failure verification, replacement, and others ( C. Liu, Cichon, Królczyk
& Li , 2022). Using a determined type of data depends on the development
adopted approach.
2.3.2 Approaches
In Prognostics and Health Management ( PHM), there has been a longstanding
tradition of distinguishing between prognostics and prognosis approaches. A
comprehensive overview of various categories delineated in the state-of-the-art
over the past two decades can be found in ( C. Liu et al. , 2022). By focusing on
solving prognostics or prognosis problems, the current section describes popular
techniques for developing physics-based, knowledge-based, statistical-based, and
data-driven models.
Prognostics and Health Management preserved a strong preference for
developing physics-based approaches by trying to explain machinery degradation
with mathematical expressions. However, this preference was drastically reduced
given the widespread adoption of Internet-of-Things ( IoT) solutions and the vast
and valuable data that condition monitoring sensors can produce, information
which can be analyzed thanks to technological advances in computer hardware
and data analysis techniques ( Berthelsen et al. , 2021). Therefore, this section
explains data-driven models with a primary focus on AI-based approaches,
given their capability to learn from vast amounts of sensor data, rather than
statistical-based ones. It also highlights the advantages of developing hybrid
models by combining physics-based or statistical-based methods with AI-based
methods.
2.3.2.1 Physics-based models
Physics-based approaches characterize the failure mode or its advent using
physical laws, which requires a strong understanding of the system behaviour
(Heng et al. , 2009). In failure prognostics, these approaches can present high
accuracy estimations when mathematical modes are developed using technicians’
knowledge of the monitoring system. However, achieving an accurate model for
complex systems is hard because it requires a complete system understanding.
In failure prognosis, the RUL is estimated by solving an equation or set of
equations derived from empirical data. This fact facilitates the definition of
estimation confidence intervals. However, industrial machines usually have
multiple items, and developing prognosis models becomes complicated with the
growth of mathematical variables.
2.3.2.2 Knowledge-based
Knowledge-based approaches assess the similarity between target estimations,
health states, or RUL and a database of accumulated expertise. These
approaches can be further divided into two subcategories: expert systems and
fuzzy systems
— 23 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
• Expert systems deal with complex problems, usually solved by
technicians’ expertise by loading human knowledge from a database
through an inference engine. The data loaded comprises facts, procedures,
and rules (i.e., IF and THEN statements) accumulated over the years. It
facilitates the model development, explaining prognostics and establishing
prognosis confidence intervals. However, like physics-based models,
developing expert systems becomes complicated with the growth of inputs
and output variables, which implies defining more rules.
• Fuzzy systems also load IF and THEN statements, but instead of
defining precise rules via true or false logic, these systems partite the
feature space in fuzzy sets to reasoning rules imprecisely. The feature space
partition reduces the number of rules compared with expert methods and
allows the definition of confidence intervals in failure prognosis problems.
However, predictions become imprecise, requiring the support of other
prognostics or prognosis models to assist decision-making processes.
2.3.2.3 Statistical-based
Statistical-based approaches are primarily adopted to develop prognosis models
supported by estimation confidence intervals because machines usually degrade
stochastically. The development of these models considers that health
degradation can be modelled as a stochastic process, whose uncertainty is mainly
caused by the following variability sources: temporal variability, unit-to-unit
variability, nonlinear variability, and measurement variability ( C. Liu et al. ,
2022).
Statistical approaches usually present results as a conditional Probabilistic
Density Function ( PDF) because they produce estimations based on empirical
knowledge (Si et al., 2011). Among the most common models are auto regression
(AR), random coefficient, Wiener process, Gamma process, invert Gaussian
process, proportional hazard, Markov-based, and filters.
• Auto-Regressive (AR) models assume that the future health state
linearly depends on its previous values and random errors ( Sikorska,
Hodkiewicz & Ma , 2011). Therefore, an AR model is defined through
a stochastic difference equation. By adopting the Moving A verage (MA)
process, an approach for univariate time series modelling, more complex
models of time series are developed, e.g., Auto-Regressive Moving A verage
(ARMA) and Auto-Regressive Integrated Moving A verage (ARIMA). The
main advantage of these models is the simplicity of their development.
However, the disadvantage is that their performance highly depends on
the trend of condition indicators ( Yaguo et al. , 2018).
• Random coefficient models describe the stochasticity of the health
degradation by adding random coefficients that are assumed to follow a
normal distribution. The main advantage of these models is their ability
to capture the inherently stochastic nature of the phenomenon, which
facilitates presenting PDFs via, e.g., Monte Carlo simulation and Bayesian
— 24 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
filters ( Yaguo et al. , 2018). However, it typically falls short of capturing
the temporal variability inherent in estimating RUL (Pandey et al. , 2009).
• Wiener process models are generally presented as a drift term plus a
diffusion term following a random “Browning” motion of particles on a
fluid. A Wiener process is a vector-valued stochastic process, and each
of its monitored units, where each unit is real-valued by the standard
Gaussian process used to describe the browning motion ( Breit, Feireisl
& Hofmanová , 2017). Wiener process models can describe the temporal
variability of the degradation processes. However, estimating the RUL
may not fit a natural machinery degradation because fluids theory assumes
that particle motion does not depend on past behaviour as Markov
property indicates ( Yaguo et al. , 2018). Moreover, analytical solutions of
RUL estimation in PDFs are challenging to derive for the state-dependent
Wiener process.
• Gamma Process models assume that the increments of health
degradation processes at disjoint time intervals are independent random
variables that follow a gamma distribution, being more versatile for RUL
prognosis. However, these models are restricted to the assumption of
Markov property. Moreover, noise degradation must follow a gamma
distribution, not being effective in describing the monotonic processes.
• Markov-based models assume that the health degradation transforms
within a finite state space by accomplishing the principle of Markov
property ( Kharoufeh, 2003). For RUL estimation, these models describe
the machinery degradation assuming that the health states can be observed
directly. However, health states cannot be observed, making it impossible
to assign their corresponding transition probability. Hidden Markov
Models (HMMs) aim to solve this problem by defining the number of
states, the number of distinct observation symbols per state, the state
transition probability distribution, the observation symbol probability
distribution in the state, and the initial state distribution. HMMs are
applicable to nonlinear and non-stationary systems ( Sikorska et al. , 2011).
However, its Markov property assumption limits its applicability in real
applications. Moreover, modeling HMM is a heavy workload, being
practical with a short number of states.
• Inverse Gaussian Process models set an Inverse Gaussian (IG) process
and assume that the degradation processes of machinery have independent
increments following an IG distribution. The main advantage of IG process
models is that they can incorporate different kinds of random effects, being
useful for describing various degradation processes. However, IG process
models have similar properties to gamma process models. Thus they are
still restricted to Markov property and monotonic processes.
• Proportional Hazards (PH) models, firstly introduced by ( Cox,
1972), assume that the hazard rate of a system was composed of two
multiplicative factors. The first factor, e.g., a covariance function,
— 25 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
characterizes the health degradation with historical failure data. On
the other hand, the second factor, e.g., a hazard function, predicts the
RUL. Given PH models integrate event data and condition monitoring
data, they present more accurate prediction results when enough event
data and condition monitoring data are available. However, it is difficult
to capture these two kinds of data simultaneously. Moreover, adding
covariate functions increases the computational workload’s burden.
• Filters aims to minimize the noise of time series data. Kalman
Filters (KFs) and Particle Filters (PFs) are two widely used filters in
RUL estimation. Kalman Filter is an effective recursive digital process
technique used to minimize the RUL estimation error. While the Kalman
Filter has been used to describe a linear degradation process, the Extended
Kalman Filter (EKF) has been applied to estimate non-linear systems.
Despite KFs having contributed to enhancing the RUL estimation, they
are still limited by the Gaussian noise assumption. Particle Filters (PFs)
are a KF alternative applicable to non-linear systems with non-Gaussian
noise. Unfortunately, PF suffers from one long-standing limitation: sample
degeneracy. ( T. Li et al. , 2014).
2.3.2.4 Intelligent approaches
Artificial Intelligence ( AI) aims to mimic and go beyond human capabilities. In
PHM, AI-enabled systems analyze and contextualize condition monitoring and
event data to assist technicians in triggering effective and efficient maintenance
operations. Two main method types emerge in this context: Machine Learning
and Deep Learning ( Ramezani et al. , 2019; Wen et al., 2022; Yaguo et al., 2018).
Machine Learning ( ML) and Deep Learning ( DL) methods aim to learn
machinery health degradation patterns from data, liberating humans from
getting knowledge from vast amounts of examples by themselves ( Kan, Tan
& Mathew , 2015; Sikorska et al. , 2011). However, they differ in the
feature-gathering optimization level illustrated in Figure 2.7. While ML methods
require the feature to be extracted and selected manually by signal processing
data engineers, DL automatically learns features by the deep architectures
composed of multiple layers of non-linear operations. While ML depends on data
engineers’ expertise, DL automatizes the feature extraction process by learning
complex functions that map the input data to the output data.
Figure 2.7 : Development pipeline of predictive models via ML and DL.
Among the most used ML methods are: Support Vector Machines
— 26 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
(SVM), Decision Trees (DTs), k-nearest Neighbor (kNN), K-means, Principal
Component Analysis ( PCA), Functional Data Analysis ( FDA), and others.
On the other hand, among the DL methods adopted are: Deep Bayesian
Networks (DBNs), Fully Connected Neural Networks ( FNNs), Convolutional
Neural Networks (CNNs), Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs), encoder-decoder,
and other variants.
Depending on the availability of labeled data, AI methods could adopt
Supervised, Semi-supervised, or Unsupervised Learning approaches to develop
Condition-Based Maintenance (prognostics) and Predictive Maintenance
(prognosis) models. In this context, Figure 2.8 illustrates AI approaches,
separating them into statistical-based and Artificial Neural Network ( ANN)
based and relating them with the learning procedure that typically configures.
Figure 2.8 : Model development alternatives using AI methods.
• Dimensionality reduction transforms a high-dimensional dataset into
a lower-dimensional dataset without losing the valuable properties of the
original data. This problem is prevalent when ML methods are adopted
and feature engineering tasks are done manually, viz., feature extraction
and feature selection. To reduce the dimension of datasets, linear or
non-linear methods can be adopted, where linear methods involve linearly
projecting the original data onto a low-dimensional space. While the
most popular linear methods are Principal Component Analysis ( PCA),
Independent Component Analysis (ICA), Linear Discriminant Analysis
(LDA) and Factor Analysis (F A), the most popular non-linear methods
are kernel PCA and Isomap. Functional Data Analysis ( FDA) has also
been used in PdM as an alternative because it refers to a collection of
techniques for analyzing data over a curve, surface, or continuum.
• Clustering is more common in classification problems, where a
measurement aims to be related to a determined group or cluster. Despite
clustering needs are more common in failure prognostic problems, e.g.,
separating healthy and faulty measures, clustering has also been used for
discovering multiple health degradation stages. K-means, for example, is a
bottom-up approach where each observation is assigned to a cluster based
on the mean value of the cluster.
• A Support Vector Machine (SVM) is a discriminative classifier
that finds an optimal separating hyperplane with the maximum distance
between the plane and the nearest data. In failure detection, SVM
implementation assumes the division of the condition monitoring data
— 27 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
into failure and healthy examples. For more complex problems, there
are two main options: 1) including more classes in the case of failure
prognostics, or 2) moving to Support Vector Regression (SVR) in the case
of failure prognosis. SVM and SVR are superior in dealing with small
datasets. However, they highly depend on the definition of kernel functions
(e.g., linear and sigmoid), whose choosing methods are not well-defined in
literature.
• A Decision Tree (DT) is a decision-support hierarchical model that uses
a tree-like for classification or prognosis problems. Random Forest (RF),
a model composed of many trees, is developed to reduce over-fitting and
error induced by bias. Decision Trees (DTs) can generally handle large
datasets with many input features, but they are inadequate for predicting
continuous values that usually are presented in RUL estimation problems.
• Bayesian Networks (BNs) , also called BayesNets or Belief Networks,
are probabilistic graphic models that integrate historical data and expert
knowledge to overcome the uncertain, incomplete, and even conflicting
properties of the data. Bayesian Networks are usually used in failure
prognostics by graphically presenting the causes and symptoms of the
faults and quantitatively evaluating them. Despite Deep Learning versions
of these networks called Deep Bayesian Networks (DBNs) can benefit from
unlabeled data, regression analysis is carried out on the basis of the BN
classification.
• An Artificial Neural Network ( ANN) is an interconnected group of
nodes inspired by a simplification of neurons in a brain. ANNs have robust
pattern recognition capabilities for failure prognostics and prognosis.
These networks can be classified as static and dynamic, considering their
architecture. While static architectures (e.g., feed-forward) consider that
the inputs of a specific layer depend on the outputs of the previous layers,
dynamic architectures consider that the inputs of a specific layer depend
on the outputs of the previous layer and the previous iterations of the
network itself, e.g., Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs). Most ANNs
are static-based, such as Multilayer Perceptrons, Radial Basis Function
Networks (RBFNs) and General Regression Neural Networks (GRNNs).
On the other hand, dynamic networks such as RNNs have been mainly
used in RUL estimation due to their ability to recognize data’s sequential
characteristics. Deep Neural Networks emerged to increase the skills of
ANNs, e.g., the limitation of storing information for a long time of RNNs.
• Deep Neural Networks extend from ANNs to mimic feature engineering
tasks conducted by human reasoning. These models extract features from
data by defining multiple layers of non-linear operations. Four network
architectures are quite popular in CBM and PdM applications such as
Fully Connected Neural Network ( FNN), Convolutional Neural Network
(CNN), Long-Short Term Memory ( LSTM), and encoder-decoder. fully
connected neural networks were introduced to replace the feature
extraction required to develop multilayer perceptrons. convolutional
— 28 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
neural networks was introduced to analyze a huge number of condition
monitoring data examples transformed into image data. LSTM was
introduced to overcome learning long-term dependencies required by
RNNs. Finally, encoder-decoder architecture transforms the input data
to a low-dimensional space by aiming to reconstruct measurements taken
from normal operating conditions accurately.
2.3.2.5 Hybrid approaches
The methods mentioned above have their own limitations. Hybrid approaches
aim to integrate the capabilities of two or more previous methods, usually
preferred when multiple failure types are assessed simultaneously. For example,
some studies integrate PHM and probabilistic risk assignment to consider health
degradation as a stochastic process ( Soualhi et al. , 2022). Other studies combine
ML methods with physics-based approaches to include the health degradation
interpretation in support of decision-making processes ( H. Li et al. , 2024).
2.3.3 Development stages
With the increasing prevalence of AI and the IoT, the modern industry
rapidly embraces these technologies to build intelligent products, operations,
and services. Consequently, maintenance operations are increasingly inclined to
develop AI models as a preferred approach. Developing those failure prognostics
or failure prognosis models includes identifying indicators that are symptoms
representative of an incipient failure and, based on its potential operational
downtime, estimating the RUL. The challenges associated with the construction
of CBM and PdM solutions are addressed in four main stages illustrated in
Figure 2.9: data acquisition, data pre-processing, health stage division and data
model development.
Figure 2.9 : Key stages in developing AI models in for PHM.
2.3.3.1 Data acquisition
Data acquisition refers mainly to constructing the experimental scenario. The
main goal of this stage is to acquire condition monitoring data under the
“run-to-failure” philosophy. Collecting run-to-failure data means getting time
— 29 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
series data, or a sequence of random measures, x1,x 2,...,x n, taken for a finite
time period n (Shumway & Stoffer , 2017). This data is gathered by monitoring
the equipment’s health degradation through M monitoring sensors with a
determined rate in Hertz (Hz). When data from condition monitoring systems
is insufficient, collecting data from digital twins facilitates the development of
proof of concept applications.
When adopting data-driven approaches, the data acquisition stage should
integrate data transformations, e.g., noise reduction and data cleaning, before
applying feature engineering techniques ( Atamuradov, Medjaher, Dersin &
Lamoureux, 2017). Given condition monitoring data is often unreliable, time
series data are often large and dirty. There are two approaches to dealing with
time-series data errors: discarding and cleaning data ( X. Wang & Wang, 2020).
When time series present inconsistent or missing in some sensor measurements,
data cleaning techniques must solve these problems. When time series present
inconsistencies in continuous measurements, the use of this entire abnormal
period is put in question, being discarded periods when appropriate.
2.3.3.2 Data pre-processing
Although data discarding and cleaning might better represent data trends, there
are cases in which time series data does not directly represent equipment health
degradation ( Atamuradov et al. , 2017). Therefore, this stage is responsible
for discovering features named Health Indicator ( HI), where a single HI signal
results from one or more condition indicator values cumulated to indicate a need
for maintenance action ( Budeanu & Markou , 2022). When DL methods are not
preferred, engineers conduct feature engineering tasks: feature extraction and
feature selection.
• Feature Extraction and dimensional reduction techniques are mainly
adopted when datasets are large or original sensor signals do not directly
reveal machinery degradation, mapping data into a low-dimensional
feature space ( Vogl, Weiss & Helu , 2019). Extracting features from
signals depends on the technicians’ expertise. Statistical and engineering
knowledge-based methods are applied to discover HI depending on the
situation ( Tsui & et al. , 2015). Statistical methods mainly allow
technicians to gather degradation insights by analyzing all input sensors
simultaneously with PCA, ICA, Analysis of Variance (ANOV A), Distance
Evaluations, Mutual Information (MI), and, among others ( Javed et al. ,
2012). Engineering Knowledge-based methods analyze each sensor signal
once, depending on the target domain. Figure 2.10 illustrates the most
common features that can be extracted from the time and/or frequency
domain.
– Time domain features are commonly obtained from non-periodic
signals ( Khanh et al. , 2018).
– Frequency domain features are commonly extracted from periodic
signals ( B. Zhang, Zhang & Xu , 2015).
— 30 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
Figure 2.10 : Typical features extracted from time and/or frequency domain.
– Time-frequency domain features are preferred when limitations
on frequency domain features exist, for instance, handling
non-stationary waveform signals ( Tsui & et al. , 2015).
• Feature selection techniques evaluate and choose the features that
better solve a defined problem, avoiding the use of the redundant and
the irrelevant ones ( Samina, Tehmina & Shamila , 2014), consequently
optimizing the use of computational resources. Feature selection mainly
aims to improve the performance of data-driven models ( Javed et al. ,
2012; Speiser et al. , 2019). Feature evaluation and selection theory classify
methods as filters, wrappers, and hybrid ( Atamuradov et al. , 2018).
– Filters use ranking metrics, e.g., Monotonicity, Trendability,
Correlation, Robustness, and Prognosability. These metrics evaluate
individual signals or groups of them ( Atamuradov et al., 2018; Coble
& Hines , 2009; B. Zhang et al. , 2015).
– Wrappers perform better than filters when ML methods are adopted
because the feature selection process is optimized for the model
adopted, e.g., Genetic Algorithms and Random Forest ( Samina
et al. , 2014). Although wrappers can enhance the performance of
predictive models, wrapper methods are expensive because analyzing
large feature spaces implies a high computational cost.
– Hybrid methods are preferred when neither filter nor wrapper can
find an optimal feature set individually. The advantages of the hybrid
methods are the same as the wrapper methods, but hybrid ones are
better in computational complexity. Lasso regression, elastic net,
Short-Term Load Forecasting (STLF), and selective kernel ensemble
based on Relevance Vector Machine (R VM) are examples of them
(Z. Hu et al. , 2015; Khaire & Dhanalakshmi , 2019; Yang et al., 2016).
— 31 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
2.3.3.3 Health stage division
In failure prognostics, the health stage division is similar to classifying healthy
and faulty measurements. However, in failure prognosis, health stage division
refers to separating the continuous degradation processes of machinery into
different HIs according to their varying trends. When machinery degrades
gradually, dividing health stages does not make sense because there is only
one stage. However, this scenario is unrealistic because machinery usually
experiences imperceptible degradation stages, and just after a certain point,
the machinery degradation accelerates. Figure 2.6 illustrates two health stages
divided by a prognostics threshold. A multi-stage division usually separates
imperceptible from degradation and critical degradation stages. Two-stage
division approaches have been known as elbow point detection in the literature.
Because this problem corresponds to time-series classification, this process has
been automatized via clustering or classification ML methods, e.g., K-means,
SVM, ANNs, and others. While classification requires assuming a prognosis
threshold, such as a time-window technique, clustering approaches need to
identify the number of health stages, e.g., k clusters, that the problem better
fits.
2.3.3.4 Data model developing
AI models usually use a training dataset for model development, while a testing
dataset is reserved for performance evaluations. Data model development relates
health status with raw condition monitoring data, extracted features, or HIs.
Classification or regression models are constructed depending on the problem
definition, failure prognostics, or prognosis. Failure prognostics typically aim to
distinguish between healthy and faulty measurements by configuring a binary
classification, where 0 indicates a healthy state and 1 indicates a faulty state. On
the other hand, failure prognosis could configure two approaches that depend
on the RUL definition. Usually implemented by defining label data, the first
definition considers the RUL at i time as the distance RULi = |tEoL−ti|
between the theoretical end-of-life tEoL and the current moment ti. The second
approach, usually implemented via statistical-based methods because the health
degradation process is assumed to be a stochastic process, defines the RUL as
the time left before the health states of machinery cross a Failure Threshold. In
these cases, the RULi is expressed as follows:
RULi =inf (l :x(l +ti)≥γ), (2.1)
where inf (. ) represents the inferior limit of a variable; x(l +ti) is the health
state at l +ti with l≥0 and γis the Failure Threshold ( Yaguo et al. , 2018).
2.3.4 Evaluation metrics
A variety of evaluation metrics have been formulated according to the different
requirements of researchers. Because metrics evaluate different aspects of
performance prediction, adopting adequate metrics is challenging and mainly
— 32 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
depends on the problem to solve. This section refers to three problems to guide
readers in choosing de appropriate ones: classification, regression with a single
model, and regression with multiple models.
2.3.4.1 Classification
Classification metrics are broadly used in failure prognostics and detection, in
which health states are usually treated as multiple classes. In an elementary
failure detection problem, solved via binary classification when zero refers to
a healthy state and one refers to a failure state, four prediction cases emerge:
True Positive (TP), True Negative (TN), False Positive (TP), and False Negative
(FN). A correct faulty prediction state is called TP, while an incorrect one is
called FP. On the other hand, a correct healthy prediction state is referred to as
TN, while an incorrect one is called FN. These considerations, commonly used
to construct a confusion matrix, give the origin to various classification metrics.
This section considered referring metrics that take into account positive and
negative classifications.
• Precision measures how many observations predicted as positive are
positive in fact. In failure detection, when faulty states aim to be correctly
detected, the precision must be increased, but this process should be
supported by using other metrics, e.g., recall.
Precision = TP
TP +FP (2.2)
• Recall measures how many observations out of all positive observations
are classified as positive. This metric also has to be increased and
supported by precision. In response to this need, metrics such as
accuracy and Fβ-score were formulated to evaluate positive and negative
classifications simultaneously.
Recall = TP
TP +FN (2.3)
• Accuracy measures how many positive and negative observations are
correctly classified. The main advantage of this metric is its easy
interpretation by non-technical stakeholders, but it is recommended
when healthy and faulty measurements are balanced because this metric
performs well in the majority class.
Accuracy = TP +TN
TP +TN +FP +FN (2.4)
• Fβ-score combines precision and recall in a single metric, in which β
refers to how much recall is cared about over precision.
Fβ−score = (1 + β2) Precision×Recall
β2×Precision +Recall (2.5)
— 33 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
There are more straightforward and more complex metrics, even for multiple
classifications, e.g., micro-average evaluation metrics. However, explaining the
wide variety of evaluation metrics differs from this section’s focus.
2.3.4.2 Regression with a single model
In failure prognosis, selecting appropriate metrics is more challenging because
it depends on the learning approach used, such as supervised or unsupervised
learning. In the presence of labeled data Y , which corresponds to supervised
learning approaches ˆY =f (X), evaluation metrics aim to evaluate the distance
between the theoretical Yi and the estimated ˆYi RUL at the i-th time.
• Mean Absulte Error ( MAE) quantifies the average absolute deviation
between a set of predictions and the corresponding theoretical values,
disregarding the direction of the errors.

in common. They tend to zero when models tend to present similar results.

2.4 Aircraft health monitoring
Aircraft Health Monitoring is a technique that uses Prognostics and Health
Management (PHM) estimations, approaches, and evaluation metrics to predict
potential faults or failures in aircraft components or systems. By adopting this
technique, airline operators aim to go towards Aircraft Health Management
vision by firstly putting effort into sensing, acquiring, transferring, and analyzing
before assessing decision-making challenges.
In Aircraft Health Monitoring, the RUL is estimated in terms of flight
hours to align with scheduled checks, whose maintenance operations extend the
aircraft reliability and continuing airworthiness certification. Thus, airlines can
guarantee departures as scheduled without delays or cancellations.
The literature on RUL estimation of equipment is vast, even limiting to the
aviation industry. Because health monitoring of aircraft systems is central to
reducing maintenance, repair and operations costs ( Mitici & de Pater , 2021),
this section exploratory scenarios with available data from aircraft systems.
2.4.1 Exploratory scenarios
Condition monitoring data in this section accomplishes two selection criteria:
1) data comes from equivalent aircraft systems, and 2) data comprises many
run-to-failure trajectories. Both criteria were applied to open-source and
private datasets gathered through active participation in the project Real-time
Condition-based Maintenance for Adative Aircraft Maintenance Planning
(ReMAP) of the European Union’s Horizon 2020, which aims to contribute to
reinforcing the European leadership in aeronautics by developing an open-source
solution for aircraft maintenance, the Integrated Fleet Health Management
(IFHM) system.
The data from turbofan engines and cooling units, described in Table 2.1, was
collected from Boeing commercial airplanes and digital twins systems. While the
health degradation of turbofans is simulated using the Commercial Modular
Aero-Propulsion System Simulation ( C-MAPSS), the health degradation of
cooling units from the Integrated Cooling System ( ICS) of Boeing 787 series
was monitored for accomplishing the main goal of ReMAP project.
Commercial aircraft systems are identified using a code defined by the Air
Transport Association ( ATA). Therefore, Table 2.1 describes the datasets in
terms of aircraft series, data provider, system of interest, and measurement
sample rate. While ICS come from Boeing commercial aeroplanes, C-MAPSS
corresponds to a Digital Twin that simulates turbofan degradation based on
physics models. While measurements from ICS are gathered at every second
1Hz, measurements from C-MAPSS are taken every ten seconds 1
3600 Hz of
every hour 1
10 Hz. Although the availability of various gigabytes of data
from commercial aircraft, experiments on synthetic data from C-MAPSS gain
relevance due to the availability of theoretical RUL values.

2.4.1.1 Cooling Units
The Cooling Units ( CUs) are part of the Integrated Cooling System ( ICS)
of the Boeing 787 series. The ICS senses and regulates the temperature
of the aircraft to maintain it in operable conditions. The temperature is
regulated by transporting the liquid through the modules (line spacing boxes)
illustrated in Figure 2.11. In the Bulk Cargo Compartment, where ICS work is
concentrated, the liquid is transported through two pumps, four CUs, and four
motor controllers, being equivalent between them.
Figure 2.11 : Liquid transported on cooling units in the ICS system.
The current diagram has been extracted from slides of ( Basora et al. , 2021).
Dataset
In the development of ReMAP project, condition monitoring and event data
were collected from a fleet of Boeing 747 series to study the health degradation
of ICS and its subsystems. Condition monitoring sensors were anonymized to
accomplish airline data privacy concerns, but event data was revealed to analyze
cooling units. Event data comes from the personal staff registry. This staff
has documented maintenance interventions of three types of events, i.e., Flight
Deck Effects (FDE), Aircraft Technical Log (ATL), and Predictive Maintenance
(PdM) interventions.
The dataset provided to ReMAP researchers comprises data extracted from
17 different aircraft using a sample rate of 1Hz. This data was collected by
measuring 18295 flights during 29 months. During this period, 32 failures with
available data occurred, of which 24 failures corresponded to verified ones, while
the other 2 and 6 corresponded to dubious and likely ones, respectively.
— 37 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
The sensors installed in ICSs correspond to cooling units, pumps, doors,
and informative signals. Since each cooling unit allocates nine sensors and
ICS comprises four, 36 input signals correspond to these cooling units. The
data provided by the rest 22 signals correspond to sensors installed in pumps,
doors, and information data, e.g., flight id, departure date time, flight phase, row
number, altitude, and among others.
Multiple flight phases are measured to study aircraft health degradation. ICS
data providers revealed that these phases are easily identifiable by sorting the
following information columns: plane tail, flight id, departure date time, flight
phase and row number. In Figure 2.12, 14 flight phases are experienced in a
single flight that takes approximately five hours.
Figure 2.12 : Flight phases experienced by Boeing 747 series.
Applications
In the context of ReMAP, data from Integrated Cooling Systems has been used
to detect anomalies in cooling units by implementing a Deep Learning ( DL)
architecture ( Basora et al. , 2021). The DL encoder-decoder, implemented
using CNNs, uses data from multiple flights to construct a health indicator
for then alert over the presence of anomalies if this indicator crosses a
determined threshold. Despite this data-driven anomaly detector has achieved
acceptable accuracy, the RUL of cooling units has not been assessed using
this dataset. Other studies have estimated the RUL of cooling units
but applying statistical-based approaches and anonymized sensor data from
unknown providers ( Mitici & de Pater , 2021).
2.4.1.2 Turbofan engines
The RUL estimation of turbofan engines has been widely assessed thanks
to NASA published open-sources repositories obtained from the Commercial
Modular Aero-Propulsion System Simulation ( C-MAPSS). This software
simulated the health degradation of a fleet of turbofan units, in which a
single unit degrades along multiple flights. A health degradation trajectory
— 38 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
or run-to-failure example, composed of multiple flights conducted with the same
unit, comprises a set of operating or flight cycles that vary on flight modes, e.g.,
landing, cruise, and others. In this sense, the current RUL orRULi =|tEoL−ti|
refers to the difference between the current operating cycle ti and the cycle when
the aircraft system becomes inoperable tEoL.
Condition monitoring data from turbofans corresponds to: an engine id, an
cycle count, sensor variables and operating conditions. Operating conditions
Ow refer to the altitude, Mach Number ( MN), temperature at fan inlet, and
Throttle-Resolver Angle ( TRA). On the other hand, sensor variables Xs refer
to monitoring sensors located at multiple rotating components and gas path, as
illustrated in Figure 2.13:
• Rotating components : fan, low-pressure compressor (LPC),
high-pressure compressor (HPC), high-pressure turbine (HPT),
low-pressure turbine (LPT), turbine axes (N1) and turbine shaft
(N2).
• Gas path : HPC, HPT and LPT.
Figure 2.13 : Diagram of a turbofan engine and its external real appearance.
The diagram of a turbofan engine has been extracted from ( Saxena & K , 2008). The
General Electric engine J79 in the photography has been donated by the Spanish Air
Force to the School of Engineering of the University of León, Spain.
Datasets
Public repositories have been mainly delivered in three main repositories. The
first repository, Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation ( Saxena & Goebel ,
2008), comprises four development and test sets with theoretical RUL values or
labels. The second repository, corresponding to the PHM08 challenge ( Saxena
& K, 2008), is similar to the first repository, except that the RUL is not revealed
and comprises only one development and testing set. Finally, the last repository,
named Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation-2 from the PHM21 challenge
(Arias Chao et al. , 2021), provides realistic run-to-failure trajectories for a small
fleet of aircraft engines. This last repository contains more realistic data because
degradation was simulated under real flight conditions recorded on board a
— 39 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
commercial jet covering climb, cruise, and descent flight phases. Particularly,
this research prioritizes the use of labeled repositories.
Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation
Datasets from this repository have been widely preferred instead of PHM
challenge datasets because this repository provides theoretical RUL (ground
truth) and contains thousands of run-to-failure trajectories. This repository,
constructed by gathering sensor measurements at 1
3600 Hz, includes four datasets
comprising development and test sets. The FD001, FD002, FD003, and FD004
datasets, described in Table 2.2, vary in the number of fault modes, flight phases,
and trajectories. While fault modes are unknown, flight phases and the number
of run-to-failure trajectories are identifiable by processing data.

of flight phases emerges by combining the operation conditions(Listou Ellefsen et

Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation-2
This repository, named N-CMAPSS by ( Arias Chao et al. , 2021), was
constructed by gathering sensor measurements with a sampling rate of 0. 1Hz.
Therefore, this repository of 14GBs is larger than previous turbofan repositories.
This new CMAPSS repository comprises various datasets DS composed of
development and test splits. Each dataset, described in Table 2.1, comprises
data from a determined number of turbofan units, in which each unit experiences
certain failure modes on the following rotating component of the turbofan
engine: fan, LPC, HPC, HPT, and LPT. While failure modes can be affected
by degradation in flow and efficiency, the flight class (FC) is determined by the
length of flights that the unit experiences: short, medium, and long flights.

Applications
Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation repository comprises FD00x datasets
divided in development and test sets, each set sometimes with a different number
of run-to-failure trajectories, and therefore, multiple turbofan units. While
trajectories of testing splits do not contain the RUL of the last operating cycle
tEoL, trajectories of training splits do. Hence, if a health degradation function
is assumed, RUL estimation can be modeled by adopting a Supervised Learning
approach. While some researchers took advantage of the massively available
run-to-failure trajectories to map condition indicators and RULs via typical ML
and DL methods ( X. Li, Ding & Sun , 2017; Mathew et al. , 2017; Olivares et
al., 2019; Rossi & Conan-Guez , 2005), other researchers took this advantage to
develop prognosis models based on unit-to-unit similarity ( Mosallam, Medjaher
& Zerhouni , 2016; J. Shi et al. , 2016). While some research concentrates
efforts on health stage division ( Baptista, Henriques & Goebel , 2021), others
— 41 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
concentrate on proposing novel prognostic approaches ( Listou Ellefsen et al. ,
2019; M. Liu et al. , 2020; Wu et al. , 2020).
Datasets from the Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation-2 repository,
listed in Table 2.3, also comprise development and test sets. Those DS datasets
from N-CMAPSS are shorter than previous C-MAPSS datasets in terms of
turbofan units but larger in terms of the number of measurements per turbofan
unit given the sample rate set at 1
10 Hz, making them ideal for experimenting
a variety of DL architectures. Besides using DL as data-driven approaches,
physic-based ( Arias Chao et al. , 2022) and statistical-based ( Basora, Viens,
Chao & Olive , 2023) models have also been used to construct more robust
solutions. Given DS datasets, which also include degradation parameters, health
indicators, and virtual sensor measurements, a variety of approaches have been
tested for addressing anomaly detection and failure prognosis challenges.
2.4.2 Challenges
Many factors make Condition-Based Maintenance and Predictive Maintenance
very challenging because of the need for dependable failure prognostics and
RUL computation. To guarantee the aircraft’s airworthiness, high estimation
performance is a crucial aspect that requires further investigation before
deploying it in production ( Atamuradov et al. , 2017). While Aircraft Health
Management points to compute the uncertainty of the RUL estimation (e.g.
using PDFs), moving from failure prognostics to prognosis requires not only
identifying failure stages but also catching health degradation ensigns in
condition monitoring data. This research is aware that seeing these ensigns
is complex in data from aerial systems because aircraft degrade by switching in
flight modes, e.g., taking off, climbing, cruising, and landing.
Due to the increasing integration of onboard sensors and aircraft condition
monitoring systems in modern aircraft, researchers are primarily focused on
enhancing data acquisition and data processing stages to identify indicators
of health degradation in aircraft systems and components. Given the
interconnected nature of system components, this research emphasises analyzing
aircraft systems.
Given the limited availability of data repositories containing run-to-failure
data obtained from real aircraft, this work analyses data collected explicitly
from Boeing 747 systems under the scope of ReMAP project. Alternatively, this
research uses data from digital twins such C-MAPSS data to explore AI model
development using massive run-to-failure trajectories.
Model development requires not only adopting but also describing techniques
to map condition monitoring data with health states. In addition, adequate
RUL estimation evaluation metrics should allow the prognosis model to alert
technicians to perform maintenance interventions before experiencing critical
damage. In fact, to accomplish the expectations of predictive maintenance
regarding maintenance costs and aircraft on ground decreasing, a RUL
estimation should be as close as possible but less than the theoretical remaining
system’s life.
Defining the maximum lifespan of machinery is crucial to estimate its RUL
— 42 —

CHAPTER 2. AIRCRAFT HEALTH MONITORING
at a given moment. To do so, event data or aircraft production-generated
reliability gains relevance. Using run-to-failure data from digital twins makes
that definition easier because simulators allow monitoring the entire lifetime of
aircraft systems.
By using data from an aircraft fleet and simulators, this research aims to
develop AI models but avoid the automatization of feature engineering in order
to focus on the health degradation explainability. Because AI approaches tens to
give results from a black box, especially Deep Learning, this research considers
the construction of health indicator at hand. With this vision, the construction
of hybrid approaches is considered to exploit the advantages of multiple data
analysis techniques.
2.5 Summary
Given the advantages of 1) adopting Condition-Based Maintenance and
Predictive Maintenance strategies, 2) the growing adoption of onboard sensors
and aircraft condition monitoring systems, and 3) the broad adoption of
data-driven approaches to developing predictive models, this chapter presented
Aircraft Health Management as a unified capability that monitors aircraft
health. Then, Prognostics and Health Management ( PHM) is introduced as
a discipline dedicated to constructing data-driven predictive models that fit
with the needs from data acquisition to model development of Aircraft Health
Monitoring.
This chapter mainly focused on describing the approaches, developing stages,
and evaluation metrics used to prognostic failures and estimate the Remaining
Useful Life ( RUL) of aerial systems. After describing the main stages of PHM
for developing AI models, this chapter describes condition monitoring gathered
from aircraft cooling units and turbofan engines. Finally, the main challenges
in materializing the ambition of Aircraft Health Monitoring are presented
to concentrate on developing predictive models instead of decision-making
processes.
— 43 —
