CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
T
his chapter is devoted to reviewing the literature regarding collaborative
prognosis via Federated Learning ( FL) based on the following facts:
1. Collaborative prognostics and collaborative prognosis are two concepts
extending the two main problems of Prognostics and Health
Management ( PHM) for decentralized data scenarios: failure prognostics
(Adrià Salvador et al. , 2019) and failure prognosis ( Dhada, Jain, Herrera
et al. , 2020). Both collaborative problems enable AI model owners to
improve their prediction performance by learning from failures experienced
by equivalent machines possessed by other owners;
2. Collaborative prognostics and prognosis are suitable in applications with
highly reliable policies, e.g., commercial aviation, where an individual
aircraft/fleet would not experience enough failures and in different
operating conditions to generate representative condition monitoring data;
3. Restriction to exchange condition monitoring data due to data-privacy
legislative regulations (e.g., GDPR1, CCPA2, and PDPA3) and lucrative
purposes where enterprises would not share confidential information with
competitors; and,
4. The wide acceptance of Federated Learning, a growing privacy-preserving
and distributed Machine Learning ( ML) approach that collaboratively
improves the prediction performance of multiple models while keeping data
in producers ( Q. Li, Wen, Wu et al. , 2019).
3.1 Introduction
Data scarcity is the primary obstacle hindering the development of Predictive
Maintenance (PdM) solutions, particularly those using AI/ML methods, across
various industrial sectors ( Pandhare, Jia & Lee , 2021). For instance, in
the aviation industry, airworthiness regulations limit condition monitoring or
data gathering under the philosophy of “run-to-failure” ( Budeanu & Markou ,
2022). This chapter concentrates on describing privacy-preserving collaborative
prognosis.
Collaborative prognosis is a technique that faculties machines with the ability
to predict the advent of failures by learning from the failures of equivalent
machines in a fleet ( Dhada, Jain, Herrera et al. , 2020). A collaborative prognosis
had been conceived on the influence of cloud computing, where uploading data to
a central server is not a concern. However, this computing paradigm experiences
communication overhead and data privacy concerns, mainly when systems from
multiple companies produce high volumes of data. Edge computing aims to
1https://gdpr-info.eu/
2https://oag.ca.gov/privacy/ccpa
3https://sso.agc.gov.sg/Act/PDPA2012
— 46 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
overcome this problem by providing computing capabilities to computational
devices at the network’s edge and reducing the transmission of confidential
information ( Xia et al. , 2021). Federated Learning, a decentralized Machine
Learning ( ML) approach, fits the edge computing paradigm while allowing the
development of multiple collaborative problems ( Brecko et al. , 2022), such as
collaborative prognosis.
This chapter focuses on reviewing the state-of-the-art of collaborative
prognosis via Federated Learning. Section 3.2 introduces edge computing
and compares its advantages over cloud computing. Section 3.3 describes the
characteristics, key steps, types and data partitioning settings of FL, calling to
the attention of aggregating knowledge from data that differ in feature and
sample space. Section 3.4 describes the federated solutions that deal with
three different data partitioning settings. Section 3.5 presents the available
frameworks for speeding up the deploying of FL. Section 3.6 describes the main
challenges of constructing FL applications for collaborative prognosis. Finally,
Section 3.7 summarizes this chapter.
3.2 Edge computing
With the proliferation of the Internet-of-Things ( IoT) and the vast amounts of
data generated by mobile and stationary devices, the demand for processing
capabilities at the network edge has driven the transition from cloud computing
to edge computing paradigms ( Mansouri & Babar , 2021).
Cloud computing has moved the way how people communicate through the
Internet. Since its conception in 2005, cloud computing has delivered virtualized
but centralized software (SaaS), platform (PaaS) and infrastructure (IaaS).
This data consumer-oriented computing paradigm eliminated the complexity
of managing information technology infrastructure but failed to serve real-time
IoT applications.
Edge computing, a paradigm conceived in the past two decades, encompasses
technologies that enable computation to be carried out at the network’s
edge ( W. Shi et al. , 2016). Figure 3.1 illustrates the differences between
edge and cloud computing architectures. While nodes from a cloud
computing paradigm consume services provided by the cloud, nodes from an
edge-computing paradigm are supplied with computational capabilities to reduce
data transmission between data producers and the cloud. In other words, edge
computing considers downstream data from cloud services and upstream data
from IoT services.
Table 3.1 summarizes the main differences between nodes in both computing
paradigms considering four factors: topology, computational resources, data
origin, and latency. In contrast to cloud computing, edge computing provides
limited and distributed computing resources such as memory and storage.
Unlike cloud nodes, which primarily consume data, edge computing nodes are
considered data producers and processors. Finally, given that edge nodes aim to
process data in real-time, they ensure less latency than cloud computing, mainly
because this last one provides data that is not urgent in time.
— 47 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
Figure 3.1 : Components of cloud and edge computing.
Table 3.1: Comparing the main factors of cloud and edge computing.
F actor Cloud computing nodes Edge computing nodes
Topology Centralized architecture Decentralized architecture
Computing Resources A vailable memory Limited memoryand storage and storage
Data Consumers Producers and consumers
Latency Process data in Get processed data thatreal-time is not time-urgent
3.2.1 Benefits
The decentralized topology of edge computing enables it to handle data
producers’ unavailability and mobility while facilitating the integration of data
privacy policies (e.g., GDPR, CCPA, and PDPA). It is possible by processing
sensitive data of multiple producers at the edge ( Dr Suresha & Shaheen , 2023).
• Mobility is a crucial characteristic in transportation applications, and
edge nodes play a vital role in enabling seamless connectivity for constantly
moving vehicles while maintaining low latency and high bandwidth.
Applications like self-driving vehicles and air transportation deploy edge
devices along roadways or airstrips to facilitate real-time processing and
communication.
• Low latency achievements and delivering real-time solutions are possible
by locating edge devices close to data producers and end-users ( Dimou
et al. , 2022). In decentralized computing, the communication between
the cloud and edge nodes is reserved for specific updates to the network
members.
• A vailability and reliability decrease as long as data processing moves
towards edge-based paradigms because edge nodes do not always process
data ( Mansouri & Babar , 2021).
— 48 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
• Data privacy is respected by processing data at the edge while
non-sensitive and punctual information is transmitted through the
network.
3.2.2 Paradigms
Despite edge computing having multiple advantages over cloud computing, its
typical architecture described in Figure 3.1 cannot cover all decentralized data
processing applications. Therefore, researchers have been adopting variations
of the typical edge computing paradigm, i.e., mobile edge computing, cloudlets,
and fog computing.
• Fog computing extends the cloud computing paradigm by translating
the resources and services from the cloud to edge nodes ( Yousefpour et al.,
2019). This paradigm facilitates the distribution of computing and storage
resources along the path connecting IoT devices and cloud computing,
ensuring user expectations. As a result, fog computing encompasses a
wide range of devices, whether they possess computing, storage, or network
capabilities. These devices include single computers, wireless/wired access
points, network devices, and others.
• Mobile edge computing gained interest due to the proliferation of
mobile devices and the need to manage their constrained resources
efficiently ( Abbas et al. , 2018). This computing paradigm is a subset of
Fog Computing because it provides virtualized computing resources with
moderate capabilities near resource-constrained mobile devices. The key
idea of this paradigm is to enable mobiles to be served by edges when users
connect to train stations, stadiums, shopping, and other mobile locations
like cars.
• Cloudlets are small-scale cloud data centers or server clusters
strategically positioned at the network edge ( Satyanarayanan et al. ,
2009). Cloudlets act as intermediate points between end-user devices
and the network’s core while balancing local processing and offloading
computational tasks to the network’s center (Dr Suresha & Shaheen, 2023).
3.2.3 Applications
The advantages of edge computing have paved the way for a multitude of
diverse applications across various domains. Some notable examples include
the implementation of smart cities, smart homes, health monitoring systems,
the advancement of self-driving cars, and others. Depending on the functional
requirements of these applications, engineers can implement more than one of
the following edge computing foundations:
• Real-Time application execution based on data processing is usually
supported by fog computing and mobile edge computing. Emergent
applications, e.g., self-driving cars or healthcare systems for patients in
— 49 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
emergency circumstances, aim to improve throughput between IoT and
edge nodes by processing data at the network’s perimeter, e.g., fog servers.
• Cloud offloading refers to data produced and consumed at the border
(W. Shi et al. , 2016). In cloud computing applications, e.g., video
streaming, only the data is cached at the border. On the other hand,
in edge computing, data processed and operations should be cached at the
border.
• Collaborative edge refers to the collaboration among multiple
organizations ( W. Shi et al. , 2016). In cloud computing, data owned by
various organizations is rarely shared due to privacy concerns and the
formidable cost of data transportation. However, edge nodes can also be
part of the logical concept by enabling the sharing of processed and/or
non-confidential data.
• Data analytics at the fog/edge permitted converting modern industries
thanks to the widespread adoption of IoT and monitoring sensors
(Dr Suresha & Shaheen , 2023). This capability represents a significant
departure from traditional computing paradigms, making it one of the
essential foundations of edge computing.
• Edge AI is a modern way of AI/ML allowed by computationally more
efficient edge computers (Singh & Gill, 2023). Data-centralized approaches
become impractical in mission-sensitive systems where data is inherently
distributed across multiple owners. Given that distributed data can take
too much time to be transmitted to a central server, real-time inference
takes comparatively little computing power at the network’s edge. Despite
edge AI reducing latency and maintaining data locally, training an AI
model on a large dataset is analogous to cloud computing. In this AI
application, data privacy remains a concern when model updating is
needed because it implies uploading data to the network core. For tackling
this problem, Federated Learning is a viable solution.
3.3 Federated learning
Federated Learning ( FL) is a highly advantageous technique in the realm of
edge computing applications, effectively harnessing the computational power of
edge nodes and the wealth of distributed data generated by edge data producers
(McMahan, Moore, Ramage & Arcas , 2016). With its ability to facilitate local
model training, Federated Learning has emerged as a favoured solution for
tackling privacy concerns in edge AI (X. Wang et al., 2019). Enabling AI models
to be trained directly on local devices ensures that sensitive data remains under
the control of its owners and is not transmitted in its raw form. The strength of
FL lies in its iterative knowledge aggregation approach, where parameters of ML
methods, like Artificial Neural Networks ( ANNs), are collaboratively treated to
construct an AI global model.
— 50 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
3.3.1 Components
In a typical and repeated knowledge aggregation process based on edge AI,
edge nodes interact throughout the intervention of a central server orchestrated
by a communication-computation framework ( Abdulrahman et al. , 2021).
Considering a collaborative prognosis problem that monitors more than one
machine type, Figure 3.2 illustrates the main components of a typical federated
environment, including:
Figure 3.2 : Components of FL applied to collaborative prognosis.
• Nodes, also known as federated beneficiaries or parties, are data and AI
model owners.
• The central server is a communication manager between edge nodes
and usually stores the AI global model resulting from the federation.
• Communication-computation framework refers to the federated
solution that trains the AI global model ( Kallista et al. , 2022).
3.3.2 Key steps
Federated Learning succeeds by executing a series of K knowledge aggregation
iterations, which can be determined beforehand or conducted depending on a
stop-learning criterion, e.g., running until achieving the convergence problem.
Four standard steps are performed consecutively at each knowledge aggregation
iteration, as illustrated in Figure 3.3. Those standard steps include 1) AI global
— 51 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
Figure 3.3 : Standard steps of FL performed at each global model computation.
model broadcasting, 2) local training, 3) isolated model uploading, and 4) AI
global model computing.
Once the AI global model is initialized at k = 0 and its prediction performance
is estimated, the central server broadcasts it to edge nodes. Local training
refers to fitting a AI model at the edge node using data of a particular
FL beneficiary. Then, each node uploads its model to the central server in
which the AI global model is computed depending on the operations defined
by the communication-computation framework or FL solutions. Finally, k is
incremented by one, and the four key steps are repeated for a new knowledge
aggregation iteration.
3.3.3 Scale of federation
Considering the unavailability and mobility of data producers, Federated
Learning considers that not all edge nodes are available to share their models
at the k-th knowledge aggregation iteration. Depending on the scale of the
federation, which is based on the number of edge nodes or parties and their
computational resources, Federated Learning have considered two application
types, i.e., cross-device and cross-silo FL (Q. Li, Wen, Wu et al. , 2019).
• Cross-device considers the participation of numerous edge nodes or
parties in business-to-consumer (B2C) transactions.
• Cross-silo considers a small number of edge nodes or parties,
usually organizations or companies, fitting a business-to-business (B2B)
transaction.
— 52 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
Edge nodes of cross-device FL are sometimes unavailable for every aggregation
iteration. Therefore, this B2C transaction may be better in mobile edge
computing. On the other hand, beneficiaries of cross-silo FL are highly available
and could hold their own computational servers (Kallista et al., 2022). Therefore,
this B2B transaction may be better with cloudlets. Notably, the business
model highly influences the adoption of a determined computing paradigm.
However, Federated Learning is not closed for adopting central server-dependent
architectures.
3.3.4 Types of architecture
Federated Learning literature identifies two distinct architectures, distinguished
by the approach used to aggregate local models: centralized and decentralized
(Brecko et al. , 2022; Khan et al. , 2020). The choice between these architectures,
illustrated in Figure 3.4, determines whether the aggregation process occurs
centrally or is distributed across multiple nodes.
The centralized FL architecture refers to the typical edge computing paradigm
in which edge nodes upload their local AI models to the central server and
compute the AI global model. On the other hand, the decentralized FL does
not consider central server participation because nodes can orchestrate model
aggregations in peer-to-peer (P2P), graph, or blockchain networks.
Figure 3.4 : Centralized vs decentralized federated approaches.
Choosing between centralized and decentralized FL architectures mainly
depends on four factors: privacy, security, easy implementation, and desired
prediction performance. While centralized FL is easier to implement, and its AI
global model can present a prediction performance close to a model trained with
all network available data, decentralized FL aims to fill the privacy and security
gap by storing the knowledge in a single server ( Brecko et al. , 2022). While
decentralized Federated Learning offers increased flexibility regarding node
availability, opting for this approach can significantly hamper the prediction
performance of the AI global model.
— 53 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
3.3.5 Collaborative prognosis data properties
The adoption of ML methods mainly depends on the supervised,
semi-supervised, and unsupervised learning approach and the problem to solve:
classification, regression, and clustering. Federated Learning is an extensive area
that proposes solutions for almost all ML problems, and learning approaches.
Therefore, defining the data properties for the collaborative prognosis problem
helps into defining the research scope. Focusing on the collaborative health state
prediction of aircraft systems, such as cooling units and turbofan engines, the
following data properties are identified:
• Labeled data is expected to configure a supervised learning approach
Y =F (X) in whichY represents a health state and X means the condition
indicators.
• Time-series data comprise multiple measurements by conducting
condition monitoring. A single time series, associated with a unique
failure identifier, describes the machine degradation until experiencing a
failure. Because each edge node collects data from multiple failures, its
local dataset comprises multiple run-to-failure trajectories.
• Non-independent and Non-identically Distributed Data
(non-i.i.d.) means that each edge node generates its private dataset
based on its unique environment ( D. Chen et al. , 2021). Hence, no
local dataset will be representative of the population distribution. This
supposes one of the big challenges of FL because it causes model drift
among edge nodes and, thus, the prediction performance fluctuation of
the AI global model.
• Unbalanced data refers to some nodes that will generate more data than
others, varying in amounts of local data.
3.3.6 Data partitioning settings
Among data properties, the adoption of specific federated solutions is also
influenced by the distribution of samples and features across nodes’ datasets
within the federated network. Federated Learning literature identifies three data
partitioning settings: horizontal, vertical, and hybrid ( Q. Li, Wen & He , 2019;
Su & Lau , 2021). These data partition settings can be generalized considering
that a dataset D of N data samples (composed of inputs X and labels Y ), M
features and shape {Xi∈RM,Y i}N
i=1 is distributed along J edge nodes. The
shape of the i-th input sample corresponds to Xi∈RM because every sample
{p1,p 2,...,p M}is composed of M features.
Considering a federated network composed of three edge nodes, differences
between data partitioning settings are illustrated in Figure 3.5 and defined in
Table 3.2. In Figure 3.5, coloured boxes represent inputs X and grey boxes
represent labels Y of different edge nodes. In Table 3.2, Nj and Mj represent
the number of samples and features of the j-th node. Therefore, its dataset Dj
takes a shape of {Xj
i∈RM j
,Y i}N j
i=1.

• Horizontal data partitioning considers that each edge node j is the
owner of different samples but shares the same feature space M with the
other beneficiaries. In this context, the global dataset D is formed by the
union∪J
jNj of J different edge node datasets, each of size Nj.
• Vertical data partitioning considers that the j-th edge node dataset
Dj of shape{Xj
i∈RMj,Y i}N
i=1 is composed of N samples. While the j-th
edge node has the input space Xj∈RMj of dimension Mj, the dimension
of the global dataset D corresponds to the union of the feature space ∪J
jMj
of J edge nodes.
• Hybrid data partitioning considers that the j-th edge node dataset
Dj of shape {Xj
i ∈RMj,Y i}N j
i=1 is composed of Nj samples with an input
spaceXj∈RMj of dimension Mj. Horizontal and vertical data partitions
are exceptional hybrid cases because they might differ in sample or feature
space.

Depending on the data partitioning setting, three FL categories emerge:
Horizontal Federated Learning ( HFL), Vertical Federated Learning ( VFL), and
Hybrid Federated Learning ( Hybrid-FL). Various FL solutions have been
proposed for these categories. While some ML methods have adopted for HFL
and VFL, other methods integrate both federated approaches via Hybrid-FL,
making the classification of federated solutions difficult.
— 55 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
3.4 Federated solutions
Categorization of existing federated solutions has been challenging due to the
vast number of ML methods. This section reviews federated solutions based on
the core idea adopted to optimize the global cost function F (w), the method
used to deal with non-i.i.d. data, and the data partitioning settings supported,
in which w represents the weight space. In Figure 3.6, solutions are grouped
based on the core idea used to optimize F (w). At the same time, they are
tagged by color boxes that represent the FL category allowed, i.e., HFL, VFL
and Hybrid-FL.
Figure 3.6 : Tentative classification of federated solutions.
• Adaptive optimization methods generally aim to accelerate or reduce
learning fluctuations given by the well-known federated solution named
Federated A veraging ( FedA vg) ( Q. Li, Wen, Wu et al. , 2019). These
solutions are called adaptive because they adapt the learning process of the
standard Stochastic Gradient Descent ( SGD) optimization algorithm by
correcting weight updating to deal with data heterogeneity mainly. Among
the most reused solutions of this type are: Federated Adaptive Moment
Estimation (FedADAM) (Reddi et al., 2020), Federated Adaptive Gradient
(FedADAGRAD), Federated YOGI (FedYOGI), Momentum Federated
Learning (MFL) ( W. Liu et al. , 2019), Fair Federated Learning with
Momentum (F AIR-F ATE) (Salazar et al. , 2023), Stochastic Controlled
A veraging for Federated Learning (SCAFFOLD) ( Karimireddy et al. ,
2019), Federated Dynamic Regularization (FedDyn) ( Acar et al. , 2021),
Federated Proximal Term (FedProx) ( Sahu et al. , 2018), Federated
Stochastic Variance Reduced Gradient (FSVRG) ( D. Chen et al. , 2021),
Federated Newton-Type Method (FedDANE) ( T. Li et al. , 2020),
Variance Reduced Local SGD (VRL-SGD) ( Liang et al. , 2019), Federated
— 56 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
Decoupling and Correction (FedDC) ( Gao et al. , 2022) and Federated
Learning scheme via maximizing correlation (FedMac) ( X. Liu et al. ,
2023).
The listed solutions are model-agnostic methods because they allow
adopting ML methods compatible with SGD optimization algorithm.
In other words, the global cost function optimization F (w) of those
federated solutions can fit a wide range of AI models, including Neural
Networks (NNs), Support Vector Machines (SVM) and Linear Regression
(LR) and graphical models. However, most of these federated solutions
were conceived for supporting horizontal data partitioning. The only
federated solution of this group that supports hybrid data partitioning is
the Hierarchical Federated Learning (Hierarchical-FL) ( Su & Lau, 2021), a
solution that defined a multitier-partitioned NN architecture to decompose
the prediction problem at the sample and feature space by adopting a
primal-dual transform.
• Neural matching/alignment based solutions have been adopted as an
alternative to the SGD optimization (Q. Li, Wen, Wu et al. , 2019). These
solutions aggregate the knowledge of multiple participants by exploiting
the architecture of classic neural networks. By matching neurons,
federated solutions such are Probabilistic Federated Neural Matching
(PFNM) ( Yurochkin et al. , 2019), Federated Matching A veraging FedMA
(FedMA) (H. Wang et al. , 2020) and Feature-Aligned Federated Learning
(Fed2) ( Yu et al. , 2021) have required less number of knowledge
aggregation iterations K to achieve the improve prediction results of
federated solutions SGD-based.
By taking advantage of the architecture of CNNs, the Federated Group
Knowledge Transfer (FedGKT) solution transfers and distills knowledge to
a server-side CNN with a large capacity. By considering local and global
feature extractors, the Hybrid Federated Matched A veraging (HyFEM)
solution aggregates local models by matching their parameters ( X. Zhang
et al. , 2020a).
• Multitask Learning (MTL) consider fitting separated but related
models simultaneously. These solutions do not follow a typical FL
procedure where edge nodes contribute to constructing a AI global model.
To deal with non-i.i.d. data, the MOCHA solution considers fitting a
matrix of weight vectors for each task, where each task corresponds to
a model of a single edge node. It also constructs a matrix of model
relationships among tasks by applying primal-dual optimization ( Smith
et al. , 2017). The federated solutions FedU and its decentralized version
dFedU, outperformed MOCHA in MTL settings by using Laplacian
regularization to explicitly leverage the relationships among the models
of edge nodes for MTL ( Dinh et al. , 2021).
• Meta-Learning solutions train a model on various tasks to construct a
generalization knowledge that can be fine-tuned or adapted to solve a new
task or edge problem. Federated solutions, such as FedMeta and MAML,
— 57 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
are agnostic models because they adjust the SGD optimization algorithm
to solve different issues by using a variety of ML methods. While the
AI global model of MAML is trained using a small number of gradient
steps with a small amount of training data from a new task, FedMeta
shares a meta-learner instead of a AI global model. Despite these federated
solutions require fewer model aggregation iterations K than FedA vg, they
are limited to horizontal data partitioning settings.
• Decision Trees (DT) based solutions aim mainly to support vertical
data partitioning settings and achieve similar performances obtained in a
data-centralized approach, in which a single model is trained with D stored
in a central server. While SecureBoost ( Cheng et al. , 2019) and SimFL
(Q. Li, Wen & He , 2019) have conceived for HFL and VFL, FederBoost
(Tian & et al. , 2020) was designed for both data partitioning settings.
• Coordinated optimization based solutions also aim to deal with vertical
data partitioning but can be used by ML methods compatible with SGD
optimization algorithm. Federated solutions, e.g., Federated Stochastic
Block Coordinate Descent (FedBCD) and Vertical Asynchronous
Federated Learning (V AFL), extend from parallel SGD to synchronize the
weight updating for given blocks or batches of samples. With this purpose,
these federated solutions require edge nodes to synchronize their sample
indices first and then share their predictions.
The following sections, dedicated to introducing federated solutions on
horizontal, vertical, and hybrid data partitioning settings, focus on describing
those based on the SGD optimization. It is because of the usefulness of SGD in
training a wide ML methods, especially in artificial neural networks.
3.4.1 Horizontal federated learning
This section introduces the fundamental concepts of federated solutions that
support horizontal data partitioning on a supervised ML approach. That is,
input parametersXi are mapped to the output label Yi throughF (w). Federated
solutions based on adaptive optimization methods are presented in two groups,
depending on whether they perform the optimization operation at the edge node
or the central server. The Algorithm 1 describes the difference between both
groups by detailing the four FL standard steps: 1) AI global model broadcasting,
2) local training, 3) isolated model uploading, and 4) AI global model computing.
3.4.1.1 Optimization at the central server

weights result from minimizing the loss function fj

accurate the model is predicting the i-th data sample.

Gradient Descent (GD) and Stochastic Gradient Descent ( SGD) optimizations
algorithms to understand the federated solutions referred to in Algorithm 1 .
• Gradient Descent (GD) is a first-order optimization algorithm used to
minimize a loss function fj
i (wj) by iteratively moving in the direction of

where the learning rate η >0 determines how much the weights are
updated to reach a (local) minimum. Because the number of samples
Nj is typically very large, GD seems impractical, needing to process the
whole dataset Dj to evaluate a single gradient and update the model.
Gradient descent can experience significant acceleration through the
utilization of stochastic sampling methods. This is primarily because,
despite the convergence of GD, the algorithm still necessitates at least one
pass over all the data in each iteration i to update the model’s parameters.
• Stochastic Gradient Descent ( SGD) is an alternative to GD. This
algorithm samples a random function f (Xj
i,Y j

random training of Nj data points i∈{1, 2, 3,..., Nj}in an iteration i
to perform the update Equation 3.3.
Because updating wj at every i is unpractical, an average of gradients
over a batch of randomly chosen samples is usually applied. This method
works because i is sampled uniformly at random from i∈{1, 2...,N j}and
the update direction is an unbiased estimate of the gradient ∇. Thus, this
optimization algorithm is the base of most federated solutions.
Federated Averaging ( FedAvg)
Federated A veraging (FedA vg) is an elegant solution used to train a variety of
— 59 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
ML methods using SGD optimization algorithm. Given its simplicity, FedA vg
inspired the use of adaptive optimization methods in federated approaches.
As Algorithm 1 describes in Line 2, the federated optimization starts with
the central server broadcasting the model weights of the current knowledge
aggregation iteration. Then, each node fits and updates its model weights
by calculating ∇fj
i (wj) with i∈Nj samples. The local SGD optimization,
described in the third nested for loop, can be extended to multiple local training
epochs E. Despite Algorithm 1 does not refer to various epochs, the learning
procedure for the j-th party during various epochs can be implemented by just
adding a nested for loop between Lines 4-11.
Once edge nodes calculate their gradients in Line 12, they send them to
the central server, which calculates the gradient of the AI global model at a
determined epoch by averaging those local gradients. Then, the global model is
updated using the criterion of Line 18.
The momentum term of FedA vgcorresponds to the negative gradient because
it does not use a non-zero β1 and past timestep variation v. Therefore, global
weights are updated using Equation 3.3. Although FedA vgrequires relatively
few iterations to train the model, it converges to the stationary points of a
mismatched objective function in the presence of heterogeneous local steps.
In other words, FedA vg presents an objective inconsistency problem mainly
in the presence of non-i.i.d. data. Thus, it inspired the implementation of
adaptive optimization solutions at the central server, such as Momentum FL,
FedADAGRAD, FedADAM, and others.
Momentum Federated Learning (Momentum FL)
Momentum is a method that aims to reveal good learning directions to damp
oscillations given by SGD. Therefore, it has been used to design the Momentum
Federated Learning solution ( W. Liu et al. , 2019). Stochastic Gradient Descent
with momentum requires fewer iterations to reach a “local” minimum and
recommends directions. It is possible by adding a fraction of the update vector of
the past time step mκ− 1 to the current update vector mκby setting a smoothing
constant 0<β1 < 1:
mκ=β1mκ− 1 + (1−β1)∇Fκ(w). (3.4)
The federated version of this solution considers that w is updated as follows:
wκ+1 =wκ−ηmκ,
because v = 1 and ϵ≈0+. Please, see Equation 3.5.
Finding an ideal value for β1 might be difficult. Thus, ( Felbab, Kiss &
Horváth, 2019) recommended setting β1 in 0.9 because it often accelerates the
learning process and helps to get over noisy gradients and local minima ( Felbab
et al. , 2019). Other federated solutions have been implemented with the same
vision, such as Adaptive Gradient, ADAM, etc.

Federated Adaptive Gradient (FedADAGRAD)
Adaptive Gradient (ADAGRAD) adapts the learning rate ηduring its run-time,
performing smaller and larger updates ( Duchi, Hazan & Hazan , 2011).
FedADAGRAD updates its weights wκ separately during each knowledge
aggregation κ:
wκ+1 =wκ−η mκ

where ϵ≈0+ and vκ+1 is a matrix containing the squared sum of the past
gradients
vκ=vκ− 1 +∇Fκ(w)2.
Federated Adaptive Moment Estimation (FedADAM)
The federated version of Adaptive Moment Estimation (ADAM) can be viewed
as the combination of adaptive learning rate and momentum ( Felbab et al. ,
2019). Considering two smoothing constants β1 and β2, the decaying averages
of past mκis defined in Equation 3.4, while the past squared gradients vκis
defined as follows:
vκ=β2vκ− 1 + (1−β2)∇Fκ(w)2. (3.6)
The federated solution performing optimization at the central server are
elegant because global weights are updated using Equation 3.5. However, there
is not only one way to work with adaptive optimization methods. Some methods
handle the optimization at the network edge.
3.4.1.2 Optimization at the edge node
Multiple federated solutions handle the optimization locally. Federated
Proximal Term (FedProx) and Federated Stochastic Variance Reduced Gradient
(FedSVRG) are presented in this section to illustrate the modifications required
in the third nested for loop of Algorithm 1.
Federated Proximal Term (FedProx)
This federated solution aims to provide convergence guarantees when learning
over non-i.i.d. (Sahu et al., 2018). To do that, FedProx perturbs the cost function
Fj
κ(wj) at the th- κknowledge aggregation by adding the following quadratic
term µ
2||wj−wκ||2, where the proximal term µlimits the impact of local updates.
Instead of just minimizing the local cost function Fj
κ(wj), the node j uses
its local solver of choice to approximately minimize the surrogate objective
expressed in Line 14 of Algorithm 1.
The proximal term 0 < µ <1 is beneficial because this federated solution
addresses the issue of statistical heterogeneity by restricting the local updates
to be closer to the initial AI global model.
— 62 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
Federated Stochastic Variance Reduced Gradient (FedSVRG)
This federated solution was designed to reduce the communication between edge
nodes and the central server ( D. Chen et al. , 2021). FedSVRG differs from
the other SGD-based because the limited communication principle is of the
utmost importance, and minimizing the number of knowledge aggregations is
the principal goal. Stochastic Variance Reduced Gradient (SVRG), the base of
this federated solution, subsamples the available local samples Nj to accelerate
learning procedures.
This federated solution runs in two nested loops. In the outer loop presented
in Line 13 of Algorithm 1, it computes the full gradient of the whole loss function,
∇f (wk). On the other hand, in the inner loop presented in Line 9 of Algorithm
1, the update step is indicated as follows:
wji,κ+1 =wκ−η(∇fj
i,κ(wj)−∇fi,κ(wκ) +∇f (wκ))
3.4.2 Vertical federated learning
A vertical data partitioning setting refers to datasets of edge nodes not sharing
the same feature space but the same sample space. To collaboratively train a
federated model with this data partitioning setting, Federated Learning based
on the following principles:
1. The central server or a single edge node stores the column representing
labels Y of each participant.
2. Edge nodes share the same sample identifiers.
Vertical Federated Learning solution presented in this section use a sample
index synchronization strategy to guarantee that all nodes use the i-th sample
index with the i-th same label Yi correctly. It allows coordinating the SGD
optimization required to fit a AI model collaboratively.
Locally, the problem of a single node j corresponds to finding the weights
wj that minimize the local loss function fj
i (wj) at the i-th input sample.
Collaboratively, a global loss function θ(. ) evaluates how well the aggregation of
J local predictions, weighted by ψ, predicts Yi. In other words, θ(∑J
j=1ψˆYj
i ,Y i)
evaluates the distance between collaborative prediction ∑J
j=1ψˆYj
i and Yi for
the i-th sample. Because evaluating N samples individually is impractical, the
following global cost F (w) function is considered:

Vertical Federated Learning solutions that have adopted Equation 3.7 to fit AI
models by coordinating SGD optimization are: Vertical Asynchronous Federated
Learning (V AFL) (T. Chen et al. , 2020), Federated Block Coordinated Descent

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
(FedBCD) (Novikova et al. , 2022) and Vertical Federated Deep Learning ( Dai
et al. , 2021). The learning procedure of these solutions is based on privately
exchanging the local predictions with the central server, which constantly
updates a prediction matrix ANxJ of N rows and J columns.
Considering a federated network with three nodes, Figure 3.7 illustrates how
the node’s data is index-alighted to update multiple participants’ predictions in
the same row of matrix A. As models of federated beneficiaries may differ in
input parameters (yellow circles) and the architecture of local AI models differ,
the central server constructs the AI global model by joining nodes’ parameters.
Adopting the standard steps of FL, numbered by green circles in Figure 3.7,
a single knowledge aggregation iteration κis performed as follows:
1. AI global model broadcasting differs from Horizontal Federated
Learning approach. As the architecture of AI models of federated
beneficiaries may differ because they may use different input parameters,
there is no restriction to broadcast global model initial parameters. In the
case of global model architecture is defined a priori, a federated model is
initialized at the end of the first knowledge aggregation iteration κ= 1.
2. Local training refers to privately fitting the edge’s model using input
samples and predictions of determined sample indices. Block-wise
coordination supports weight updating to minimize communication
overhead by computing the local cost function Fj
κ(wj) for a given
mini-batch of size B. Collaborative predictions allow calculating the local
loss function derived w.r.t. the activation function of the last layer L of
the local model φL multiplied by the remaining chain rule derivatives to
back-propagate the error. The resulting gradient of this operation is used
to calculate the weights wj
κ+1 for the next κAI global model computation
time until achieving the problem convergence.
3. Isolated model uploading is performed after edge nodes updated local
weightswj
κ. Among edge nodes send local predictions ˆYj to update matrix
AN ×J , they may also send weights to construct a AI global model by
joining the node’s parameters.
4. AI global model computation comprises 1) creating a bigger model
that joins local parameters wj
κofJ edge nodes, and 2) updating the matrix
AN ×J by using local predictions ˆYj.
3.4.3 Hybrid federated learning
Federated solutions supporting hybrid data partitioning are scarce but widely
required because datasets of organizations interested in collaborative scenarios
usually differ in sample and feature space. Initially, Transfer Learning (TF)
was adopted to reuse knowledge from a source dataset to a similar target one
with a sub-feature space ( Q. Li, Wen, Wu et al. , 2019). Unfortunately, most
of the Federated Transfer Learning (FTL) studies asses classification problems.
In regression problems, the layers of an ANN (excepting the final layer) are
— 64 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
Figure 3.7 : Index synchronization of vertical federated learning algorithms.
frozen and transferred to a supervised learning task. By doing so, an approach
demonstrated feasibility by constructing prognosis models via Linear Regression
(LR) ( Obst et al. , 2022).
A model aggregation and fusion of models’ features in time-series form was
designed for regression problems ( Y. Hu et al. , 2019). This aggregation approach
merged models’ features before aggregating them using the well-known FedA vg
solution. Unfortunately, this approach risks losing valuable information by
fusing input signals.
Recently, a Hierarchical Federated Learning was designed ( Su & Lau , 2021).
This federated solution constructs a multitier-partitioned Neural Networks
architecture to separate the optimization problem in feature and sample space.
Because this solution adopts the Stochastic Gradient Descent Ascent (SGDA)
solution, it is compatible with Logistic Regression and SVMs. Unfortunately, it
is limited to simple models consistent with the primal-dual transform to apply
the SGDA solution. As an alternative, Hybrid Federated Matched A veraging
(HyFEM) uses distance functions (e.g., Euclidean norm) and neural matching
to solve a closed-from and classification problem ( X. Zhang et al. , 2020a).
However, this novel federated solution does not present alternatives for regression
problems.
3.5 Frameworks
This section briefly describes frameworks to facilitate the exploration of
Federated Learning. While TensorFlow Federated (TFF) 4, Federated AI
Technology Enabler (F ATE) 5, PaddlePaddle 6 and PySyft 7 are open-source
4https://github.com/tensorflow/federated
5https://github.com/FederatedAI/F ATE
6https://github.com/PaddlePaddle/PaddleFL
7https://github.com/OpenMined/PySyft
— 65 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
frameworks distributed under the Apache 2 license, NVIDIA Clara 8 is a
proprietary framework.
• TFF from Google Inc. extends the capabilities of TensorFlow to enable
distributed ML and computation across multiple devices or servers. It
focuses on privacy-preserving and decentralized learning scenarios where
data is generated and stored locally on different devices or nodes. TFF
allows developers to define complex models using TensorFlow’s standard
Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) and then distribute them
across federated devices.
• F ATE from Webank’s AI department is now an Apache Software
Foundation (ASF) top-level project. F ATE incorporates cryptographic
techniques to securely process data and model updates without exposing
sensitive information. F ATE supports multiple platforms, including Linux
and Kubernetes, and it can be accessed using various programming
languages like Python (TensorFlow and PyTorch), Java, and Go. Because
it allows horizontal, vertical, and hybrid data partitioning settings, F ATE
is suitable for industrial and enterprise-scale applications.
• PaddlePaddle from Baidu (one of China’s leading technology
companies) primarily focuses on DL methods, and it is particularly
well-suited for tasks involving natural language processing (NLP) and
computer vision. It provides high-level APIs and pre-built components
that allow developers to construct neural networks quickly without
extensive knowledge of the underlying details. PaddlePaddle supports
both Python (TensorFlow and PyTorch) and C++ interfaces, making it
accessible to a broader community of developers and researchers.
• PySyft from OpenMined extends the capabilities of famous
DL frameworks, e.g., PyTorch and TensorFlow, by incorporating
privacy-preserving techniques. PySyft is crucial in promoting responsible
AI development by addressing privacy concerns. By leveraging PySyft,
developers and researchers can contribute to advancing privacy-preserving
AI technologies while empowering individuals to maintain control over
their data.
• NVIDIA Clara is an application framework mainly related to
healthcare applications that require image processing. Developers can
accelerate medical research because NVIDIA Clara includes full-stack
GPU-accelerated and provides the Python-based Clara Train API.
3.6 Challenges
Researchers are constantly working to overcome challenges in federated
networks, such as improving communication efficiency, addressing system
heterogeneity, ensuring data privacy, etc. Given the properties of distributed
8https://developer.nvidia.com/clara
— 66 —

CHAPTER 3. FEDERATED LEARNING
condition monitoring data presented in the collaborative prognosis, the following
chapters aim to address FL challenges related to hybrid data partitioning,
non-i.i.d. and unbalanced data.
As collaborative prognosis refers to a forecasting problem and most of the
FL solutions were conceived for classification problems, the present research
work has the challenge of extending federated solutions to regression models.
If dealing with a non-i.i.d. and unbalanced data in forecasting problems is
challenging, handling a hybrid data partitioning is even worse because the
datasets of federated participants may differ in feature and sample space.
Literature suggests that federated AI models present reductions in prediction
performance compared to models trained with all available network data.
However, edge nodes’ models aim to present performance improvements when
they benefit from knowledge from other network participants. It imposes the
challenge of proposing agnostic federated solutions that improve individuals’
prediction performance, taking the prediction performance of a model trained
with all the available data as a reference. Of course, without compromising data
privacy.
3.7 Summary
This chapter introduced Federated Learning ( FL) as a key enabler for
collaborative prognosis, given the following facts:
1. Collaborative prognosis is a problem in which data and AI model owners
aim to improve the prediction performance of their forecasting models
without compromising data privacy,
2. Federated Learning is a privacy-preserving and decentralized Machine
Learning (ML) approach that respects data privacy legislative regulations
by aggregating knowledge of AI models instead of sharing raw data with
other federated beneficiaries.
As Federated Learning fits the edge computing paradigm, this chapter
starts by describing the benefits and applications of this paradigm. Then,
Federated Learning emerged as a privacy-preserving approach to developing
edge AI applications. After presenting the components, key steps, and types of
this decentralized ML approach, this chapter describes the federated solutions
available in the literature for three data partitioning settings: horizontal,
vertical, and hybrid. Hybrid Federated Learning, the FL category that addresses
hybrid data partitioning gains importance in this chapter because it fits the
data distribution approaches of collaborative prognosis, mainly when condition
monitoring data of different data owners differ in feature space. After presenting
existing FL frameworks, the last section presented the challenges to overcome
in the development of collaborative solutions.

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
A
ircraft health monitoring is a technique that promises to reduce
aircraft on ground and maintenance, repair and operations through
Condition-Based Maintenance ( CBM) and Predictive Maintenance ( PdM)
solutions. Particularly, this chapter describes the development process of
data-driven models that estimate the Remaining Useful Life ( RUL) of aircraft
safety-critical systems or aircraft systems whose inoperability would impose
operational limitations, such as cooling units and turbofan engines. This chapter
mainly focuses on answering RQ1 while highlighting the importance of gathering
run-to-failure data from real aircraft and the challenge of identifying health
degradation indications. Given that the models obtained in this chapter aim
to serve as a baseline comparison for federated solutions in the next chapters,
this chapter concentrates on developing prognosis models in a data-centralized
scenario where data from equivalent aircraft systems is placed in a central server.
Parts of this chapter have been published in the following research articles:
• Cooling units: J2 and C4.
• Turbofan engines: J1, J3, C5, C3 and C2.
4.1 Introduction
The potential of aircraft health monitoring arises in real-time detection
or estimating the advent of potential failures in aircraft safety-critical
systems. These estimations are primarily analyzed using Condition-Based
Maintenance ( CBM) and Predictive Maintenance ( PdM) strategies, with
Predictive Maintenance ( PdM) being the most adequate for balancing aircraft
availability and maintenance costs, though it is challenging due to its focus on
estimating Remaining Useful Life ( RUL) in flight hours.
Constructing RUL estimators via AI/ML approaches is challenging, especially
in early conception stages such as condition monitoring and signal processing.
Condition monitoring data of aircraft components studied in this research is
assumed to be gathered under the run-to-failure philosophy. Components
operate many flight cycles until they achieve their end-of-life tEoL and, therefore,
require maintenance, as Figure 4.1 illustrates.
The aircraft system’s Health Indicator ( HI) is estimated in a determined
momentti after a period or flight cycle composed of climb, cruise, and descent
flight conditions. Thus, the system’s RUL corresponds to the distance between
the end-of-life time tEoL and the estimation moment ti. This tEoL point comes
from the intersection between the health degradation curve and a prognosis
threshold, defined by experts after identifying the acceptable health indicators
domain and a health degradation function. Intermediate thresholds are usually
defined to identify key moments, e.g., the introduction to an abnormal or
accelerated health degradation stage.
The main contributions of this chapter can be summarized as follows:
— 70 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
Figure 4.1 : Health degradation of aircraft components/systems over time.
1. The description of a standard and adaptable PHM pipeline composed of
three main stages: data gathering, data pre-processing, and prognosis
model development. This pipeline guides PHM practitioners in
representing the health degradation of aircraft safety-critical systems for
then predicting their RUL via data-driven models.
2. Prognosis models for cooling units and turbofan engines developed in a
data-centralized approach for serving as baseline comparisons for federated
approaches.
This chapter is organized as follows. Section 4.2 describes a PHM pipeline
to develop data-driven prognosis models. This section describes the technical
processes performed in the three pipeline stages. Section 4.3 describes the
development process of a prognosis model for cooling units from a fleet of Boeing
787 series. Section 4.4 describes the development process of prognostics models
for digital twins of turbofan engines.
4.2 Prognostics and health management pipeline
As in every development process of predictive models through AI/ML methods,
data-gathering plays an essential role. In the case of the RUL estimators,
data-gathering aims to 1) collect data under the run-to-failure philosophy ( Jones
et al. , 2020) and 2) collect many failure instances ( Adhikari et al. , 2018).
While the first aims to collect data related to the health degradation path, the
second aims to identify health degradation patterns from multiple equivalent
components using AI/ML methods. Unfortunately, there is the possibility that
the failure advent can not generally be unable to be observed directly from
data, playing the construction of HIs via a physical-based model an essential
role. Thus, hybrid prognosis models could be constructed using outputs from
physical-based models as inputs for AI-based models.
— 71 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
The PHM pipeline for this research considers the possibility of constructing
simple AI-based models models and hybrid ones. Figure 4.2 illustrates the
development stages and the modules for constructing AI-based and hybrid RUL
estimators.
The backbone of this PHM pipeline adopted the three stages presented in
(Javed et al. , 2012), i.e., 1) data acquisition, 2) data pre-processing, and 3)
prognostic model phase. This pipeline flow has been initially conceived to
develop only AI-based approaches. This pipeline included the PHM modules
from ( Yaguo et al. , 2018) to consider the possibility of constructing hybrid
models. These processes, illustrated in grey boxes in Figure 4.2 are a)
experimental scenario, b) HI construction, c) health stage division, and d) RUL
estimation.
Figure 4.2 : Prognostics and health management pipeline.
The current pipeline, comprised of model development stages and technical processes,
guides PHM practitioners in integrating capabilities of physics-based and AI/ML models.
The listed prognostics stages describe the technical processes that can be
followed during the development of prognosis models:
• Data acquisition mainly refers to b) constructing the experimental
scenario by acquiring meaningful and enough run-to-failure data. This
stage also integrates data transformations before applying feature
engineering techniques. Transformations such as noise reduction and
data cleaning help engineers to improve the condition monitoring process
while increasing condition operation meaning. Technical processes such
as handling imbalanced data and defining development and test samples
ensure that PHM practitioners have enough and appropriate data for RUL
estimation experimentations.
• Data pre-processing refers to data transformations conducted by signal
processing or AI/ML methods before developing the prognosis model.
Feature engineering modules, such as feature extraction and feature
selection, are used to extract and select the indicators that better illustrate
the health degradation path. The current PHM pipeline, illustrated in
Figure 4.2, prioritizes adopting the data pre-processing modules from
yellow boxes when possible. When AI models do not achieve performance
expectations given non-stationary input signals, the b) HI can be derived
from physical phenomena.
• Prognostics model comprises two technical processes: c) health state
division, and d) RUL estimation. The health stage division is performed
when different health stages are assumed beforehand, given the varying
— 72 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
degradation trends of the condition indicators or processed HIs. For
example, considering two health stages, finding the diagnosis threshold
that separates an imperceptible degradation stage from an abnormal
degradation stage is well known as elbow point detection ( Baptista et al. ,
2021). Finally, the d) technical process develops the prognosis model by
adopting an AI/ML method capable of predicting the RUL within critical
health stages.
4.3 Cooling units
Data from a wide-body aircraft fleet of the Boeing 747 series were collected to
develop the ReMAP project. This project considered estimating the RUL of
Cooling Units ( CUs) from the Integrated Cooling System ( ICS) illustrated in
Figure 2.11, whose work is mainly concentrated in the Bulk Cargo Compartment.
Although cooling units are not safety-critical systems, their inoperability would
impose an operational limitation on the airline because they regulate the
temperature of the aircraft to maintain it in operable conditions ( Basora et
al., 2021).
4.3.1 Construction of the experimental scenario
Data from a major airline serve for estimating the RUL of CUs. Condition
monitoring data from the ICS system of 17 different aircraft were collected every
second for 29 months. A total of 18295 flights resulted in a dataset of 26.4 GB as
described in Table 2.1. Cooling units were not monitored under the run-to-failure
philosophy. Therefore, event data from maintenance interventions helped to
construct the experimental scenario considering the following assumptions:
1. The CUs ran until the failure before maintenance interventions.
2. The health of CUs is increased after maintenance interventions, but they
can not recover in-operation reliability.
The first assumption supports the definition of the failure threshold as
the lower HI in data. The second assumption supports the definition of an
initial RUL after a CU’s installation, overhaul or maintenance intervention.
This last assumption also enables gathering multiple run-to-failure trajectories
from the same CU when maintenance staff have documented its intervention.
Unfortunately, maintenance intervention dates and units were just provided to
the members of the ReMAP project.
Run-to-failure data construction
All run-to-failure trajectories were constructed by loading condition monitoring
data from a single aircraft tail and sorting it using the row number described in
the operating condition space W from Table 4.1. Trajectories were named with
the associated failure identifier (ID). Although 40 failures on CUs had occurred,

discarding criteria were considered in this way.
The first criterion limits considering verified failures, because 2 failures have
been labelled as “dubious,” and 10, have been labelled as “likely. ” The second
criterion limits considering run-to-failure data with representative data. There
are 7 cases in which monitoring periods do not present or present constant-value
sensor measurements in CUs. Table 4.2 describes the possible run-to-failure
trajectories regarding failure ID, aircraft tail, number of samples and flight hours.
Some run-to-failure trajectories are short because CUs were maintained within
the first 30 hours, while others surpassed the 100 hours. It suggested prioritizing
the RUL estimation within at least two days by not considering run-to-failure
trajectories with short flight hours. The minimum trajectory length was defined
while defining development and test datasets.

Noise reduction and data cleaning
Each of the four CUs from the ICS system is monitored by nine equivalent
sensors. Unfortunately, three equivalent sensors present many infinite and NaN
measures. Thus, the following sensors of the first CU were removed: xtooix*,
wqybtv*, ytauwt*. Equivalent sensors in the remaining units were also removed.
After discarding data, some of the remaining sensors were cleaned because of
their outliers. The following sensors of the first CU were then subjected to
linear interpolation with a mean threshold factor set at 1.5 ( Azevedo, Ribeiro
& Cardoso , 2020): yucqij* and wqgsfv*. Equivalent sensors from the remaining
CUs were also cleaned using the same approach because non-noisy values were
below a mean factor of 1.5.
Handling imbalanced data
The distribution of flight phases has been demonstrated to be unbalanced
because aircraft are mostly in the cruise phase during operation. Figure 2.12
illustrates the 14 flight phases on a single flight cycle performed by the Boeing
747 series, where the eighth phase corresponds to the cruise. The aggregation of
flight phases was considered to obtain the following relatively balanced phases:
start ( 1, 2), climb (3-7), cruise ( 8), descent (9-13), and finish ( 14).
Manufacturers guarantee that aircraft system degradation results from many
flight cycles. Nevertheless, there is no guarantee that degradation is proportional
to the number of flight hours, as the following linear function f (ti) suggests:
f (ti) =|tEoL−ti|, (4.1)
where ti refers to the estimation moment, and tEoL refers to the useful lifetime
determined by technicians when they intervened in the aircraft system.
Studies suggest that the health of aircraft systems experiences accelerated
degradation after a determined time, suggesting differentiating multiple
degradation stages. If CUs degrades faster within the last flight hours (e.g.,
the last 30 hours) and there are trajectories with thousands of hours, data is
unbalanced, respecting the health stages. Run-to-failure trajectories with less
than 48 flight hours were discarded to reduce this imbalance partially. The 14
trajectories from Table 4.2 result from this filtering step.
Development and test data
The trajectories for developing and testing the prognosis model were defined by
analyzing the variance of both data splits regarding flight hours.
• Training= [2, 15, 18, 21, 22, 25, 28, 107, 110, 111, 113]
• Testing= [19, 23, 30]
Those run-to-failure trajectories, whose identifier refers to the associated
anomaly ID, were selected by conducting the F-test expressed in the following

∈N. Although there is a possibility to estimate the RUL at short periods, e.g.,

4.3.2 Health indicator construction
Healthy and faulty periods in CUs have been classified through a Deep Learning
(DL) method, namely a Convolutional Neural Network ( CNN) ( Basora et al. ,
2021). Nevertheless, health degradation ensigns for RUL estimation can not be
captured through single DL methods from Figure 2.8, such as CNN, FNN and
others. Consequently, the construction of Health Indicators ( HIs) through data
transformations from time and frequency domains was considered. It supposed
the creation of a hybrid model by combining physic-based and AI models, where
the constructed HIs are the inputs of the AI model.
Data transformations from time or frequency domains have not been sufficient
to capture health degradation of CUs, such as transformations listed in Figure
2.10. It was relegated to the fact that those data transformations have
demonstrated limitations in analyzing non-stationary signals and non-linear
systems ( Heng et al. , 2009). Time-frequency data transformations deal with
this limitation by analyzing both domains in parallel, such as Short-Time
Fourier Transform (STFT), Wigner-Ville Distribution (WVD), Empirical Mode
Decomposition (EMD), Hilbert Spectrum and others.
Degradation periods discovered using Short-Time Fourier Transform
Historically, Short-Time Fourier Transform (STFT) has been one of the most
used time-frequency representations of time signals. This is because STFT,
expressed in Equation 4.3, has physical sense when linear systems and stationary
signals are analyzed. Because STFT computes Fourier Transforms in moving
windows w(�−t) centred at t, where � represents the time index, it generates a
2D time-frequency distribution (t,f) called a spectrogram.
STFT (t,f ) =
∫ ∞
−∞
x(τ)w(τ−t)e−j2πfτdτ (4.3)
Selecting an adequate moving window w depends on the application problem.
The most used ones are Hanning, Kaiser-Bessel, and Gausian. Applying STFT
to each sensor signal with a Hann window of size 129 and 50% overlap revealed
that some periods with fewer magnitude frequencies could be related to the
CUs degradation. It can be visualized in Figure 4.3 where the spectrogram of
sensor wqsfv* corresponding to the 23rd failure ID is illustrated. There are some
periods in blue with low-frequency magnitude values just before the maintenance
intervention mark by a vertical red line.

Figure 4.3 : Degradation periods of cooling units with low magnitude values.
Hilbert spectrum via Empirical Mode Decomposition
After observing that the degradation of CUs could potentially be linked to
periods with the lowest magnitude frequencies of input signals x(t), it was
considered employing a time-frequency representation adept at capturing such
occurrences. The computation of the Hilbert spectrum was considered because
it helps in distinguishing a mixture of moving signals ( Huang et al., 1998). In the
way of computing this spectrum, it is needed to: 1) mathematically decompose a
signal into Intrinsic Mode Functions ( IMFs) (see Definition 1); and 2) apply the
Hilbert transform to compute the instantaneous frequency of each component
by calculating
w(t) = dθ(t)/dt,
the derivative of the phase θw.r.t the time t (Huang et al. , 1998). The Hilbert
spectrum is approximated by performing the n IMF signals.
Definition 1. “An Intrinsic Mode Function refers to any function having the
same numbers (or at most differing by one) of zero-crossings and extrema,
and also having symmetric envelopes defined by local maxima and minima,
respectively. (Gang et al., 2010)”
In practice, the Hilbert-Huang transform (HHT) has been widely used to
obtain the Hilbert spectrum X(t) (Huang et al. , 1998):
X(t) = Re
 n

ak(t)ej



, (4.4)
whereRe[] represents the real part of terms within brackets and both ak andwk
are constants.
The HHT transform uses the Empirical Model Decomposition ( EMD)
technique to get the n IMF signals ( Gang et al. , 2010). Then the Hilbert
transform is applied to each signal. The EMD was implemented by using the
IMF definition from Definition 1 and the following steps in the sifting process:
1. Identify all the local extrema of the input signal x(t).
2. Separately connect all the maxima and minima with natural cubic spline
lines to form the upper, u(t), and lower, l(t), envelopes.
— 77 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
3. Compute the mean of the envelopes as m(t) = [u(t) +l(t)]/ 2.
4. Find a tentative IMF h(t) as the difference between the x(t) and m(t),
h(t) = x(t)−m(t).
5. Check if h(t) satisfies the IMF definition and stoppage criterion.
6. If h(t) does not satisfy the definition, repeat steps [1–5] many times til it
satisfies the definition.
7. If h(t) does satisfy the definition, assign the tentative IMF as an IMF
component, c(t).
8. Repeat steps [1–7] on the residue, r(t) = x(t)−c(t), as the data.
9. Stop when the residue contains no more than one extremum.
Physics-based Health Indicator construction
The described Health Indicator ( HI) construction approach captures the periods
in which a sensor signal x(t) experiences the lowest frequencies by computing
the Hilbert spectrum. Thus, capturing the physics of the failure. The current
Hilbert spectrum construction considers the accumulation of periods with similar
frequencies using IMFs. This physics-based model concentrates on the first IMF
because it identifies and decomposes the finest component of the shortest period
at each instant ( Hamdi et al. , 2012). In this way, the following mathematical
operations are performed to get the first IMF c1(t):
X(t)−m1, 1(t) = h1, 1(t);
m1, 1(t)−m1, 2(t) = h1, 2(t);
m1,k − 1(t)−m1,k (t) = h1,k (t);
h1,k (t) = c1(t),
where indices refer to an iteration k of each EMD step. In this case, the EMD
is applied to get the first IMFc1. In this way, k iterations are performed while:
• Difference DS, defined in Equation 4.5 according Cauchy type criterion,
satisfies SD ≮ϵ(Huang et al. , 1998).
• The term h1,k (t) does not satisfy the Definition 1.

4.3.3 Health stage division
The health degradation of CUs was initially considered within two stages: 1)
an imperceptible and 2) an abnormal degradation stage. A binary classification
problem was constructed for Equation 4.7 to identify the point that separates
both stages. This problem, also known as elbow pointdetection (Baptista et al. ,
2021), was conducted by setting in 0 the points in an imperceptible degradation
stage and setting in 1 the points in a potential abnormal degradation stage as
follows:
f (t) =

Although statistical procedures have been applied for health stage
classification, the current experiment adopted ML algorithms since they provide
accurate distinction: Multilayer Perceptron ( MLP), Random Forest Classifier
(RFC) and Support Vector Classifier (SVC). Given the many possibilities of
elbow pointin Equation 4.8, a sliding window technique was adopted to identify
the best separation as illustrated as the blue vertical line in Figure 4.4a.
Figure 4.4 : Health stage division and hyperparameter tunning.
a) Health stage division is performed via sliding window technique (vertical blue line).
b) Hyperparameter tunning using AUC and elbow point at 44 remaining flight hours.
Initially, hyperparameter tunning for MLP, RFC, and SVC models was
conducted by fixing the elbow point in 48. It resulted in the hyperparameters
identified in Table 4.3. Once hyperparameters were defined after having the
maximum values for the Area Under the Curve (AUC) illustrated in Figure
4.4b, elbow point took values from 16 to 72 with a step size of 4. This resulted
in accuracy, F1-score, and AUC values, as shown in Table 4.3.

The best classification performances regarding the F1-score and AUC were
obtained between the last 28 and 44 flight hours. The values in bold from
Table 4.3 result from averaging the classification performance of RFC, SVC and
MLP. Given the need for early estimating the remaining useful life of CUs, the
prognosis model was developed by setting the elbow point at 44.
4.3.4 Prognosis model
Predictive maintenance aims to estimate the equipment lifetime, ideally since it
starts to be monitored. When not possible, estimating the lifetime within an
abnormal or accelerated health degradation stage helps technicians to minimize
aircraft on ground.
The RUL estimation model was developed considering a piece-wise health
degradation curve defined as follows:
f (t) =
{
elbowPoint if t ≤elbowPoint
⌊tEoL−t⌉ , (4.9)
where the initial RUL in the imperceptible heath degradation stage is set with
the elbow point value, and the abnormal degradation curve is linear. This
equation’s floor and ceiling operators are limited to RUL∈N.
In RUL estimation, a MLP has been useful to perform Equation 4.7 using
the same normalized input signals for elbow point detection, i.e., Xs and Ow.
Computing many samples at a sampling rate of 1Hz requires a long training
process. Therefore, downsampling strategies have been applied. A 1
360 Hz
sampling rate significantly reduced the training process and estimation error.
The adopted multilayer perceptron comprises two hidden layers with 7 and 4
nodes. While hidden layers configure tanh activation functions, the output layer
configures a Relu function. The optimization problem is solved by configuring
a Root Mean Squared Error ( RMSE) loss function (see Equation 2.7) and the

Estimation performance
Estimation performance evaluations have been concentrated on comparing single
AI models alimented with raw data vs hybrid models thanks to HI construction.
Ten RUL estimators were created for each approach for measuring estimation
uncertainty. Table 4.4 presents the estimation performance of each approach for
three different elbow point values. In this table, HI construction outperforms
the RUL estimation performance, while values in bold refer to the performance
achieved when the RUL is prioritized to be displayed during the last 48 operating
hours. In Figure 4.5, the RUL estimation of the first CU of the aircraft enwslczm
is compared by using different inputs. Clearly, a hybrid model that considers HI
construction with time-frequency data transformations helps to capture health
degradation ensigns in the last operating flight hours.

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
4.4 Turbofan engines
In the aviation industry, monitoring turbofan units under the run-to-failure
philosophy has been difficult because failure in those units compromises
passengers and aircraft safety. To study the health degradation of those
safety-critical units, synthetic condition monitoring data from digital twins was
created using the C-MAPSS simulator.
Using labeled data from this simulator, owned by The National Aeronautics
and Space Administration - USA (NASA), RUL of turbofan units can be assessed
mainly using supervised learning strategies. Particularly, C-MAPSS repositories
differ in the number of run-to-failure trajectories contained and the number of
flight phases considered during the health degradation simulation. This chapter
uses two C-MAPSS repository versions described in Chapter 2, i.e., Turbofan
Engine Degradation Simulation ( Saxena & Goebel , 2008) and its second version,
also known as N-CMAPSS dataset ( Arias Chao et al., 2021). The main difference
between both repositories arises in the operating conditions Ow used during
health degradation simulation. Sections 4.4.1 and 4.4.2 describe RUL estimation
of turbofan units by using synthetic and real operating conditions.
4.4.1 Synthetic operating conditions
The RUL of turbofan engines has been widely assessed since the publication
of the Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation repository ( Saxena & Goebel ,
2008). This repository comprises four FD00x datasets with many run-to-failure
trajectories for development and test splits described in Table 2.2. This research
has concentrated on the FD004 dataset because besides presenting a considerable
number of run-to-failure trajectories for conducting FL studies, it covers the
maximum fault modes (2) and flight phases (6) at the same time.
Although there are diverse prognosis models in literature for FD00x datasets,
this research concentrates on models based on Artificial Neural Networks
(ANNs). Particularly, this section describes two model architectures reused in
FL experiments, i.e., a Multilayer Perceptron (MLP) followed by a Kalman Filter
(KF) know as ( MLP-KF) and a Functional Multilayer Perceptron ( FMLP).
From the variety of ML methods, MLP-KF was initially chosen because MLP
is the canonical example of federated algorithms SGD-based. Given the high
estimation error of MLP-KF, FMLP is presented as the extension of MLP that
considerably reduces the prediction error.
The content of this section is organized as follows. Section 4.4.1.1 describes
the technical processes of the two first PHM pipeline stages for ( MLP-KF)
and FMLP. Section 4.4.1.2 presents the details about ( MLP-KF) architecture.
Section 4.4.1.3 describes the details of FMLP. Finally, Section 4.4.1.4
summarizes the results achieved in both architectures.
4.4.1.1 Construction of the experimental scenario and data pre-processing
Studies suggest that turbofans degrade following a piece-wise function because
they experience an imperceptible degradation stage before entering an
— 82 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
accelerated degradation stage ( Heimes, 2008). This piece-wise degradation
function is formulated as follows:
f (t) =
{
Rc if 0≤t≤tSoF
|tEoL−t|if t SoF≤tEoL

whereRc corresponds to the initial constant RUL, which usually varies from 120
to 130 cycles ( Heimes, 2008) and SoF (start-to-failure point) corresponds to the
time when the unit starts degrading. Figures 4.6a and 4.6b illustrate how a
linear degradation function is transformed to a piece-wise degradation function
in FD004 units if Rc is set at 130.
Figure 4.6 : Definition of the health degradation function of turbofan units.

Max-Min normalization, performed by Equation 4.11, limits a sensor signal x(m)
having values within [0,1].

Z-score normalization, on the other hand, considers turbofan units to degrade
differently at each flight phase. Therefore, a clustering process is carried out
using the K-means algorithm to apply a normalization per flight phase or
operational regime r (e.g., landing, cruise and descent). While the clustering
process relates each sample given the combination of operating conditions Ow
(altitude, MN and TRA), Equation 4.12 normalizes a sample xm
i with data from

When Z-score normalization is performed, some signals must be discarded
because presenting infinite or NaN values or do not represent health degradation
very well. According to ( Saxena & K , 2008; Scott et al. , 2022) the sensors
that better represent the health degradation of turbofan units are 2, 3, 4, 6,

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 15, 17, 20, 21. Those sensor signals, referring to M
sample features, are inputs for constructing the MLP-KF. The 249 run-to-failure
trajectories from the FD004 development dataset were partitioned with an 85%
allocation for training and 15% for testing. The resulting prognosis model is
evaluated using data from the 248 units.
4.4.1.2 Multilayer Perceptron followed by a Kalman Filter (MLP-KF)
The MLP adopted is composed of three layers. The input layer takes data from
M features corresponding to sensor signals Xs and/or Ow. The hidden layer
contains 10 nodes with a sigmoid activation function. Finally, the output layer
contains only one node activated by a linear function. The loss function adopted
to optimize the weights of the MLP was the RMSE (see Equation 2.7). This is
done using the SGD algorithm with a learning rate ηof 0.001. An early stopping
callback was used to prevent overfitting, waiting for 10 epochs before stopping
when the performance during validation did not progress. Training procedure
demonstrated to be effective by configuring a maximum of 180 complete training
cycles or epochs E, in which all training samples are visualized.
Predictions from the MLP model are noisy. It can be seen in Figure 4.7, where
the estimated RUL for the first unit is illustrated in a red curve. The estimation
performance is improved by applying a kalman filter to the entire run-to-failure
state vector to get the green curve. This filter consists of prediction and update
stages executed in the inner loop of Line 2 from Algorithm 2.
The prediction stage is executed in Lines 3-5, in which:
• ˆY −
t corresponds to the priori estimate of the state vector ˆY at time t,
• P −
t corresponds to the priori error estimate matrix,
• P corresponds to the posteori error estimate matrix, and
• Q = 1/ 209 refers the degradation rate corresponding to 209 operating
cycles in average when using FD00x datasets ( Olivares et al. , 2019).
The prediction stages assume an initial conditions ˆY0 = 1 and an initial
degradation error P0 = 0 for configuring the initial imperceptible degradation
stage according to Equation 4.8. The update stage, composed of Lines 6-7,
updates the state vector ˆx and the posteriori error estimate matrix Pk; both
depending on the gain Gt. An estimate of measurement variance R =z2 is set
at 0.09 because ( Olivares et al. , 2019) adopted a heuristic evaluation z = 0. 3.
Finally, the estimated ˆRUL∈[0,Rc ] results from multiplying ˆY with the initial
constant Rc. The smoothest blue from Figure 4.7 results from applying the
kalman filter to the noisy prediction given by the MLP.
4.4.1.3 Functional Multilayer Perceptron
The Functional Multilayer Perceptron ( FMLP) extends from traditional MLP
to use functional inputs and get smoother predictions comparable with deep
learning methods, such as CNN (Beyaztas & Shang , 2022) and LSTM (Zheng
