et al. , 2017). The FMLP architecture, which adds a functional layer before
numerical hidden layers, aims to eliminate output filtering (e.g., KF) by learning
the underlying structure of continuous and smooth curves.
In the presence of non-stationary signals, functional statistics are an
alternative to feature extraction from the time domain. This proposal uses
the Functional Time Series (FTS) method because it has reached promising
step-ahead and multi-step-ahead forecasts at the beginning of the 21st
century. The FTS method decomposes smooth curves into functional principal
components and associated scores via Functional Principal Component Analysis
(FPCA) ( Beyaztas & Shang , 2022).
Adding a functional hidden layer, before numerical hidden layers from
traditional MLP, requires building functions of a finite number of observations
and variables. By building so, the functional layer receives vectors instead of
numbers and applies basis functions to extract relevant features. Input data
preparation and FMLP architecture are illustrated in Figure 4.8.
Considering that M sample features (sensor signals) are obtained by
monitoring each turbofan unit, the functional time series forecast problem
considers T -dimensional curves as functional input from RT ×M to predict the
— 85 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
Figure 4.8 : Input data preparation and FMLP architecture.
target variable from R (Rossi & Conan-Guez , 2005). Figure 4.8a illustrates
how the i-th sample of M features is divided in a finite set of T -dimensional
curves. Therefore, a FMLP input Xi, illustrated in Figure 4.8b, can be written
as [Xi, 1(t);... ;Xi,M (t)] s.t. t = 1,...,T . Considering that among sensor signals
Xs, smooth curves from operating conditions Ow may also be included to
estimate the RUL of turbofans, the forecasting problem RT ×M →R can be
described using Equation 4.7.
From numerical to functional neurons
In a typical Multilayer Perceptron ( MLP), a neuron maps an uni-dimensional
input Xi to the real output σ(wXi +b), where σis an activation function from
R to R, w a numerical weight in R, and b the bias term. Considering c neurons
from the input layer, wXi is a linear expression Rc that enables MLP to accept
multi-dimensional inputs ( Rossi & Conan-Guez , 2005).
In a Functional Multilayer Perceptron ( FMLP), a functional weight W from
thec-th neuron must be compatible with a vectorial input Xi because the input
layer gets a set of T -dimensional curves Xi ∈RT ×M . Because manipulating
linear forms on arbitrary vectorial space is simply impossible, the restriction to
use functional inputs was described in ( Rossi & Conan-Guez , 2005; Rossi et al. ,
2002). Those studies generalize to this neuron mapping as σ(W (Xi) +b), where
W (Xi) is the generalization of wXi to arbitrary vectorial space.
The current FMLP implementation assumes the presence of Borel measures
µ∈RT . This assumption enables Xi to be considered as a Lp(µ) space of
measurable functionsf from RT to R, s.t.∈|f|pδµ<∞. Therefore, a functional
neuron map elements of Lp(µ) to R, where q is the conjugate exponent of p.
Concretely, the real output of the functional hidden layer H(Xi) composed of c
functional neurons is given by:
H(Xi) =

where bc are real numbers and Wc are functions in Lq(µ).

Input data preparation differs from the previous MLP model, despite ˆRUL is also
obtained by multiplying the Y and Rc. Differences between MLP and FMLP
refer to how the last one finds health degradation ensigns. Instead of using
input data from RM as in MLP, FMLP accepts samples Xi∈RT ×M . Those
T -dimensional curves were constructed by setting T at 31. While MLP uses
the Z-score data normalization from Equation 4.12, FMLP adopts the Max-Min
normalization from Equation 4.11. Another difference arises in the number of
input sensor signals. While MLP uses sensor signals that better represent the
health degradation of turbofan units, the FMLP uses the whole sensor space
Xs because health degradation is looked at the first functional components P ,
which are defined during forward propagation by the PFVE hyperparameter.
The FMLP comprises one functional layer followed by two numerical hidden
layers. While the functional layer comprises 16 nodes, the numerical layers are
8 and 4 nodes, respectively. More details about the hyperparameters evaluated
can be found in ( Llasag Rosero, Silva & Ribeiro , 2023a). The FMLP works with
an extra hyperparameter referred to as the fraction variance explained PFVE .
After a hyperparameter optimization through the grid search method, PFVE was
set at 0.9, considering the functional components representing at least 90% of
variance.
Similarly to the traditional MLP, the weights of the FMLP were also optimized
using the SGD optimization algorithm with a learning rate of ηof 0.001.
The same percentages of data for training, evaluating and testing were used.
However, despite overfitting on FMLP was also avoided using the early stopping
algorithm, the number of epochs for looking at performance losses was set at 8
when training the FMLP.
4.4.1.4 Comparing RUL estimation of MLP-KF and FMLP architectures
Performance evaluations of MLP-KF and FMLP architectures were conducted
using data of the 248 units from the FD004 test split. A total of 30 models,
trained with different initial weights, were obtained for both architectures to
quantify performance deviations. Table 4.5 summarizes the results obtained.
The MLP presented a MAE of 20.59±2.23, a RMSE of 24.39±2.11 and ETA
of 5092.91±112.12. On the other hand, the FMLP presented a MAE of 9.25±
10.2, a RMSE of 14.63±0.97 and ETA of 201.44±17.99. Therefore, FMLP
clearly outperforms MLP-KF, especially in the ETA evaluation metric, which
— 88 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
was reduced by almost five thousand. It can be seen in Figure 4.9, in which a
RUL estimation curve is smooth without requiring output filtering. Those final
results are even comparable with DL architectures, such as CNN (Beyaztas &
Shang, 2022) and LSTM (Zheng et al. , 2017).
Figure 4.9 : RUL of the 25th turbofan from FD004 using FMLP architecture.
4.4.2 Real operating conditions
Although the RUL estimation of turbofans has been widely assessed through the
use of the Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation repository from ( Saxena
& Goebel , 2008), those studies do not address a realistic health degradation
of turbofan units during the exchange of flight conditions. While the health
degradation of turbofan units on previous C-MAPSS datasets was simulated
only using operating conditions from the standard cruise phase, the health
degradation of units in the N-CMAPSS dataset, created by ( Arias Chao et al. ,
2021), was simulated using real flight conditions recorded on board a commercial
jet. Thus, it covers the climb, cruise, and descent flight phases.
Data gathering configured a sampling rate of 0. 1Hz, being of N-CMAPSS
a large dataset of 14GBs compared to previous turbofan datasets. This new
dataset comprises various subsets DS as Table 2.3 indicates. Each DS subset
comprises two splits to facilitate developing and testing data-driven models.

Each subset comprises data from a determined number of turbofan units
experiencing a single flight class (FC), determined by the short, medium and
long flight length. Each unit experiences a certain number of fault modes as
a consequence of degradation in flow and efficiency on the following rotating
sub-component of the turbofan engine: fan, low-pressure compressor (LPC),
high-pressure compressor (HPC), high-pressure turbine (HPT) and low-pressure
turbine (LPT).
The N-CMAPSS dataset was designed to experiment with two PHM problems:
anomaly detection and failure prognosis. In this way, two types of output
targetsY were provided: health indicator hi and theoretical RUL. Current RUL
estimation experiments mainly use input variables described in Table 4.6. While
operating conditions Ow refers to the flight measurements collected from the
commercial jet, sensor variables Xs refer to the data from using the C-MAPSS
simulator.
Given the huge samples of DS sets, N-CMAPSS is ideal for experimenting
with DL algorithms (Arias Chao et al. , 2021). Despite hybrid models developed
by including physical ( Arias Chao et al. , 2022) or statistical models ( Basora et
al., 2023) outperforming single data-driven models, current experiments focus
on using ANNs compatible with the SGD optimization algorithm.
The inception-based deep Convolutional Neural Network ( CNN) from ( DeVol
— 90 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
et al. , 2021) has been adopted because it obtained promising prediction
performance on the 2021 PHM Society Data Challenge using some Ds splits. For
constructing the RUL estimation problem, data from a determined degradation
unit in a DS set is gathered by defining a turbofan unit identifier. This identifier
was increased DS-hundred times to facilitate its finding because there are units
with the same identifier in different DS sets. Because a measure per second
implies high computation costs, data is subsampled to have one measure every
20 seconds.
The pre-processing stage starts by normalizing Ow and Xs variables using
the following Z-score normalization version:

f (t) =
{

RULt if hi t = 0 , (4.22)
where RC corresponds to the theoretical RUL of the last point. Point in which

The inception model, illustrated in Figure 4.10, configures a sliding-window
setup with a single stride, s.t., each input sample Xi∈RT ×M . While T = 30
refers to window length or the number of values, M = 18 refers to the input
feature space resulting from the concatenation of OwT × 4 and XsT × 14.
Figure 4.10 : Inception-based deep CNN (DeVol et al., 2021).
The success of the adopted CNN model arises in using inception modules to
detect features within a shallower neural network. Those modules, illustrated
inside dotted rectangles in Figure 4.10, configure parallel 1D convolutional layers
of different sizes ( 1, 3 and 5) and a single max pool layer of size 3. Features
— 91 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
from those parallel convolutional layers are concatenated and passed to the next
layer. Particularly, the second inception module incorporates convolutions of
size 1 in every convolutional layer and after the max pool layer for dimensional
reduction purposes. The output of the second inception module is flattened and
fully connected through a hidden layer of 256 nodes. A dropout at a rate of
50% was used in this layer to reduce overfitting. Finally, a single node with a
linear activation is configured at the last layer to get an unidimensional output
multiplied by Rc to obtain the RUL in flight hours.
Estimation performance
The inception-based CNN is trained with 85% of samples from the development
splits, while the remaining 15% are used for validating the performance gains
during training. The 100% of samples from the test split is used to evaluate the
RUL estimation performance in terms of RMSE, MAE and ETA1.
Batches of size 128 were configured to speed up the training process, which
was conducted for a maximum of 200 epochs. To avoid overfitting during the
minimization of the RMSE loss function, an early stopping callback, waiting for
unfavourable training during five epochs, was configured. The SGD optimization
algorithm with learning rate ηof 0.01 was used to minimize the loss function.
The model development model was repeated five times using different model
weights wκat zero time κ= 0 to estimate prediction deviations. Results,
presented in Table 4.7, presented a RMSE of 12. 41±1. 21, a MAE of 9. 8±0. 4,
and a ETA1 of 2960. 94±30. 3. Those results are similar to those in ( DeVol et
al., 2021), a study which used a portion of the available DS sets. Predictions,
illustrated in Figure 4.7, show how prognosis models estimate the RUL of
turbofan units performing flight classes (FCs) 1 and 2, corresponding to short
and long flight lengths. Particularly, Figure 4.7 presents the RUL per flight cycle

4.5 Discussion of results
The aviation industry strongly aims to develop predictive maintenance solutions
to reduce AOG and MRO costs. Those solutions arise on estimating the RUL of
— 92 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
Figure 4.11 : RUL estimation on turbofan units from flight classes 1 and 3.
Note: a coloured line refers to the theoretical RUL of a determined unit, while the area
and the dotted line refer to the estimated mean and the variance per cycle, respectively.
aircraft systems or components. Given the availability of condition monitoring
data from a fleet of Boeing 747 aircraft during the conduction of ReMAP project
and public synthetic condition monitoring data from NASA repositories, this
study was able to estimate the RUL of Cooling Units ( CUs) and turbofan
engines.
Turbofans are safety-critical systems because human lives are compromised
when they fail. Cooling units are not safety-critical systems; their inoperability
may impose operational limitations. Although data from CUs was gathered
from real aircraft, health degradation ensigns were demonstrated to be more
challenging to highlight. The RUL estimation of CUs was assessed after
considering some assumptions about the theoretical RUL and constructing HIs
from a physical-based model to use them as inputs of a AI model. RUL
of turbofan units, on the other hand, were easier to handle because of the
availability of synthetic but public NASA datasets with theoretical RUL. Two
repository versions from NASA were used while experimenting with turbofan
units. The main difference between them arises in the synthetic or real operating
conditions used to simulate the degradation of turbofan units through C-MAPSS
simulator.
All solutions presented in this chapter were developed following technical
processes from an adaptive PHM pipeline composed of three stages: 1)
construction of the experimental scenario, 2) data pre-processing, and 3)
Table 4.7: RUL on units from N-CMAPSS using inception-based CNN.

CNN (DeVol et al., 2021) - 12.5 -
— 93 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
prognostic model. Particularly, this PHM pipeline guides the construction of
a hybrid model to estimate the RUL of CUs. To develop this hybrid model,
HIs were constructed from time and frequency domains and used as inputs of a
AI model. It enabled the prediction of RUL within the last 48 operating hours
with a RMSE of 8.62±0.77. However, this prognosis model was developed in
a data-centralized scenario using just 14 health degradation examples of CUs,
being impractical for distributed ML approaches.
Data of NASA repositories enables the experimentation of FL in distributed
ML approaches because of the availability of data from many turbofan units.
In data-centralized scenarios, condition monitoring data simulated through the
use of synthetic operating conditions was used to create two AI models: using a
MLP-KF architecture and using a FMLP architecture. The second architecture
reduces the produced prediction error. While MLP-KF architecture presents a
RMSE of 24.39±2.11, FMLP presents a RMSE of 14.63±0.97.
Finally, condition monitoring data from a new NASA repository called
N-CMAPSS dataset, simulated using real operating conditions, was used
to experiment with the RUL of turbofan units using DL methods. Thus
completely delegating the feature extraction procedures to the capability of DL
architectures. An inception-based architecture from ( DeVol et al. , 2021) was
reused to evaluate the RUL of all available turbofan units of DS splits presented
in N-CMAPSS dataset from ( Arias Chao et al. , 2021). Given the availability of
condition monitoring data from many turbofan units and a RMSE of 12.41±1.21
in a data-centralized scenario, this dataset is considered for decentralized ML
experiments.
4.6 Summary
This chapter described the development of prognosis models for estimating the
Remaining Useful Life ( RUL) of cooling units and turbofan engines to partially
answer research question RQ1. Those models were created in a data-centralized
scenario where the absence of data privacy concerns is assumed to get a baseline
estimation performance for experimenting with decentralized Machine Learning
(ML) approaches via Federated Learning ( FL).
Despite data from real aircraft being used to experience cooling units by
constructing Health Indicator ( HI) through time-frequency feature extraction,
the unavailability of many health degradation examples makes cooling units
not ideal for experiencing with FL. As an alternative, data from digital twins of
turbofan engines is considered for those purposes, mainly due to data availability
from two Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulator repositories.
The first repository from ( Saxena & Goebel , 2008), comprised of condition
monitoring data simulated with synthetic operating conditions, enabled the
development of two prognosis models created with MLP-KF and FMLP
architectures. Both architectures evaluated the maximum failure modes
and operating conditions available, in which FMLP has the best estimation
performance. Results that are comparable with Deep Learning ( DL)
architectures.
The repository from ( Arias Chao et al. , 2021), comprised of condition
— 94 —

CHAPTER 4. REMAINING LIFETIME OF AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS
monitoring data simulated with operating conditions from a real jet, enables
the experimentation of DL architectures because of the availability of data from
many turbofan units and because they present real operating conditions and
different flight lengths: short, medium and long. Particularly, an inception-based
CNN architecture is considered for experiencing with FL because of its success in
presenting comparable RUL estimation performance using data from all available
DS sets.

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
F
ederated Learning (FL) is a privacy-preserving and decentralized Machine
Learning ( ML) approach mostly adopted to solve classification and
regression problems through supervised learning. Collaborative prognosis and
prognostics enable machines to improve their AI-based health estimators by
learning from equivalent machines within a fleet. The main difference between
them arises in the problem of overcoming the prognosis of failure or the prognosis
of failure. It should be noted that this problem does not restrict the use of FL,
whose adoption has been considered due to data privacy requirements.
This chapter introduces Federated Health Assessment ( FHA) as an evaluation
framework that guides the feasibility analysis of collaborative problems via FL,
addressing research question RQ2. In contributing to the state-of-the-art in
FL, this chapter primarily describes knowledge-aggregation strategies in various
data-decentralized approaches to address research question RQ3. As this
question is related to collaborative prognosis, the RUL estimation of turbofan
engines is experimented with in three data-partitioning settings to evaluate
the success of federated solutions using public data. In addition to outlining
various knowledge aggregation strategies essential for achieving research goal
RG3, this chapter details the design and implementation of a federated solution,
“FedLabSync”, which is compatible with diverse data partitioning scenarios and
contributes to addressing research goal RG2.
Parts of this chapter, depending on the data partitioning setting analyzed,
have been published in the following research articles:
• Horizontal data partitioning : J1, J2, C5 and C3.
• Vertical data partitioning : C2 and J2.
• Hybrid data partitioning : J1, J2 and C1.
5.1 Introduction
Collaborative prognosis arises in data-decentralized approaches where multiple
edge nodes, referred from now as federated parties or simply parties, store
condition monitoring data on isolated devices and aim to learn from each
other’s data. This need mainly arises due to data scarcity problems on isolated
parties, which present difficulties in gathering condition monitoring data with
a wide range of operating conditions under the run-to-failure philosophy to
train representative prognosis models. This collaborative technique aims to
allow parties to predict the health state of operating conditions experienced
on equivalent machines by other parties and possibly increase the prediction
performance of models trained using data from isolated operating environments.
Adopting the health condition estimation problem from previous chapters,
in which machines refer to aircraft systems, this chapter introduces Federated
Health Assessment ( FHA) as an evaluating framework dedicated to evaluating
the potential of Federated Learning (FL) in solving collaborative prognostics and
— 98 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
prognosis problems. Within this scope, this chapter describes the characteristics
of collaborative prognosis problems and various knowledge aggregation strategies
to solve them depending on the three data partitioning settings identified by FL:
horizontal, vertical and hybrid.
Using public condition monitoring data and operating conditions from
turbofan engines available in the Turbofan Engine Degradation Simulation
repository from NASA, this chapter describes the performance achieved in
federated experiments on the collaborative RUL estimation of turbofan units.
Specifically, this chapter uses solutions compatible with ML architectures based
on Artificial Neural Networks ( ANNs) given its widely applicability.
The main contributions of this chapter can be summarized as follows:
1. The description of a Federated Health Assessment evaluation framework
adaptable to different data partitioning settings and knowledge
aggregation strategies adopted by federated solutions. This chapter
adopted this framework to evaluate the prediction performance achieved
by federated solutions to estimate the RUL of turbofan units.
2. The description of the RUL estimation of turbofan units using ANNs on
collaborative prognosis scenarios under three data partitioning settings:
horizontal, vertical and hybrid.
3. The design and implementation of a federated solution compatible
with horizontal, vertical and hybrid data partitioning settings named
FedLabSync. The success of this solution arises from sharing knowledge
through the propagation of a collaborative error obtained from similar
samples. The similarity of party samples, based on the equality of input
data, helps synchronize the training process of party AI models.
This chapter is organized as follows. Section 5.2 describes the Federated Health
Assessment evaluation framework, highlighting the problem characteristics,
data partition settings covered and knowledge aggregation strategies to
adopt accordingly. Section 5.3 describes the RUL estimation performance
obtained by experimenting with basis federated solutions designed for
horizontal data partitioning scenarios. Section 5.4 describes the results
obtained by experimenting with a basis federated solution designed for
vertical data partitioning. Finally, Section 5.5 describes a federated solution
named FedLabSync. The knowledge aggregation strategy based on label
synchronization used for this solution is deeply explained in this chapter to
describe how this solution can support horizontal, vertical and hybrid data
partitioning settings.
5.2 Federated health assessment
The collaborative prognosis concept has emerged in the Prognostics and Health
Management ( PHM) discipline. This concept has been guiding the estimation
of machine health conditions through data-driven failure prognosis solutions
(Dhada, Jain & Parlikad , 2020). Those collaborative solutions aim to improve
— 99 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
the ability to predict the RUL of machines when individual machines can not
generate enough run-to-failure examples in a wide range of operating conditions
to build representative prognosis models ( Adrià Salvador et al. , 2019).
The feasibility of a collaborative prognosis has been identified when equivalent
machines share compatible information gathered from condition monitoring
systems experimenting with similar operating environments. However, ( Dhada,
Jain & Parlikad, 2020) alerted the PHM community to adopt privacy-preserving
technologies during cooperation. To guide companies sensing equivalent
machines in implementing collaborative solutions, this chapter introduces FHA
as an evaluation framework dedicated to the feasibility analysis of FL in solving
collaborative problems.
5.2.1 Problem
The collaborative prognosis problem involves J parties privately sharing
knowledge to enhance the prognosis capabilities of their AI models, thereby
assisting technicians in performing maintenance on machines only when
necessary. Collaborative parties in this problem refer to airlines that sense
equivalent aircraft systems to reduce Aircraft On Ground ( AOG), but their
fleets can not produce representative condition monitoring data under the
run-to-failure philosophy due to aircraft airworthiness policies.
Individual airlines aim to gather health degradation ensigns from many
run-to-failure examples, also called run-to-failure trajectories. Because a single
trajectory is delivered by a single aircraft by experimenting with multiple
flight cycles, it is assumed that a single party will store many run-to-failure
trajectories from a single aircraft system or its own air fleet. In addition,
because prognosis modes are trained with time-series data ranging from aircraft
systems operating in operating conditions till their failure, data downloading
from aircraft is reserved during AOG. This may guarantee the availability of the
involved parties in a business-to-business (B2B) topology during K knowledge
aggregation iterations ( Q. Li, Wen, Wu et al. , 2019). In this topology, also
known as cross-silo FL, data downloading when the aircraft is at boarding may
be reserved for inference purposes.
Federated Learning aims to create more assertive AI models by incorporating
knowledge from all airlines, or parties, since they cannot share sensitive data
due to privacy policies. Knowledge aggregation is expected by applying different
strategies, e.g., aggregating model weights as the federated server from Figure 5.1
illustrates. To evaluate the feasibility of federated solutions, baseline prognosis
models were developed in the previous chapter using data from all available
parties stored in a central server.
As parties monitor equivalent aircraft systems, the analysis of equivalent
sensor signals is expected ideally. Yet, it is conceivable that aircraft systems
may diverge in sensor configurations or prioritize the analysis of specific sensor
signals. Figure 5.1 illustrates how five sensors measure turbofan engines.
However, parties use a subgroup of those sensors to create prognosis models.
This FHA framework considers the variability of input parameters P in isolated
prognosis models while guiding the evaluation of federated solutions.
— 100 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
Figure 5.1 : Federated health assessment problem and federated components.
Note: this problem considers the variability of input parameters across models’ parties.
5.2.2 Data partitioning settings
Depending on the natural distribution of a global dataset D, composed of
M features (input parameters P of prognosis models) and N samples (sensor
measurements), along J parties, three data partitioning settings have been
identified in Federated Learning: horizontal, vertical and hybrid. The proportion
of samples or features within the j-th party and the global dataset D has been
described in Table 3.2 for every data partitioning and collaborative problem.
Considering that previous description, this section describes data partitioning
settings adopting the RUL estimation problem of aircraft systems.
• Horizontal data partitioning : emerges when airlines monitor
equivalent aircraft systems through a common feature space M , while
sensor measurements of D come by the union of parties’ sensor
measurementsNj.
• Vertical data partitioning : assumes that airlines use the same indices
for N sensor measurements. Because the feature space of D is formed by
combining each party’s individual feature space Mj with the same index,
this data partitioning does not reflect a realistic federated scenario in the
aviation industry.
• Hybrid data partitioning : represents the most complex data
partitioning, wherein the feature and sensor spaces of D are derived
from the union of features Mj and sensor Nj spaces of airlines’ datasets.
Because this data partitioning does not restrict to airlines sharing the
same sensor measurement indices, this data partitioning emerges when
airlines monitor equivalent systems through minimal variations in the
feature space as Figure 5.1 illustrates.
— 101 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
5.2.3 Evaluation framework
The Federated Health Assessment ( FHA) evaluation framework was used in all
federated experiments conducted during the development of this thesis. This
evaluation framework aims to guide data scientists in constructing collaborative
problems via Federated Learning, inside and outside PHM discipline, i.e.,
collaborative prognostics ( Adrià Salvador et al. , 2019), collaborative prognosis
(Dhada, Jain, Herrera et al. , 2020) and anomaly detection ( Llasag Rosero, Silva
& Ribeiro , 2023b).
Besides answering RQ3, the research question referring to how to aggregate
knowledge from prognosis models from different airline companies, FHA
evaluation framework serves to evaluate the feasibility of federated solutions
by formulating the following questions:
• How well are prognosis models from isolated airlines predicting the RUL
of machines that experience similar operating conditions?
• Do federated models achieve similar prediction performances from a
data-centralized approach?
• Do federated models collaboratively improve the RUL estimation of
machines on the federated approach?
Those questions, which emerged during the development of federated
solutions, are answered in four main stages illustrated in Figure 5.1. This
figure uses a particular colour to paint parties’ models (small circles) and
run-to-failure databases. While a big blue circle represents a global model from
a data-centralized, the federated model for the j-th party is represented by a
big circle, particularly coloured. Please, note that a data-centralized model can
be seen as the union of isolated datasets, while a federated model can be seen
as the union of isolated AI models.
• Isolated models refers to parties’ models constructed using a common
ML method and hyperparameters but using just local data. At this stage,
the j-th isolated model is evaluated using data from the j-th party.
• Transferability tests are performed by evaluating the prediction
performance of the j-th isolated model using data from the other parties.
At this stage, model transferability can be possible when the j-th isolated
model presents similar or better estimation performance when predicting
the health condition on data measured under operating conditions from
other parties.
• Data-centralized models are constructed using all development data
from the federated network D to have a baseline estimation performance
during proof-of-concept experimentations. A resulting predictive model is
evaluated using 1) data from the j-th party, and 2) data from all parties.
• Federated models are compared with a model from a data-centralized
model during the proof-of-concept experimentations. This is done by
— 102 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
using 1) data from j-th party, and 2) data from all parties. When data
centralization is unfeasible in real applications, performance gains are
evaluated by directly comparing federated and isolated models.
All tests, resulting from the possible combinations among models and datasets,
are summarized in the table of Figure 5.2. Note that isolated, data-centralized,
and federated models owned by a particular party can be evaluated using
data from all or individual parties. While horizontal comparisons evaluate
the performance gains along data distribution approaches, vertical comparisons
evaluate the prediction variability among parties.
Figure 5.2 : Evaluations using federated health assessment.
Note: this framework guides the transition from data-isolated to federated approaches.
5.2.4 Knowledge aggregation strategies
Depending on the data partitioning setting and subsequent Federated Learning
category ( HFL, VFL, and Hybrid-FL), various knowledge aggregation strategies
have been adopted. This research has focused on describing strategies to
aggregate knowledge from one of the most popular ML methods in PHM and
FL, the Artificial Neural Networks ( ANNs).
Knowledge aggregation strategies primarily vary based on the type of
non-sensitive information exchanged between parties and the data partitioning
employed. Federated Learning have initially aggregated gradients from ANNs
from multiple parties. This knowledge aggregation strategy was adopted by the
Federated Stochastic Gradient Descent (FedSGD) solution (Shokri & Shmatikov,
2015), the solution that served as a reference for subsequent federated solutions
SGD-based. Those solutions comprise four standard steps illustrated in Figure
3.3 within a single knowledge aggregation iteration κ: 1) broadcasting of AI
global model, 2) local training, 3) uploading the model, and 4) computing the
AI global model.
FedSGD was tailored for horizontal data partitioning. Its effectiveness hinges
on averaging gradients computed for each data batch of size B, which forms the
— 103 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
cornerstone of its success. The Federated A veraging (FedA vg) solution arose as a
response to the high communication costs associated with FedSGD ( McMahan,
Moore & Ramage , 2016). Its effectiveness lies in averaging model weights wj
fromJ parties. However, its adoption presented limitations for addressing model
drift among parties due to Non-independent and Non-identically Distributed
Data (non-i.i.d.) settings ( Martínez Beltrán et al. , 2023). As a result, numerous
SGD-based variations have emerged within horizontal data partitioning to
ensure optimization problem convergence, e.g., approaches such as sharing
momentum term ( W. Liu et al. , 2019), the proximal term ( Sahu et al. , 2018),
among others previously presented in Algorithm 1.
As an alternative to the weight-averaging strategy in horizontal data
partitioning, a matching strategy at the neuron level from FedMA ( X. Wang &
Wang, 2020) was designed to deal with model heterogeneity given by non-i.i.d.
settings. Model heterogeneity due to non-i.i.d. settings was assessed using
Transfer Learning (TL) processes, e.g., knowledge distillation ( Martínez Beltrán
et al. , 2023). Many knowledge distillation solutions share weights w, logits
z (output vectors before softmax), prediction outputs ˆY , intermediate feature
maps and labels Y to facilitate the adaptation of a AI global model to a party’s
feature set, e.g FedMD ( D. Li & Wang , 2019), FedDistill ( Jiang et al. , 2020),
MATH (L. Hu et al. , 2021), FedGKT (He, Annavaram & A vestimehr, 2020) and
others.
Transfer Learning (TL) has mainly been adopted to deal with model
heterogeneity, resulting from the architecture variability of ANNs from multiple
parties. Because architecture variability is strictly related to feature space
variability on parties, TF has mainly been adopted in Hybrid-FL studies
(Martínez Beltrán et al. , 2023). Unfortunately, TL has mainly assessed
classification problems, presenting few and limited results in regression problems,
such as using linear regression ( S. Li et al. , 2020).
Alternative methodologies to TL have surfaced in Hybrid-FL studies,
including the aggregation of intermediate feature maps to construct feature
ensembles (X. Zhang et al. , 2020b), the separation of the optimization problem
into sample and feature spaces through the primal-dual transform ( Su & Lau ,
2021), and the advent of neural matching techniques. However, those were
predominantly targeted at classification tasks, given the limited adaptations
to regression problems, e.g., using primal-dual optimization to fit a linear
regression model. This chapter introduces a novel Hybrid-FL solution named
FedLabSync, whose knowledge-exchanging strategy named label synchronization
is inspired by index synchronization used in VFL (Dai et al. , 2021). Before
introducing Hybrid-FL in the RUL estimation of turbofan engines, this chapter
firstly describes the prediction performance achieved by basic HFL solutions
SGD-based and a VFL solution named Vertical Synchronous Federated Learning
(Dai et al. , 2021).
5.3 Horizontal data partitioning
This section describes experiments on federated solutions using a
weight-averaging strategy to aggregate knowledge from multiple parties
— 104 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
configuring horizontal data partitioning. Using synthetic condition monitoring
data from turbofan engines monitored under limited operating conditions, two
ANN architectures previously introduced in Chapter 4 were primarily explored:
MLP-KF and FMLP.
5.3.1 Weight-averaging
The averaging of weights is an elegant strategy used by many model-agnostic
federated solutions to fit a determined AI model by adapting the well-known
SGD optimization algorithm. As Algorithm 1 describes, knowledge from
multiple parties can be aggregated by simply averaging gradient weights by
accomplishing two conditions:
1. The central server broadcasts a AI global model to all parties before every
κknowledge aggregation iteration.
2. Parties’ models share the same architecture, e.g., the same hidden layers
and number of neurons at each layer of an ANN. In other words, all
parameters of the AI global model are updated at κtime without suffering
architecture modifications.
Many federated solutions have adapted the optimisation of the SGD
algorithm to accelerate or ensure learning convergence by correcting the simple
weight-averaging strategy of FedA vg. The main advantage of those adaptations
is that they just require exchanging or storing historical optimization data, e.g.,
momentum and past weight parameters.
Given the extensive number of federated solutions SGD-based, whose
knowledge aggregation strategy is mainly based on averaging models’ weights,
this research started by analyzing the potential of FedA vgand FedProx from
Algorithm 1. This research adopted FedA vgbecause it is the pioneer solution
based on weight-averaging that inspired many adaptive optimization variations
described in Algorithm 1. The FedProx solution was adopted not only because
it is one of the adaptive optimization variations of FedA vgbut also because it
aims to provide convergence guarantees by adding a proximal term µto correct
AI global model drift when learning over non-i.i.d. settings ( Sahu et al. , 2018).
5.3.2 Number of federated parties
Federated experiments started by identifying the number of beneficiary parties
J ∈N, s.t. J> 1. A business-to-business (B2B) architecture was identified
because a limited number of airlines or parties is expected for a collaborative
prognosis problem. In practice, the number of parties was selected according
to the characteristics of the FD00x datasets described in Section 2.4.1.2. As
C-MAPSS data contains a variable referring to the turbofan unit monitored, a
clustering per unit was carried out with the use of the Jn selection criterion,
which means unique unit identifiers are distributed using the module operator
after those identifiers being shuffled. The number of units of the j-th party is
identified as follows:

to guarantee problem convergence. For example, configuring 124 parties to have

5.3.2.1 Collaborative prognosis convergence under non-i.i.d. settings
Run-to-failure data of turbofan units vary not only on operating cycles but
also in the length of the imperceptible health degradation stage constructed
using Equation 4.8. Because this non-i.i.d. setting is extended to federated
experiments in which parties get data from a determined number of turbofan
units randomly chosen, the FedProx solution was used to analyze the problem
convergence when fitting the MLP model from MLP-KF architecture.
Federated solutions based on adaptive optimization, described in Algorithm
1, analyze convergence over time by considering a single training epoch before
the κ-th knowledge aggregation. However, experiments on C-MAPSS data
demonstrated the need to train an isolated MLP during E epochs before every
knowledge aggregation, a process that is performed by running E times the for
loop comprised between Lines 4-11 of Algorithm 1.
Initial experiments in FL considered a fixed number of E epochs. The red
and green curves in Figure 5.4 correspond to using FedA vg withE in 20 and 60,
respectively. The blue curve uses a different number of epochs by configuring an
early stopping callback with patience of 10 and 180 maximum epochs. Notably,
E variability through an early stopping algorithm had less MAE. Therefore,
subsequent experiments on C-MAPSS data adopt early stopping.
The principles of FedProx are based on the model convergence over time
without needing the variability of training epochs E during local training ( Sahu
et al., 2018). Experiments on FedProx considered 20 local training epochs using
different values between zero and one for the proximal term µ. The learning
evolution curves from these experiments, illustrated in Figure 5.5, are compared
with experiments on FedA vg using an early stopping algorithm and fixing E at
20. More experiments on FedProx were conducted using 0< µ≤0. 01 because

estimation error on experiments using µ >0. 01 got similar results to FedA vg
when the number of local training epochs was fixed at 20. A FedA vg experiment
that was outperformed when using early stopping.
Over these experiments, it is noticeable that using µ∼0. 01, collaborative
learning process curves take similar values compared to the model trained on
the centralized scenario and FedA vg with early stopping. It can be observed after
comparing the cyan curve with the blue curve. Furthermore, it is evident that
FedProx not only achieved prediction errors of the FedA vg with early stopping
but also presented fewer oscillations after 53 K iterations. Thus demonstrating
that collaborative prognosis achieves a MAE of 24. 8±2. 61 when adopting a
MLP-KF architecture.

5.3.3 Improving performance predictions
The MLP-KF architecture was useful to start experimenting with Horizontal
Federated Learning solutions. However, the performance prediction of MLP-KF
has been outperformed using a variety of ML methods, mainly DL methods.

Implementing a Functional Multilayer Perceptron ( FMLP) architecture was
considered to outperform collaborative predictions because it demonstrated to
be comparable with DL methods in a data-centralized scenario presented in
Section 4.4.1.3.
The horizontal data partitioning approach presented in Section 5.3.2 was
reused to conduct functional time series forecasting experiments. In contrast
to experiments on MLP-KF architecture, experiments on FMLP were repeated
many times to calculate the prediction variability within and between parties. As
FedA vg demonstrated similar results to FedProx when considering the variability
of local training epochs E by adopting early stopping, experiments on FedProx
were not performed anymore. Thus leveraging the evaluation of many µvalues.
The number of participant parties J in the federated network was reevaluated
using FMLP. Table 5.3 describes the prediction error in terms of MAE and
ETA1 when J was set at 2, 4 and 8. After repeating the federated experiment
30 times, in which each global FMLP model was initialized with different weights
w0, it is noticeable that prediction error decreases when J increases. Because
the cost of evaluating federated experiments is proportional to the number
of participant parties J, especially when repeating federated experiments to
evaluate prediction variability, the federated network comprised just four parties
in subsequent experiments.
Although prediction performances from Table 5.3 suggest that FedA vg does
not achieve the performance obtained in a data-centralized scenario using FMLP,
it is noticeable that collaborative prediction performance is better than the

encourages exploring federated solutions with non-horizontal data partition with
the possibility of gathering similar results.
5.4 Vertical data partitioning
This section introduces federated experiments when parties vary in feature space.
Adopting the Vertical Synchronous Federated Learning solution ( T. Chen et al. ,
2020), the prediction error of MLP-KF and FMLP architectures is re-evaluated.
This section remembers the principles of the knowledge aggregation strategy
based on index synchronization used by VFL solutions presented in Section
3.4.2. Particularly, this section introduces vertical data partitioning resulting
from monitoring sensor variability when parties assess the RUL estimation of
turbofan engines by using determined sensors Xs.
5.4.1 Index synchronization and collaborative forecast
Knowledge aggregation under vertical data partitioning is possible thanks to
the collaborative forecast and error propagation of index-synchronized samples
performed by Equation 3 (T. Chen et al. , 2020; Llasag Rosero et al. , 2023b).
Index synchronization strategy arises in two principles:
1. Parties share the same sample indices (identifiers).
2. The communication-computation manager (e.g., a central server) updates
auxiliary matrices containing the labels Y and predictions of participant
parties.
As parties’ models differ in input space, their model architecture also does.
Therefore, a AI global model can be constructed by joining parties’ parameters
as the central server from Figure 3.7 illustrates. However, its construction may
be unnecessary in real applications because the central server does not store
isolated datasets Dj. Hence, its construction is reserved for proof-of-concept
evaluations. The comparison of models trained with isolated datasets Dj and
federated models (subspaces of the AI global model) is preferred to evaluate the
potential of label synchronization and collaborative error propagation strategy
in federated solutions. A strategy depending on updating an auxiliary matrix
A given a batch of parties’ sample indices.
— 110 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
5.4.2 Towards vertical synchronous federated learning
The need to update a prediction matrix AN ×J of N rows and J columns in a
computational server is noticed in Algorithm 3. During this updating process,
illustrated in Figure 3.7, indices (i,j ) of matrix A are used to renovate parties’
predictions of determined samples. Lines 0-10 of Algorithm 3 describe how
parties communicate with the central server through pull and push requests to
guarantee that parties exchange local predictions instead of sensitive data. The
differences in federated key steps from HFL and VFL solutions SGD-based are
highlighted in the following key steps previously described in Section 3.3.2:
1. AI global model broadcasting is reserved for HFL solutions using
weight-averaging. In non-horizontal FL, parts of the AI global model
are transmitted to beneficiary parties. As the architecture of AI models of
parties may differ because they may use different input features, there
is no restriction to broadcast a common AI global model. In fact,
the architecture of the global model can only be defined after the first
knowledge aggregation iteration κ= 1 once parties have already shared
their models and the central server has joined their parameters. If parties
decide to store a federated model at the central server, AI global model
broadcasting, performed by executing Line 13 of Algorithm 3, refers to
transmitting back the corresponding parameters of parties’ models that
were joined to create the AI global model. Although downloading parties’
models is optional, downloading current parties’ predictions is required
for local training. Once parties perform local predictions and push them
by executing Lines 14-15, determined parties’ predictions are downloaded
when the j-th party executes Line 16.
2. Local training refers to the j-th party privately fitting its isolated
model using their local samples and collaborative predictions. Block-wise
coordination is used to reduce communication overhead when exchanging
predictions of N samples, as predictions from other parties are involved
in fitting isolated models. This block-wise coordination updates model
weights after computing the local cost function Fj
κ(wj) for a given block
or mini-batch of size B. This process is executed in the second inner for
loop of Algorithm 3 after pulling batch predictions from the central server.
Those predictions allow calculating φL′
, the local loss function derived
w.r.t. the activation function φL of the last layer L. This derived is then
multiplied by the remaining chain rule derivatives to back-propagate the
error. The resulting gradient of this operation is used to calculate the
weights wj
κ+1 for the next κknowledge aggregation until achieving the
problem convergence.
3. Isolated model uploading is performed in Line 23 after parties updated
local weights wj
κat κtime. In contrast to HFL, VFL does not obligate
the uploading of isolated models because knowledge sharing is based on
collaborative error propagation instead of weight-averaging. However,
uploading local predictions ˆYj is required to update matrix AN ×J and

step is optional in VFL, this step was reserved for updating the matrix
AN ×J by using local predictions ˆYj. This update is performed at the
central served when the j-th party executes Line 15.

5.4.3 Prediction variability among parties and experiments
Similar to Horizontal Federated Learning experiments, the variation of
prediction performance of FMLP and MLP-KF was also analyzed in Vertical
Federated Learning. Because the adopted Vertical Synchronous Federated
Learning solution enables parties to differ in model architecture, prediction
— 112 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
variability among parties is inevitable mainly due to model and feature
space heterogeneity. Although this section compares the RUL estimation
performance of ML methods using distance-based metrics, it compares them
using variability-based metrics from Section 2.3.4.3. Therefore, guiding PHM
practitioners to evaluate prediction variability among parties and experiments.
The federated experiments with vertical data partitioning consider that
airlines monitor turbofan engines with ≈80% of the sensor signals Xs that
better describe the health degradation of turbofans. During the data-centralized
experiments with the MLP-KF architecture, presented in Section 4.4.1.1, 16
of the 24 available sensor signals Xs better described the health degradation.
Those sensors are 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 15, 17, 20 and 21 ( Saxena &
K, 2008; Scott et al. , 2022).
Experiments in VFL consider that parties use 13 from those 16 sensor signals
(features M ) for diverse reasons, e.g., sensor unavailability or incorrect sensor
readings. Therefore, parties use a combination of 13 C16 during data partitioning
to define Mj. Similarly to HFL experiments, VFL experiments also considered
the participation of four parties J and the distribution of the FD004 dataset.
In contrast to horizontal, vertical data partitioning considers parties sharing the
same sample indices of development and test splits from the FD004 dataset.
A data partitioning that does not correspond to airlines’ reality but helps to
introduce non-horizontal partitioning settings.
After constructing isolated datasets Dj, the development of predictive
models takes different data pre-processing steps and training hyperparameters
depending on the MLP-KF or FMLP architectures previously described in
Sections 4.4.1.2 and 4.4.1.3. However, the AI global model of both ML
architectures is developed with Algorithm 3 during a maximum of 750 knowledge
aggregation iterations K with a single local training epoch E. Similarly,
collaborative learning was conducted to avoid overfitting by configuring an
early stopping criteria waiting for performance losses during eight knowledge
aggregation iterations.
For both ML architectures, many isolated, AI global models and federated
models for each party j were developed with different initial weights w0 to
calculate prediction variability among parties and federated experiments. While
distance-based metrics (e.g., MAE and RMSE) evaluate the distance between
the theoretical RUL and predicted ˆRUL in flight hours, variability-based
metrics (i.e., OA V, OAB and reproducibility) evaluate the distance between
the normalized target Y∈[0, 1] and model output ˆY .
Using ten times the 248 run-to-failure trajectories from the FD004 testing set,
we collected local predictions ˆYj and ˆRULj estimations of 10 AI global models
and 40 federated models trained with different feature spaces for each federated
experiment and ML architecture. Those predictions evaluated the AI global
model’s estimation bias and the federated models’ collaborative forecast using
ˆRUL and ˆY , respectively. Prediction performance of both ML architectures,
presented in Table 5.5, reaffirm the capabilities of FMLP architecture in
federated applications because it got fewer values for distance-based evaluation
metrics using AI global models. Furthermore, values for variability-based
evaluation metrics were close to zero using federated models, demonstrating
— 113 —

the reduction of prediction variability among parties and federated experiments.
However, it is notable that the Vertical Synchronous Federated Learning solution
can not achieve the prediction performance of a data-centralized approach.
While AI global models have a MAE of 13.80±1.87, data-centralized models
have a MAE of 9.51±1.02.

Experiments in VFL conclude that prediction variability benefits more FMLP
architecture instead of MLP-KF because this last one presents a constant shift
of almost four flight hours, respecting the isolated models. On the other hand,
FMLP architecture presents a constant shift of fewer than three hours flight
hours, while bigger deviations suggest that there are federated experiments in
which federated models improved the prediction performance of isolated and
data-centralized approaches.

5.5 Hybrid data partitioning
In many engineering and industrial applications, parties differ in sample and
feature space ( Y. Hu et al. , 2019; Su & Lau , 2021). In consequence, federated
solutions compatible with hybrid data partitioning are extremely required.
However, Hybrid-FL studies are scarce, especially in collaborative forecasting
problems ( Llasag Rosero et al. , 2023b). In the aviation industry, hybrid
data partitioning emerges when multiple airlines monitor equivalent aircraft
systems while presenting sensor variability due to sensor unavailability, parties
prioritizing using different sensors and other reasons.
This section presents our Hybrid-FL solution named FedLabSync, which is
inspired by sample index synchronization from Algorithm 3 (T. Chen et al., 2020;
Dai et al., 2021). Sample index synchronization of VFL can not be adopted when
parties differ in sample space. Therefore, the FedLabSync solution argues that
synchronizing samples at the label level (target output) allows parties to share
their knowledge. The condition for adopting this knowledge-exchange strategy,
named label synchronization, arises on parties overlapping in label space Y .
Formally, label synchronization strategy requires accomplishing the following:
Condition: Given the labelsYj of J parties, there is at least one instance
of each unique labellb in thej-th party. In other words, the concatenation of
parties’ labels{vw :v∈YJ,w ∈Yj}j=J− 1
j=1 has the same number of unique labels
Lbs as thej-th isolated datasetDj has (Llasag Rosero et al., 2023b).
In case of accomplishing this condition, the problem of FedLabSync is
configured similarly to the problem of VFL because it also aims to find the
optimal weights for a global cost function F (w) expressed in Equation 3.7.
This procedure avoids evaluating a loss function θ(. ) at each training sample by
configuring batches of B samples with the objective of reducing computational
operations. Because parties do not share the same sample space, a local
training procedure based on block-wise coordination minimizes the j-th cost
function Fj
κ(wj) by assessing mini-batches of label-synchronized samples at
κtime. Figure 5.6 illustrates how the arrangement of samples’ indices in
three-party dataset batches shows a similar and distinct when configuring in
VFL and Hybrid-FL, respectively.
Similar to VFL solutions, the parallel synchronous SGD program of
FedLabSync also requires communicating their local predictions to update the
server’s prediction matrix A. This is because knowledge exchange is based on
improving collaborative predictions of samples with the same label lb instead of
the same sample index. This label synchronization strategy uses more auxiliary
matrices than the index-synchronization strategy of VFL. Figure 5.6 illustrates
in client-server knowledge exchange strategies b) and c) how parties in VFL
and Hybrid-FL require sharing predictions to central server creates matrix A.
However, due to parties’ samples in a) not sharing the same index, FedLabSync
also require creating a matrix of labels Mlbs to relate similar parties’ samples. As
Figure 5.6 illustrates, both knowledge aggregation strategies execute the same
key steps at κtime.

Figure 5.6 : FL key loop steps, ANN components and data exchanged.
Note: synchronization strategies in VFL and Hybrid-FL exchange particular data.
5.5.1 Label synchronization
The Label Synchronization (LabSync) strategy constructs a synchronization
label matrix Mj
syc for the j-th party by executing Algorithm 4. To do that,
every party should first share its labels Yj and the number of samples Nj with
the central server, which constructs a global label matrix Mlbs that is then used
to evaluate the similarity of parties’ samples.
Thej-th party uses a matrix of synchronizated sample indices Mj
syc to update
and request predictions of the remaining parties stored in matrix A. Matrices
Mj
syc and A are composed of Nj
max rows and J columns because they consider
the maximum number of samples of J parties. Similar to VFL, predictions of A
are used to calculate the global loss function θ(. ) and consequently conduct
the synchronous SGD optimization. An optimization that does not require
parties to train models with whole data samples thanks to configuring block-wise
coordination between similar samples in a mini-batch of size B.
The core steps of LabSync, specified in Algorithm 4, perform the following
tasks. Lines 1-4 get data from parties to define the shape of A and Msync
matrices. Lines 5-6 group labels and initialize a counter in zero for each unique
label lb in a matrix of counters Mlc. The number of instances of each unique
label for every party is calculated in the nested loop of Line 7. The nested
for loop of Line 12 matches the i-th sample of the interested party j with the
sample with the same label lb of the other party. In response to non-i.i.d.
data distributing settings, the conditional clause for the Line 21 ensures that
two parties could have a different number of samples with the same label lb.
It is possible by restarting the label counter Mlc(lb,j ) of the j-th party when
the number of instances of a determined label Idxlb is surpassed. Using the
LabSync algorithm to correlate similar samples via labels, FedLabSync unlocks
collaborative learning capabilities.

6: Mlc←zeros(size(Lbs),j)

8: for lb in Lbs do

5.5.2 Federated label synchronization: FedLabSync
The Hybrid-FL solution FedLabSync is model-agnostic because it can be used
with ANNs and ML methods compatible with the SGD optimization algorithm.
Like Vertical Synchronous Federated Learning from Algorithm 3, FedLabSync
also exchanges knowledge through the intervention of a central server, which
communicates with parties via push and pull requests. This can be noticed
in the first lines of Algorithm 6, namely in Lines 1-21. Figure 5.6c illustrates
how parties shared their labels with the server, which created and returned a
synchronization matrix Mj
syc to the j-th party. The indices of Mj
syc matrix are
then used to access the information of A. For example, the first party accesses
the predictions of the A matrix related to the first instance of Y = 0 by using
the pointing indexes of the first row of M 1
syc. In this sense, the first party can
privately access the predictions related to the first Y = 0 occurrence of the
second and third party in the following positions A(3, 2) and A(2, 3).
The label synchronization process has to be accomplished before training any
AI model to achieve the convergence of the global cost function F (w). Once
parties communicate their labels and number of samples though pull requests
of Lines 20-21, they receive matrices Msyc. This information is enough to
execute the key steps of Federated Learning: AI global model broadcasting,
local training, isolated model uploading and AI global model computation.
1. AI global model broadcasting works as in Vertical Federated Learning.
There is no restriction of defining a AI global model architecture at κ= 0
time because parties differ in model architecture. If parties decide to
construct a global model by joining the parameters of the parties’ models,
the central server will transfer back a part of this global model to each
party via a pull request of Line 24.
2. Local training is the most crucial step of the FedLabSync solution
because it comprises forward and backward propagation of ANNs in a
parallel synchronous SGD optimization. Considering that a single party j
downloads weights wj, it performs predictions via forward propagation
and uploads them through push requests to the serve. Parties can
get predictions for a single batch ˆYB×J through pull requests and using
matrix Mj
syc. While a collaborative prediction for the -th sample of party
j enables the computation of the global loss function θ(. ), a batch of
predictions ˆYB×J allows this party to compute the cost function F (w).
Therefore, communication costs can be minimized by applying block-wise
coordination.
In Vertical Federated Deep Learning ( Dai et al. , 2021), the Mean Squared
Error ( MSE) loss function was adopted to evaluate the collaborative
prediction of the -th sample:

5.5.3 Construction of the federated scenario
This section describes the experiments on the collaborative prognosis problem
identified in the aviation industry, in which aircraft systems of airlines’ air
fleets may vary in sensor space while airlines are interested in collaborative
and privacy-preserving approaches. Using the FD004 dataset, the prognosis
problem is constructed using horizontal and vertical data partitioning conditions
described in Sections 5.3 and 5.4.
The conditions for distributing samples and features among J parties are
described in Table 5.7. Considering the Jn selection criterion from horizontal
data partitioning, parties take data from a similar number of randomly selected
turbofan units. This data distribution is performed after shuffling the turbofan
unit identifiers of the development split. Considering feature map combinations
from vertical data partitioning, the j-th party uses 80% of the available sensor
signals Xs at each federated experiment. Considering the 16 sensors that
better described the health degradation when using MLP-KF architecture, the
last column of Table 5.7 describes the number of possible combinations to
test within a federated network. Although expensive, an elevated number of
experiments could better calculate the prediction variability given by the vast
possible combinations. The results presented in this section considered repeating
the federated experiment ten times.
Table 5.7: Distribution of samples and features of FD004 when J varies.
Turbofan units (dev split ) Features

5.6 Discussion of results
Like experiments in Horizontal Federated Learning ( HFL) and Vertical
Federated Learning ( VFL), experiments in Hybrid Federated Learning
(Hybrid-FL) also compare the prediction performance of MLP-KF and FMLP
architectures. Current experiments in Hybrid-FL use the same structure of
MLP-KF and FMLP models from VFL experiments because these models are
constructed using the same number of inputs Mj. While MLP-KF sets Mj at
13, FMLP sets it at 18.
Hyperparameters are reused from previous federated experiments. However,
experiments in Hybrid-FL include a collaborative term ψ. This value was set at
0.01, giving an importance of 1% to the collaborative prediction when calculating
the global loss function θ(. ).
The maximum number of knowledge aggregations K has been set at 750
to compare the learning process of FedA vg, Vertical Synchronous Federated
— 121 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
Learning, and FedLabSync. Using MLP-KF architecture, Figure 5.7 illustrates
the prediction error of a AI global model in terms of RMSE at each κknowledge
aggregation when considering the participation of four parties.
Undoubtedly, the prediction performance of the data-centralized scenario is
better than that of federated approaches. The weight-averaging strategy of
FedA vg allows the construction of a AI global model with fewer knowledge
aggregations. However, although vertical and hybrid data partitioning implies
loss information of determined sensor signals Xs, the prediction performance
achieved by Vertical Synchronous Federated Learning and FedLabSync is not
too far from FedA vg. In fact, index synchronization strategy from VFL has the
potential of achieving performances of data-centralized scenarios when parties
collaboratively use the entire feature map M (T. Chen et al. , 2020).
FedLabSync presents inevitable performance losses because parties use fewer
samples Nj and features Mj. However, it learns faster than FedA vg while only
sacrificing the precision a little. For instance, by considering the participation
of four parties with MLP-KF models, in which FedLabSync predicted the RUL
with an MAE of 26. 72±0. 64 and FedA vg did it with 24. 8±2. 61, the difference
in estimating the remaining time is around two flight hours. Those prediction
errors are reduced by using FMLP. Table 5.8 summarizes the results of all
federated experiments of all isolated parties to compare the performance gains
of FMLP over MLP-KF in different data partitioning settings.

trained with local data Dj to evaluate its performance gain when entering into
the collaborative approach.

is presented in the third column of Table 5.8. By considering the MLP-KF, the

MAE on the first party 20.77±0.42 suggests that a party may achieve a similar
prediction performance of a data-centralized approach. This conclusion comes
by noticing that the MAE of the first party is within the MAE range of the
data-centralized approach, namely 20.59±2.23.
A similar conclusion was obtained when using FMLP. By considering the
MAE of 9.33±0.70 on the first party, it is easy to notice that it is inside the
range of 9.51±1.02, experienced in the data-centralized approach. It suggests
that some run-to-failure trajectories better describe the degradation of turbofan
units, and the first party randomly got them during the construction of the
collaborative prognosis problem. However, evaluating which run-to-failure
trajectories better describe the health degradation of turbofans was not a goal
of this research.
Model transferability among parties
Predictive models in non-horizontal data partitioning lack transferability due to
variations in parties’ configurations of input spaces. In other words, the only
data partition that enables parties to model transferability is the horizontal one.
This is noticed in the last column of Table 5.8, in which all parties get the same
prediction performance in HFL because those parties receive the same AI model
from the central server and evaluate this model using all trajectories of the test
split of FD004 dataset.
To evaluate model transferability among parties in horizontal data
partitioning, the test split of FD004 dataset was horizontally partitioned using
Jn selection criterion. In other words, the j-th party uses its own test data,
which corresponds to approximately 25% of the turbofan units of the FD004
test split. Naturally, the isolated model of the j-th party is transferable if other
parties got similar performance results after testing this isolated model with
their own run-to-failure trajectories.
Transferability evaluations were mainly conducted using FMLP because it
outperforms MLP-KF. In this way, the comparison template of FHA framework
— 123 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
in Figure 5.1 was adopted. This comparison template, replicated in Table 5.9,
was filled with the MAE of data-isolated, data-centralized and HFL approaches.
The first row of Table 5.9 describes the results of the mentioned approaches
when using all run-to-failure trajectories from the FD004 test split. Naturally,
federated models of all parties got a MAE of 12.5±0.6. A result that is surpassed
by the data-centralized scenario while parties’ models trained with isolated data
vary from 10.2±0.6 to 15.7±3.1.
Models constructed under a data-centralized approach were evaluated using
test data from each party. Those results are presented in the first column of
Table 5.9. Notably, the prediction performance achieved on isolated parties’ data
suggests that data-centralized models may predict similarly in all test turbofan
units. However, model transferability tests are interested in discovering if a
model trained with data of approximately 25% of the turbofan units can get
similar results.
Parties’ models constructed in data-isolated approaches, evaluated with
partial test data Dj, suggest that models trained with data from operating
conditions of a single party can not get similar RUL estimation performance on
all turbofan units. Therefore, it is interesting to know if federated models can
get similar results using isolated test data Dj. Prediction performances in bold,
presented in HFL approaches at each party, suggest that federated models can
achieve similar results in isolated run-to-failure trajectories when using FMLP
architecture. The impressive outcomes achieved in HFL prompt inquiry into
whether non-horizontal federated solutions yield comparable results.

Federated experiments in non-horizontal data partitioning aimed to evaluate if

to notice that federated models minimally lose prediction capabilities. Although

Those observations call FedLabSync’s learning capabilities attention because
parties construct their federated models without approximately 20% of the
available sensor signals. Furthermore, there are cases (values in bold) in which
federated models from FedLabSync reduce prediction performance variability
of models of data-isolated approaches. This phenomenon is logical when
considering that knowledge exchange relies on propagating a collaborative
prediction error, as exemplified by Equation 5.3. In essence, the collaborative
problem configuration minimizes prediction variability among parties.
5.7 Summary
This chapter assessed the feasibility of Federated Learning ( FL) in collaborative
prognosis problems, focusing on answering research question RQ2. To this end,
a comprehensive evaluation framework, Federated Health Assessment ( FHA),
was introduced to evaluate the effectiveness of federated solutions across various
data partitioning scenarios and collaborative challenges. By configuring the
collaboration of multiple airlines to improve Remaining Useful Life ( RUL)
estimation for turbofan engines using C-MAPSS data, this chapter addresses
research goal RG3 and partially answers research question RQ3, detailing
knowledge aggregation strategies for scenarios where datasets from different
parties vary in both sample and feature spaces.
This chapter described three knowledge aggregation strategies used to
comprise prediction capabilities of multiples Artificial Neural Networks ( ANNs):
weight-averaging, index synchronization and label synchronization (target
output). Three federated solutions have been implemented to evaluate the
capabilities of those strategies. While weigh-averaging was evaluated using
well-known Horizontal Federated Learning ( HFL) solutions such as FedA vg
and FedProx, index synchronization was evaluated using a less-known solution
named Vertical Synchronous Federated Learning. The latter approach inspired
the label synchronization strategy, facilitating knowledge exchange among
parties in configuring a hybrid data partitioning method, which is especially
useful when parties’ datasets vary in sample and feature space.
The Hybrid-FL framework, called FedLabSync, was primarily developed to
address research goal RG2. It utilizes synchronization matrices generated
through the label synchronization algorithm LabSync. Those matrices enable
the computation of collaborative estimations and knowledge exchange via
collaborative error propagation. Thus enabling knowledge exchange of ANNs
via the well-known SGD optimization algorithm.
The performance of RUL estimation has been assessed through two machine
learning architectures: MLP-KF and FMLP. MLP-KF incorporated federated
experiments with ANNs, while FMLP was chosen due to its demonstrated
comparability with the remarkable prediction capabilities of deep learning
methods. Both architectures have been evaluated in HFL, VFL and Hybrid-FL
experiments that comprised the construction of models in data-isolated,
data-centralized and federated approaches.
The comparison of models constructed in those approaches enabled answering
the following interrogations:
— 125 —

CHAPTER 5. TOW ARD FEDERATED HEALTH ASSESSMENT
• How well are prognosis models from data-isolated approaches predicting
the RUL on similar operating conditions experienced by other isolated
parties?
• Do federated models achieve similar prediction performances from a
data-centralized approach?
• Do federated models collaboratively improve the RUL estimation on the
federated approach?
Model transferability tests have been conducted in HFL to answer the first
interrogation. Those tests demonstrated that parties, with almost 25% of the
run-to-failure trajectories, can not precisely predict the RUL of turbofan engines
as a model constructed in a data-centralized approach does. The comparison
between federated models and those from data-centralized approaches has
revealed that federated models inevitably exhibit slightly diminished prediction
capabilities. Consequently, the second query was answered negatively.
Nevertheless, these differences are minor.
Subsequently, models from individual parties were compared with federated
models to address the final inquiry. Federated approaches, facilitated by the
sharing of labels and predictions, empowered individual parties to develop
models capable of predicting the RUL of turbofan engines belonging to others.
The experiments conducted in aerospace engineering, employing FedLabSync,
have harnessed collaborative learning attributes by utilizing a uni-dimensional
label. This prompts the examination of FedLabSync’s applicability in diverse
industrial applications and collaborative problems in the upcoming chapter.
