CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
L
abel synchronization served as a strategy to aggregate knowledge from AI
models of collaborative parties in a federated approach. This strategy,
designed to address collaborative problems when parties’ datasets may differ
in sample and feature spaces, was evaluated in problems related to predicting a
unidimensional label. This chapter describes label synchronization strategies in a
most general solution named Federated Multidimensional Label Synchronization
(FedMulLabSync). These strategies address collaborative problems where
sharing a unidimensional label may be insufficient, contributing to answering
research question RQ3 and supporting research goal RG3.
This proposal is compatible with unidimensional, time-sharing and
similarity-based label synchronization strategies to overcome collaborative
problems. Particularly, the two last strategies are designed for problems
in which sharing labels referring to a unidimensional label is insufficient,
especially in the presence of multidimensional target outputs and model drift
among parties given by Non-independent and Non-identically Distributed Data
(non-i.i.d.) data settings. Time-sharing augments the label space with time,
while similarity-based computes the similarity between two multidimensional
target labels of different parties.
The knowledge exchange via FedMulLabSync arises from minimizing
collaborative predictions for similar samples, which are directly linked through
label synchronization strategies. Those strategies have been inspired by
federated solutions dealing with non-i.i.d. data. This chapter evaluates the
capabilities of FedMulLabSync by addressing collaborative problems across
various industries beyond commercial aviation.
In the manufacturing industry, unidimensional label synchronization has
enabled collaborative improvement in the prediction of failures in multiple
Bosch production lines. In the aviation and maritime industry, time-sharing
and similarity-based label synchronization address the two main problems of
Prognostics and Health Management: failure prognostics and prognosis.
Collaborative problems presented in this chapter, contingent upon the data
partitioning setting, are deeply described in the following publications:
• Hybrid data partitioning : J2 and C1
• Horizontal data partitioning : J1
6.1 Introduction
Leveraging the knowledge exchange capabilities of different label synchronization
strategies, this chapter addresses collaborative learning problems across
industrial sectors beyond commercial aviation using the FedMulLabSync
solution. Acknowledging the limitations of label synchronization in capturing
sample similarity solely through a unidimensional space, this chapter introduces
time-sharing and similarity-based label synchronization strategies to compute
— 128 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
the similarity between two multidimensional labels of different parties. Those
strategies are inspired by various federated solutions overcoming model drift
of models among parties given by non-i.i.d. data settings. Thus enforcing and
diversifying the use cases of the initial solution, FedLabSync.
In the way of diversifying those use cases, this chapter highlights the
necessity of information-sharing agreements among parties. These agreements
are crucial for facilitating the exchange of diverse data types, which is essential
for collaborative learning through multidimensional label synchronization. In
summary, the main contributions of this chapter are:
1. A Federated Learning architecture tailored for configuring a
business-to-business (B2B) topology. This architecture stands out
for its versatility and adaptability, seamlessly accommodating the
exchange of diverse types of data. By leveraging the collaborative power
of FL within B2B environments, organizations can benefit from the
collective intelligence of their partners while ensuring data privacy.
2. The description of two collaborative learning problems outside the confines
of the commercial aviation industry. The first problem emerges within
the manufacturing industry, while the second is situated in the maritime
industry. Both problems underscore the need for a flexible federated
solution capable of adopting diverse information-sharing agreements
regarding the types of data exchanged among parties.
This chapter is organized as follows. Section 6.2 delineates the transition
from unidimensional to multidimensional label synchronization, elucidating
the flexible federated solution in light of diverse data exchange alternatives.
Section 6.3 outlines a possible collaboration among Bosch production lines
aimed at generalizing the prediction of product failures through the sharing
of unidimensional labels. Section 6.4 recalls the collaborative prognosis problem
related to the RUL estimation of turbofan engines to introduce the advantages of
time-sharing label synchronization. Finally, Section 6.5 exploits the capabilities
of similarity-based label synchronization by solving a collaborative prognostics
problem in which multiple vessels contribute in predicting anomalies in maritime
engines.
6.2 Label synchronization capabilities
Existing federated solutions addressing the challenge of aggregating knowledge
from AI models with differing structures, due to the owner parties’ datasets
varying in feature space Mj, are scarce. Moreover, the descriptions of these
solutions have primarily focused on solving classification problems and have
not fully leveraged the versatility of Artificial Neural Networks ( ANNs) for
addressing regression problems as well.
The Hybrid Federated Learning ( Hybrid-FL) solution from ( Su & Lau ,
2021), based on the separation of optimization problem of feature and sample
spaces through primal-dual transformation, demonstrated that it is possible to
aggregate knowledge of Machine Learning ( ML) methods compatible with the
— 129 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Stochastic Gradient Descent ( SGD) optimization algorithm. Although limiting
to the use of basic ML methods for regression problems, e.g., linear regression
or support vector regression, this solution can use of over-the-air transmissions
to reduce communication costs among parties.
The FedLabSync solution, described in the previous chapter for classification
and regression problems, implements a block-wise optimization to transmit
batches of similar samples while adopting a business-to-business (B2B) topology.
This topology was adopted by assuming parties configure server-to-server
communication using powerful and highly available computational devices.
During knowledge sharing, parties are expected to exchange non-sensitive
information through the intervention of a central server. To minimize the data
transmitted while employing an effective B2B knowledge exchange strategy, all
parties agree beforehand on the information to be shared within the federated
network.
6.2.1 Information sharing agreements
Whether or not federated solutions are adopted, data transmission is crucial
for addressing collaborative problems. Thus, defining the type of data
transmitted—managed by a communication-computation manager, e.g., a
central server—will largely depend on data privacy policies, the capacity of the
communication channel, and the knowledge aggregation strategy.
Federated Learning restrict parties from sharing sensitive information, such as
raw data, to accomplish privacy-preserving policies such as The General Data
Protection Regulation ( GDPR). Because federated AI models are improved
after performing multiple knowledge aggregation iterations K, minimizing the
data transmitted in the federated network is essential to avoid communication
channel overhead.
Block-wise optimization, which involves sharing batches of non-sensitive
information, aims to address this requirement partially. To better assess
this requirement, it is crucial to avoid sharing unnecessary data when
knowledge-sharing strategies depend on transmitting various data types. It is
because federated solutions may be based on exchanging multiple data types
simultaneously, e.g., model weights w, logits z (e.g., output vectors before
softmax), prediction outputs ˆY , intermediate feature maps, output targets
Y , data scaling statistics and among others ( Martínez Beltrán et al. , 2023).
Federated Learning architecture, illustrated in Figure 6.1a, shows how parties
can store multiple data types while agreeing to share a subset of them for
collaborative purposes.
6.2.2 Multidimensional label synchronization strategies
To describe the label synchronization proposals in a multidimensional space,
which broadens the use cases for Hybrid-FL in a most general solution named
Federated Multidimensional Label Synchronization (FedMulLabSync), what is
considered a label must be clarified. A label, lb, is a sample representation in
a smaller space that hides its original meaning, e.g., a subspace of processed
— 130 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Figure 6.1 : Federated architecture using FedMulLabSync.
Note: parties share different data types based on the knowledge aggregation strategy.
features whose interpretability is reserved for collaborative parties. This
consideration emerged because this synchronization strategy involves matching
similar samples using their features.
The FedMulLabSync solution exchanges knowledge from similar samples
within a federated network to deal with non-i.i.d. data. In this solution, inspired
by studies from ( Lu et al. , 2024) and ( Zhao & Shen , 2024), the similarity
of the samples is evaluated by calculating the proximity of their labels and
discarding deviated samples in the learning procedure improves the prediction
performance of the parties’ models. Figure 6.2 illustrates how the construction
of the federated model of the first party may fluctuate when dissimilar samples
(circles and squares) from the remaining parties are considered in the learning
process.
Figure 6.2 : Scheme of sample similarity among multiple collaborative parties.
Note: party 1 uses triangles from the remaining parties for training its federated model.
The federated solution considers labels as samples’ representations in smaller
spaces. This is because comparing sample similarity across large feature spaces,
such as vectors of sensor measurements, may contribute to the computation
overhead. To deal with communication channel overhead while respecting
data-privacy policies at the same time, parties hold sensitive information such
as samples X and their identifiers ids while exchanging with the central server
less-sensitive information such as model weights wj, labels lbs, normalization
scalers and output predictions ˆY . To better assess this kind of overhead,
the collaborative loss is calculated at each party after relating similar samples
located in a batch of data B. Figure 6.1b illustrates how the central server uses
— 131 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
that less-sensitive information to exchange knowledge with parties by calculating
the collaborative loss θ(. ) for a given batch of samples as FedLabSync does in
Algorithm 6.
Delving deeper, the collaborative loss θ(. )j
i for the j-th party and i-th sample
incorporates both the collaborative prediction and the local prediction Yj
i .
It suggests that the federated solution FedMulLabSyncs, which extends from
FedLabSync, also relies on the sequence in which predictions from matrix A
are accessed using indexes from the j-th synchronization matrix Mj
syc. In other
words, the success of this federated solution is based on the label synchronization
strategy adopted for a given problem. Therefore, strategies inspired by federated
solutions overcoming non-i.i.d. data settings are described in this chapter.
Evaluating strategies by simply instantiating a different label synchronization
algorithm in a general federated solution is the contribution of FedMulLabSync.
The generalization of FedLabSync in FedMulLabSync arises on having a variety
of synchronization algorithms to be called in Line 22 of Algorithm 6, an update
that is visible in FedMulLabSync solution from Annex B.
Two label synchronization strategies for multidimensional space have
been described in this chapter: time-sharing and similarity-based label
synchronization. Before describing them, the computation of the collaborative
loss function illustrated in Figure is explained 6.1b.
Independently of the label synchronization strategy adopted, the matrix Mj
syc
contains the sample indices of every party on a single column. Those indices,
pointers to asses A elements, are defined after finding the nearest value and
index at every point of Mlbs concerning the other columns or parties’ labels.
For example, for calculating the loss −0. 067 referred to the first sample of the
first party, the indexes of the first row of Mlbs are used to point predictions 1. 9,
2. 1 and 1. 9 using points in red. However, finding the nearest value index is
inadequate when multiple instances of the same label exist within a single party
and when labels are high dimensional. Therefore, the described strategies aim
to address both challenges simultaneously.
Considerations
When dealing with discretized labels with multiple instances within an isolated
party, pairing similar labels between two parties must take into account the
following considerations:
1. The coordinates of a pair of similar samples may be interchangeable. In
other words, (1. 1, 1. 2) and (1. 2, 1. 1) may be different pairs because labels
of the first coordinate point to two different samples of the target party j.
2. A label from one party can be paired with the same label from another
if multiple instances exist. However, repetitions of index combinations
should be minimized to ensure the maximum possible participation of
samples during knowledge exchange.
The baseline unidimensional label synchronization LabSync from Algorithm
4 accomplishes both requirements. It does so by counting the instances of a
determined label lb while avoiding pairing similar labels whose indices were
— 132 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
used in previous pairs. However, this algorithm pairs just unidimensional and
discretized labels.
In multidimensional synchronization, an easy solution may be sharing labels
to construct Mlbs and then constructing Msyc by pairing indexes of closest
labels given a determined distance metric, such as Euclidean, Cosine, Hamming,
Minkowski and Manhattan distance ( Aggarwal et al. , 2001). Unfortunately,
the computation cost of a force-brute algorithm increases depending on the
dimension of lb and the number of samples analyzed Nj, while sharing the label
in raw form may imply privacy concerns. To reduce computation costs, three
main approaches may be adopted:
1. Using sophisticated manager-side pairing algorithms such as divide and
conquer, kd-trees, ball trees, locality-sensitive hashing (LSH) ( Mark et al. ,
2008), etc.
2. Sharing labels resulting from applying dimensional reduction techniques,
such as linear discriminant analysis (LDA) or principal component analysis
(PCA) ( Jia et al. , 2022).
3. Sharing processed labels for accomplishing synchronization purposes while
obscuring their meaning for accomplishing privacy-preserving policies.
The last approach is considered by the multidimensional label synchronization
strategies: time-sharing and similarity-based.
Time-sharing label synchronization
Sharing knowledge related to unidimensional labels may be insufficient
for solving some collaborative problems, e.g., when samples with different
characteristics share the same label ( Ma et al. , 2022). In such cases, especially
in regression problems when the target output is time-dependent, including the
time variable alongside the target variable can be an effective solution. Figure
6.3 illustrates time-sharing synchronization alternatives depending on the label
space: n-dimensional (nD) or complex space.
The cartesian plane of Figure 6.3a illustrates 1D and 2D label synchronization
strategies of two points or labels ( Y 1 and Y 2) based on their positive distance,
s.t. ∆ Y =|Y 1−Y 2|. When two parties analyze sample similarity just considering
target outputY , the synchronization algorithm tends to repeat a pair of indexes.
This problem, illustrated in the first dotted rectangle a), could be partially solved
by adding a noise term z before calculating ∆ Y . This induced noise reduces
repetitions and enhances data privacy ( Ma et al. , 2022; Qi et al. , 2024).
When Y is timely dependent, a two-dimensional space, as shown in b),
can better capture similarity by incorporating the time variable. However,
constructing a bidimensional label by adding a time variable or a noise term
can increase the computational cost of distance when using distance metrics,
e.g., Euclidean distance.
The suggested alternative to constructing a bi-dimensional space refers to
constructing a complex space when the time variable is embedded in the
imaginary part. The third dotted rectangle in Figure 6.3 c) illustrates how
— 133 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Figure 6.3 : Alternatives for time-sharing label synchronization techniques.
the target Y and the time variable are encapsulated into a complex number to
calculate the similarity between colored points. While Y is configured as the
real part, the imaginary part comprises the cosine difference
cos(θ) = 1−∆ Y·∆ Time
||∆ Y||||∆ Time|| (6.1)
between the differences of target and time variables. Cosine similarity is adopted
to reduce pairing repetitions in the directional space.
This time-sharing label synchronization strategy also considers adding noise
z to the imaginary term when multiple instances of the same imaginary number
occur within a single party. The only requirement for this proposal is to set a
short noise level to maintain the importance of the direction difference. This
noise can be generated by any random distribution function, such as Gaussian
distribution, with statistics close to zero, e.g., standard deviation ≈0.
Similarity-based label synchronization
This similarity-based strategy was designed by thinking on time-series
reconstruction with a multidimensional space. This strategy, inspired by
federated solutions that cluster samples based on their prediction errors ( Lu et
al., 2024), aims to reduce the similarity analysis to a unidimensional space and
eliminate the use of distance metrics in a multidimensional space. To do that,
parties require sharing feature scalers and output targets Yj with the central
server while sharing model wj weights is optional. Instead of creating a matrix
Mlbs with parties’ output targets Yj, this strategy aims to create J matrices of
relative errors by reconstructing the relative error ∆ , s.t.
∆ = ||Y−Y||g

6.3 Failure detection in production lines
This section describes the capabilities of Federated Learning with unidimensional
label synchronization in solving a collaborative problem in the manufacturing
industry, whose data partition naturally corresponds to a hybrid one.
Experiments in manufacturing are considered because the demand for
high-quality products has compelled this industry to adopt new methods and
— 135 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
tools, such as data modelling, simulation, expert systems, and decision-making
support ( Hernandez et al. , 2006).
6.3.1 Control quality process
Manufacturing activities assisted by AI create the opportunity to optimize
manufacturing processes. Quality enhancement is one of the most valuable
activities, given the need to satisfy customer standards ( Hernandez et al. , 2006).
Predicting which products require inspection could increase the founding of
defective products while optimizing the control quality process ( Ning et al. ,
2022). Therefore, a strong interest exists in using AI models to assist product
inspection decisions (Kofi et al., 2022). However, making predictions on products
ensembled in many workstations has been challenging because data is inherently
distributed ( Moldovan et al. , 2019).
6.3.2 Bosch production lines
From three of the most illustrative manufacturing processes datasets (SECOM 1,
SEFTI2 and Bosch 3), the Bosch production lines dataset of 14.3GB seems to
be ideal because it aims to evaluate the capabilities of a Hybrid-FL solution.
This dataset was chosen because it prioritizes product inspection based on
failure predictions and because products are ensembled in multiple workstations
and production lines ( Ning et al. , 2022). Besides, the nature of a hybrid data
partition facilitates the simulation of a collaborative problem in the real world.
Bosch, one of the leading manufacturing companies, measured and tested
the assembling of 1184687 and 1183748 products, respectively. Each product,
identified as a single sample in this dataset, has different assembly processes.
Figure 6.5 shows that each sample has a maximum of 52 features ( S0−S51)
distributed at four production lines ( L0−L3). Features of those samples can
have one of the three types of variables: numerical, categorical, and date features
(Zhenyu et al. , 2020). Because it is possible to get the timestamp for each side
of the product passes, it is possible to identify the available features of a single
sample.
6.3.3 Problem and evaluation metrics
Given a series of features for each sample is named according to the production
line (L) and the stations ( S) that pass, such as L0_S1_D1 for the first feature,
it is possible to construct a dataset of N samples and M features to develop AI
models. Current experiments use 1184687 samples and 968 numerical features
distributed along production lines, a data distribution that naturally requires
adopting a Hybrid-FL solution. Table 6.1 details the number of features per
production line, along with the count of positive and negative samples in this

The prediction for Bosch products corresponds to a binary classification
problem. Therefore, the output dimension of the decoding model is configured
as 0 when a product passes the quality control process and 1 when a product
inspection is required. The F1-score classification metric is adopted to balance
the importance of precision and recall in current experiments, following previous
studies in manufacturing monitoring ( Ning et al. , 2022).
6.3.4 Baseline model in data-centralized approach
In developing an AI model for the current binary classification problem, two
main stages gain relevance: data cleaning and feature selection. Exhaustive
data-cleaning procedures are required because only a few workstations collect
data from most product identifiers IDs. After analysing the proportion of missing
observations per feature and sample, data-cleaning is performed at two levels as
(Carbery et al. , 2019) suggests: samples and features levels.
When cleaning samples, 1183748 products of the test split are discarded
because they can not be identified as positive or negative samples. This cleaning
procedure also considers removing duplicated samples and samples containing
more than 142 features with missing values ( Zhenyu et al. , 2020). Finally, when
cleaning features, features with duplicated names, zero variance, and those with
more than 70% of missing values are discarded. These cleaning procedures
obtain a dataset of 1094995 samples and 163 characteristics, which allows the
stage of feature selection and the construction of the test set with
— 137 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Table 6.2: Bosch data used in model development.
Samples Train 80% Eval 20% Test 20% (no SMOTE)
Positive 513918 128479 217727
Negative 256958 64240 1271
Selecting the input parameters influencing the outcome result from applying
a PCA. The application of PCA is performed in two groups of features. While
the first group comprises features of L0,L1 andL2 production lines, the second
group refers to features of L3. Applying a PCA aims to reduce the feature space
to 22 in each group. The number of principal components is set at 22 because,
according to ( D. Zhang et al. , 2016), those components can represent more than
95% of the variance.
The proportions of positive and negative samples in the original dataset point
to an unbalanced one. Therefore, oversampling techniques must be required
before training the AI model. The Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique
(SMOTE) is applied in this experiment using the configurations described by
(Carbery et al. , 2019).
SMOTE is configured to randomly reduce the entire dataset by 10%,
maintaining a 1:3 ratio of negative to positive samples. This oversampling
process results in 642,397 positive samples and 321,199 negative samples in
training and evaluation sets, each with 44 features. In developing the AI model,
those samples are distributed for training, evaluation and testing purposes
according to the percentages indicated in Table 6.2.
Although studies have constructed Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM)
networks to obtain the long-term dependence on time series data ( Carbery et
al., 2019), current research constructs an ANN using only numerical features
without the interest in capturing time dependencies. Table 6.3 writes in bold the
hyperparameters used to construct this ANN. This configuration was adopted
through a grid search method and fixing the training procedure at 400 epochs
E when using SGD optimization.
The performance of the data-centralized approach is evaluated considering
thirty models trained with different initial weights w0. It allows the computation
of the performance variability, having an F1-score of 0. 83±0. 005. Because this

result seems to be comparative with previous works ( Moldovan et al., 2019; Ning

6.3.5 Experiments in unidimensional label synchronization
Data pre-processing reveals that objects are measured differently depending on
the number of stations ( S) they pass through on a given production line ( L),
as Figure 6.5 illustrates. Therefore, it suggests that production lines may be in
different locations for practical purposes while are interested in privately sharing
knowledge to improve the prediction of defective products.
The collaborative problem starts by assuming the participation of four parties
whose datasets configure a hybrid data partitioning, as Table 6.4 suggests.
While the samples of the global dataset D are distributed using the 4n selection
criterion, features are distributed considering the production lines of each party:
L0-L2 orL3. This data partition does not presuppose losing information because
every party shares approximately 240899 samples and features of one group
of production lines. Thus, the performance prediction of a data-centralized
approach is directly comparable with models in federated solutions.
The knowledge exchange among parties is orchestrated by FedMulLabSync,
which uses the LabSync algorithm to synchronize the unidimensional output
target. Similarly to the data-centralized approach, thirty AI global
models summarize the prediction performance and its variance. While the
data-centralized approach has a F1-score of 0.83 ±0.005, the federated approach
has a F1-score of 0.79 ±0.009. All AI global models were trained with different
initial weights w0 within 400 knowledge exchange iterations K.
Performance predictions in federated approaches appear to decrease by 4%
compared to data-centralized approaches. This slight reduction in the prediction
performance of AI global models was observed in previous federated experiments.
However, since global models are typically used only for proof-of-concept
experiments, it is important to determine whether federated models (subspaces
of the global model) gain or lose performance after being federated.
Performance gains at each party
Thirty federated experiments resulted in 120 models trained with partial data Dj
and 120 federated models, having each party own 25% of them. The prediction
performance in terms of accuracy and F1-score at each party is summarized in
Table 6.5. Noticeably, the failure prediction at each production line has been

improved after the parties shared their knowledge. For example, the fourth
party obtained a classification accuracy of 0. 821±0. 014 by using only its local
data, but it was able to increase this performance by almost 4% after entering
the federation. The same phenomenon is repeated by comparing F1-scores.
Although federated models of isolated parties are expected to perform worse
than a data-centralized model, experiments with the Bosch dataset suggest
that federated models can exceed expectations. Similar effects were observed in
C-MAPSS datasets when using FMLPs ( Llasag Rosero, Silva & Ribeiro , 2022),
leading to the conclusion that these performance improvements may be related
to the properties of the data used. Therefore, FedMulLabSync capabilities have
been evaluated in relation to other collaborative problems.
6.4 RUL of turbofans with real flight conditions
This section evaluates the capabilities of the time-sharing multidimensional
label synchronization strategy for solving a collaborative prognosis problem via
Federated Learning. This collaborative problem, referring to the estimation of
the RUL of turbofan engines and configuring a horizontal data partitioning,
differs from the previous chapter because current experiments use N-CMAPSS,
the repository whose condition monitoring data was simulated using flight
conditions from a real jet. Thus, a more realistic collaboration among airlines
can be created.
6.4.1 Collaborative prognosis problem
The federated experiments conducted in this section take advantage of the
baseline RUL estimator of turbofan engines under a data-centralized approach
described in Section 4.4.2 and the variability of flight classes described in Table
2.3 for the N-CMAPSS repository. Given the interest of aviation stakeholders
in determining whether models from individual airlines can assertively predict
the RUL of aircraft systems operating under various conditions, this section
describes the collaboration of airlines whose air fleets operate in different flight
classes (FC).
— 140 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Air fleets
The current collaborative prognosis problem is motivated by the composition of
the DS subsets of the N-CMAPSS repository, which each one contains data on
turbofan engines operating on short, medium, and long flights. In the federated
scenario, the length of flights—referred to as flight classes (FCs)—inspires the
simulation of cooperation among three airlines offering national, continental, and
international flights. Considering this data partitioning setting, the number of
turbofan units for each air fleet associated with a specific flight class is described
in Table 6.6.

6.4.2 Experiments in time-sharing label synchronization
Using the Federated Health Assessment ( FHA) evaluation framework, AI
models were constructed in data-isolated and federated approaches for each
flight class to assess the feasibility of time-sharing label synchronization. The
performance of these models is compared with a baseline model from the
data-centralized approach described in Section 4.4.2. This baseline model,
built on an inception-based CNN network from ( DeVol et al., 2021), achieved a
prediction error of 9.8 ±0.4 in terms of MAE after being developed and tested
with data from all DS sets five times with different initial weights. To evaluate
the prediction performance of each flight class in data-isolated, federated, and
data-centralized approaches, the models of these approaches are tested with data
from local turbofan units and all units.
Isolated models
Predictive models in data-isolated approaches have been developed using data
from turbofan units of a determined flight class (FC). Those models adopt the
same inception-based CNN network and training hyperparameters exposed in
— 141 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Section 4.4.2. Batches of size 128 were configured to expedite the training
process, which was conducted over a maximum of 200 epochs. An early stopping
callback, triggered after five epochs of unfavourable training, was used to prevent
overfitting during the minimization of the RMSE loss function. The SGD
optimization algorithm was adopted with a learning rate of 0.01.
A series of isolated models presented an MAE around 5.09, 7.22 and 10.2
for the first, second and third flight classes, respectively. Because results are
similar to those in ( DeVol et al. , 2021), the data partition is considered for
federated experiments. Those experiments aim to evaluate if airlines could reuse
an airline’s model trained with data from a single flight class. To answer this
question, the prediction performance of isolated models is calculated using test
sets of all FCs.
Table 6.7 presents the RUL estimation error in terms of MAE for models
developed using data-centralized, data-isolated, and federated approaches.
These models were evaluated using data from a single FC as well as from all
FCs combined.
The bold values in the first row indicate that the RUL estimation error
increases when an isolated model is evaluated using data from other flight classes.
This phenomenon is evident in the first-row comparison of Figure 4.11, where
the model trained on the first FC was tested with local turbofan units and
then with units from all FCs. For example, the MAE of approximately 10.2,
obtained using units from the third flight class, increases to around 21.57 when
the isolated models are tested with units from all FCs. This result suggests
that models developed in data-isolated approaches are nontransferable, giving
relevance to the evaluation of federated solutions.

Federated models
Federated models are developed by configuring FedMulLabSync with a
time-sharing label synchronization. In this collaborative problem, parties share
a bidimensional label embedded in a complex space. While the real part
corresponds to the RUL of the turbofan unit, the second dimension refers to
the cosine difference between the RUL and the time cycle. Time-sharing label
— 142 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
synchronization is chosen because turbofan units may present samples (sensor
measurements) associated with the same RUL during a flight (time cycle).
When defining the label to share within the federated network, random noise is
added to the complex term to prevent duplication when pairing similar instances.
This noise follows a Gaussian distribution with a zero mean and a standard
deviation of 0.0001. Federated experiments use the same hyperparameters
and callback while creating models in data-isolated approaches, except for the
number of training local epochs, which is one in this case. FedMulLabSync is
configured to get federated models in a maximum of 900 knowledge aggregation
iterations K while the collaborative weight hyperparameter ψis set at 0.01.
Looking at the results from Table 6.7, it is easy to notice that models
constructed on a data-centralized scenario reduce the MAE of isolated models

when tests with data from all FCs. With this premise, federated models from
individual parties aim to evaluate the following:
• If they perform similarly to isolated models when tested with data from
local turbofan units;
• If they perform similarly to the data-centralized model when tested with
data from turbofan units from all FCs.
Looking at the second, third and fourth rows from Table 6.7, it can be seen
that isolated models from FC1 and FC2 perform better than the data-centralized
scenario. The isolated model from FC3 does not, suggesting that data from
FC3 is deviated. This makes sense because this party experiences long flights
where health degradation becomes apparent only after many flight hours.
Consequently, a model in a non data-centralized approach effectively learns and
captures degradation over extended flights.
The MAE values of approximately 9.89, 9.97, and 10.2 in the first row of
Table 6.7 are highlighted in bold because they represent an improvement in the
prediction accuracy of models from data-isolated approaches when tested with
data from all FCs. Although these values do not surpass the performance of the
data-centralized model, which has a MAE of 9.80±0.4, they are close.
Examining the second and third rows of Figure 6.6, which compare the
RUL predictions per flight cycle for units from FC1 and FC3 using federated
and data-centralized models, it is evident that the federated model from FC1
performs better in FC3 compared to either data-centralized or federated models.
To facilitate this comparison, Figure 6.6 shows the mean RUL per flight cycle
as a dotted line and the prediction range per flight cycle as a shaded area of the
same color. Performance improvements were also evident in FC2 and FC3 in
the remaining federated models.
6.5 Anomaly detection on maritime engines
This section outlines the capabilities of FedMulLabSync in tackling
a collaborative prognostics problem in the maritime sector through
similarity-based label synchronization. This knowledge aggregation strategy is
particularly adequate for anomaly detection in maritime engines, as it involves
reconstructing sensor signal measurements and measuring their deviations,
which usually have big values in abnormal operating conditions.
6.5.1 Context and motivation
Monitoring a vessel’s main engine is crucial because it is the primary subsystem
and the main source of propulsion power during navigation. Failures during the
engine operation may cause several accidents and significant business losses ( Kim
et al. , 2020). For example, the cargo ship named “Ever Given” was grounded
on the banks of the Suez Canal in 2021, resulting in a blockage that caused
trade losses of approximately €8.3B per day ( Shiqi et al. , 2022). Consequently,
leading companies in the maritime sector, such as WinGD (Winterthur Gas and
— 144 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Diesel Ltd.), a manufacturer and developer of maritime engines, are striving to
adopt AI-assisted maintenance strategies.
A work collaboration with WinGD has been beneficial in developing
this thesis. Although interested in adopting Predictive Maintenance, this
company has focused firstly on adopting Condition-Based Maintenance solutions
motivated by the complexity of gathering labeled data from in-production
environments. The development of prognostic models is suitable when prognosis
models cannot assertively predict the RUL of machines due to the lack of health
degradation insights throughout their life cycle.
Failure prognostics involve detecting anomalies in real-time and potentially
diagnosing their impact ( Biggio & Kastanis , 2020). Anomaly detection
classifies Health Indicators ( HIs) of machines via supervised, semi-supervised
or unsupervised learning techniques. Unsupervised learning has recently
gained attention into the development of AI models capable of reconstructing
measurements under healthy conditions ( Fink et al. , 2020). Deviations during
the reconstruction of abnormal measurements serve as models for alerting about
potential anomalies ( Chao et al. , 2019; Michau et al. , 2018).
Signal reconstruction via encoder-decoder architectures liberates tedious
data label processes, facilitating PHM practitioners in developing models by
considering samples from healthy periods ( Basora et al. , 2021). In doing so,
anomaly detection is tested using time periods specified in event data. In other
words, samples in which anomalies were detected by technicians are primarily
used in testing environments.
6.5.2 Problem and dataset
Engines of modern maritime vessels utilize onboard sensors and data acquisition
systems to monitor their health. WinGD included onboard sensors in their
otto-cycle internal combustion and direct-coupled two-stroke engine called
12X92DF (Printz et al., 2022). In this engine, operated by dual fuel with natural
gas and diesel, various sensors have been installed to monitor the functions of
subsystems in the powertrain ( Cimniak et al. , 2022).
The propulsion of a vessel primarily depends on the cylinders, liner walls, and
other components of the combustion chambers. These components support the
movement of each of the twelve pistons in the 12X92DF engine, which are in
direct contact with the flame spread. Because the friction between cylinders and
liner walls directly impacts engine efficiency, three temperature sensors (A, B,
and C) have been placed at a circular distance of 120º to detect overheating on
those stressed components. Figure 6.7 illustrates the 36 temperature sensors
installed to detect anomalies in these components, particularly the cylinder
scuffing phenomenon. This issue typically arises when localized welding occurs
between the piston ring and the liner surface.
Previous studies from ( Cimniak et al. , 2022) and ( Albisser, 2023) have
been joined operating settings Ow from Table 6.8 to sensor signals Xs for
their reconstruction. While fuel mode, fuel command, and relative engine
speed describe the engine’s basic operating conditions, the number of activated
injection nozzles directly affects the liner wall temperature distribution. For

instance, when a single nozzle is activated, it produces a non-uniform flame,
elevating temperatures in specific regions. With those input variables, anomalies
are detected by comparing the reconstructed signals Xs with the original ones

6.5.3 Evaluation metrics
In the current CBM solution, a potential cylinder scuffing event is considered
when a big difference exists between the predicted Xs and real sensor
measurement Xs. This difference is normalized w.r.t a distance reference
captured from the validation data |∆ val|=|Xsval−ˆXsval|. Considering γ= 1
and t = γ×Q(0. 99) as the 99 percentile from |∆ val|, the anomaly score a is
defined as:
a =|Xs−Xs|
t . (6.4)
This score is filtered to have smooth predictions because it is naturally
noisy. Current experiments apply an Exponentially Weighted Moving A verage
(EWMA) to a for having a smoothest value at t time:
EWMA t =a′(λ,N) =
∑N − 1
i=0 λiat− 1
∑N − 1
i=0 λi , (6.5)
where N represents the length of the noisy signal. After filtering a, the t-th
time is considered abnormal when EWMA t crosses a predefined threshold. This
threshold is useful for configuring a binary classification problem in which 1
— 147 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
suggests the presence of an anomaly while 0 does not. A period-wise anomaly
detection analysis was adopted because detecting anomalies at every sample
may be impractical at low sampling rates, such as when measurements are taken
every second. A percentage p of samples crossing an anomaly threshold Tθis
configured to activate alarms. By setting p in 0.8, Figure 6.8 illustrates how the
first period of five samples alerted an anomaly after being colored in red because
80% of their values on EWMA crossed the threshold set in 1. The green period
indicates normal operating conditions, as less than %80 of the samples surpassed
the anomaly threshold.
Figure 6.8 : Period-wise anomaly detection when p = 0. 8 and Tθ= 1.
The period-wise analysis is compatible with traditional binary classification
metrics. Since the AI-based solution reconstructs multiple temperature
measurements simultaneously, the current experiments utilize micro-average
precision, recall, and F1-score (F1), simulating a multi-classification problem.
When computing the multi-classification performance on twelve cylinders Cyl ,
the following considerations are taken into account due to the nature of the
problem:
• True Positives (TP)as scuffing events detected correctly.
• False Negatives (FN)as scuffing events do not detected.
• False Positives (FP)as days with anomaly alerts when there is no scuffing.
• True Negatives (TN)as days with normal behaviour detected as healthy.

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
6.5.4 Experiments in similarity-based label synchronization
Adopting the FHA framework described in Section 5.2, anomaly detectors
were constructed on data-centralized, data-isolated, and federated approaches
to evaluate the feasibility of similarity-based label synchronization. Before
evaluating whether models on data-isolated and federated approaches can
assertively predict anomalies in operating conditions experienced by other
vessels, this section first describes the model development process using the
PHM program outlined in Section 4.2.
Data-driven model construction
The model development of anomaly detectors is described in three main stages:
data acquisition, pre-processing, and prognostics model. In 2021, during data
acquisition, vessels navigating from Europe to Asia and vice versa throughout
the Suez Canal were measured every second. Figure 6.9 illustrates the navigation
route of a vessel from February to June.
Figure 6.9 : Route navigation between Europe and Asia via the Suez Canal.
Technicians inspected a fleet of vessels, primarily finding anomalies in the
cylinders of three vessels. This event data is useful for defining development and
test sets. As the development of AI model uses data in normal conditions to
alert when the reconstruction of samples in abnormal conditions presents high
deviations, periods in which vessels do not present failures are required. Table
6.10 describes the periods in which three vessels were measured and the periods
in which the healthy vessels operated normally, defining the development sets.
On the other hand, the development of test sets is influenced by verified cylinder
scuffing events previously presented in Table 6.9.
During data pre-processing, duplicate samples are discarded. Samples are also
removed if the fuel mode value is not 1, 3, or 4, corresponding to diesel, gas, and
dual modes. Table 6.11 details the percentages of samples for each fuel mode
across different vessels, indicating that the diesel mode is the most frequently
used, especially in the third vessel. The number of injection nozzles is limited

For developing the prognostics model, the adoption of deep learning methods
was influenced by previous studies from ( Cimniak et al. , 2022) and ( Albisser,
2023). Given the availability of long datasets, DL models have obtained
promising results by developing encoder-decoders, particularly via LSTM and
CNN. Both use a data matrix to reconstruct a vector. This thesis adopted
the encoder-decoder CNN from ( Ronneberger et al. , 2015) by configuring 1D
convolutions and reducing the number of filters to 32. This encoder-decoder,
named U-net, has been adopted because ( Albisser, 2023) has presented
promising results into detecting anomalies in liner walls using this neural
network.
As the original U-Net gets image data as input, the current U-net version
— 150 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
configures a sliding-window setup in which time series of shape XT ×M . This
time series is to reconstruct the first row when T = 1 . Sliding windows of ten
timesteps are constructed by configuring a single stride and setting T in 10 and
M in 40. Configuring that, each vessel has a development set of 57528 sliding
windows and test set of 14472. As was mentioned in Equation 6.5.2, 40 features
fromOw andXs are used to reconstruct only the 36 features of Xs. Therefore,
while the input has a shape of X 10× 40, the output has a shape of Y 1× 36.
Interpretation of anomaly alerts
To evaluate the detection of anomalies at each engine, test periods of Table 6.10
were used. The specified periods were chosen because they comprise key events
from Table 6.9, indicating when a determined cylinder presented scuffing and
when it was overhauled. Because engines present scuffing and overhaul events
in multiple cylinders, sometimes in parallel, periods with anomalies and healthy
conditions were deeply analyzed with the expert’s supervision.
Samples where scuffing occurs were not labeled by technicians during
the dataset’s construction. Consequently, confusion matrices are manually
constructed for each cylinder. To measure the anomaly detection performance,
micro-averaged metrics from Equations 6.5.3, 6.5.3 and 6.5.3, are used. In the
way of conducting period-wise evaluations, periods of 6 hours are individually
analyzed after reconstructing Xs. Once the EWMA signal is obtained by
evaluating the reconstruction of Xs, the following criteria are used to distinguish
between healthy and unhealthy states. First, the threshold is set at 2. Next, the
number of samples exceeding this threshold are counted. A period is classified as
anomalous if more than 95% of its samples surpassed the threshold. Conversely,
periods where this condition is not accomplished are considered healthy.
Adopting the classification criteria above, an anomaly alert is triggered for a
given day if there is at least one abnormal interval of six hours in any of the
cylinder regions: A, B, or C. For this particular classification problem, TP, FP,
TN and FN are counted depending on the nature of the analyzed period. Using
data from the second vessel as a reference, Figure 6.10 illustrates the health
periods and outlines the expected detection status for each, using the isolated
prediction for the second vessel as a reference:
1. Green period refers to samples gathered during normal operating
conditions. During this time, a detection is considered as a FP, while
a missed detection is considered as a TN.
2. Red period refers to days in which inspections confirmed the presence of
cylinder scuffing. An anomaly alert in this period is considered as a TP
while a missed detection is considered as a FN.
3. Overhaul arrow indicates the date and the location in which the
maintenance intervention was performed.
4. Yellow period refers to two days before and five days after a confirmed
cylinder scuffing. The days before the red period indicate that the
anomaly may be earliest detected, while the days after signify the delay
— 151 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Figure 6.10 : Anticipated anomaly alerts for early scuffing detection in vessel 2.
The plot illustrates expected health states in different coloured periods for the 12 cylinders.
The table describes how to interpret daily detection results ( ✓ or×) for each period.
in recovering to a healthy condition after an overhaul intervention. An
anomaly detection during this period is considered as a TP, whereas a
missed detection is considered as a TN since missed detections are also
welcomed. Particularly, the period after overhauling the eighth cylinder
of the third vessel comprises three days, as technicians confirmed that the
health condition recovered within this time.
5. Orange period refers to the propagation of an anomaly to related
cylinders based on expert insights. An anomaly alert during this period
is counted similarly to one in the red period. However, orange periods
are included in performance evaluations only when there is at least one
anomaly alert, as anomalies rarely propagate to physically connected
cylinders.
Isolated models
Following the logic of the FHA framework, illustrated in Figure 5.2, predictive
models were initially constructed in data-isolated approaches. Three isolated
models were developed to evaluate the capabilities of the U-net to predict
anomalies in motor engines separately. The model of each vessel was trained
during a maximum of 100 epochs after configuring batches of size 128. An early
stopping callback, waiting for unfavourable training during eight epochs, was
configured to avoid overfitting during the minimization of the loss function, i.e.,
RMSE, MSE and others. Particularly, the MSE function presented promising
results by adopting the Adaptive Moment Estimator (Adam) optimization
algorithm with a learning rate 10− 3, momentum 0. 9 and weight-decay 10− 5.
To calculate the prediction performance using micro-average precision, recall
and F1 metrics, confusion matrices have been created for each cylinder and
for each engine. Figure 6.11 illustrates the confusion matrices obtained for
evaluating the classification performance of models using data from local engines.
— 152 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
The results of data-isolated experiments are presented in the first three columns
of Table 6.12.
Figure 6.11 : Confusion matrices for each cylinder using data-isolated models.
After evaluating isolated models with local data, experiments demonstrated
an F1-score of 0.74, 0.56 and 0.49 for the models from the first, second and
third vessels, respectively. Those results show the isolated model of the first
vessel as the best one, although it presents anomalies in multiple cylinders in
parallel as specified in Table 6.9. To discover if collaborative approaches may
improve those results, a model was trained using the same U-net architecture in
a data-centralized approach.
Baseline model in data-centralized approach
The U-net of the data-centralized approach was trained using data from all
maritime engines. It serves to provide a baseline performance that an ideal
federated model may achieve. The confusion matrices of Appendix C were
obtained after evaluating a data-centralized model with data from each vessel.
The values for micro-average metrics, presented in the fourth, fifth, and
sixth columns of Table 6.12, suggest that a data-centralized model reduces the
prediction performance of isolated models when trying to detect anomalies in
the engines of all vessels. The values in parenthesis of Table 6.12 means that
prediction performance was surpassed by models in data-isolated approaches.
According to expert insights, performance losses arise due to the
heterogeneous data distribution, particularly the variance in the percentage
of fuel modes described in Table 6.11. This is significant because anomalies
have mostly been identified during the switching of fuel modes while the
vessel apparently operated in different conditions ( Albisser, 2023). To evaluate
the similarity of operating conditions among vessels, the transferability of

models from data-isolated approaches was considered. Transferable models were
expected if the vessels exhibited similar operating conditions.

Model transferability tests
At this stage, models were evaluated using data from individual vessels only.
To determine if isolated models can assertively predict anomalies under the
operating conditions of other vessels, they were tested with data from the entire
fleet. The results of these experiments, presented in the first row of Table
6.13, suggest that isolated models decrease in performance when predicting
anomalies across the fleet. In other words, isolated models are non-transferable,
especially the model of the second vessel, which presented anomalies in only
one cylinder. This can be verified by comparing the F1-score of the third, fifth,
and seventh columns with the F1-score achieved by the data-centralized model.
As in previous experiments, performances written in bold indicate cases where
prediction performance is improved.

Federated models
To determine if federated models can predict anomalies under the operating
conditions of multiple vessels, federated models were constructed using the
FedMulLabSync solution. This solution was configured with a similarity-based
label synchronization strategy while maintaining the U-net hyperparameters
adopted in data-isolated approaches. Federated models were obtained in a
— 154 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
maximum of 1200 knowledge aggregation iterations K by setting ψat 10− 4.
Before collaboratively training models, parties agree to share predictions, models
and scalers to obtain Msyc matrices.
Confusion matrices from Appendix D were generated after evaluating the
federated models to obtain the values specified in Table 6.12. At this stage, all
models were evaluated using data from each vessel separately as well as using
data from the entire fleet. Values of F1-score in the “ FL” columns of Table
6.12 suggest that federated models improve the prediction performance of the
data-centralized model but lose performance when comparing them with isolated
models. It can be seen by focusing on the best predictions written in bold and
the worst predictions in parenthesis.
Federated models enable isolated vessels to detect anomalies in other vessels,
meeting the expectations for model transferability. By inspecting the values
in the second, third, and fourth rows of Table 6.12, it is evident that there
are instances where federated models outperform isolated models. The only
federated model that significantly loses performance in favour of detecting
anomalies in other engines is the one from the first vessel. This is understandable
because data pre-processing has primarily benefited the development of an
isolated model for the first vessel, while the construction of a federated model
for this vessel involves incorporating similar data from the entire fleet.
A clear example of having non-transferable models to having transferable ones
can be seen in Figure 6.12. This figure compares data-isolated (top) vs federated
models (bottom) from the first vessel. The models are evaluated using data from
the first (left) and third vessel (right). Notably, the isolated model of the first
vessel tested with data from the third vessel (top right) does not detect verified
anomalies in red periods as effectively as the federated model (bottom right).
6.6 Discussion of results
Given the complexity of gathering representative datasets for assertive
constructing AI models, aviation and other industrial sectors are interested
in collaborative solutions to take advantage of parties monitoring equivalent
things. Federated Learning gained attention due to its capability of aggregating
knowledge from multiple parties without compromising the data confidentiality
of collaborative parties.
The need for federated solutions that are compatible with various data
partitioning settings and adaptable to both classification and regression
problems led to the proposal of a Hybrid-FL solution called FedLabSync.
This solution aggregates knowledge from multiple parties by utilizing samples
with similar unidimensional labels in a synchronized learning procedure.
This chapter describes multidimensional label synchronization strategies,
including time-sharing and similarity-based methods, to address problems where
unidimensional label synchronization is insufficient.
An extension of FedLabSync named FedMulLabSync has been designed
to cover multiple problems that can be solved via different synchronization
strategies. The capabilities of those strategies have been evaluated in
collaborative problems of three different industrial sectors: manufacturing,
— 155 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
Figure 6.12 : Assessing the transferability and federation of anomaly detectors.
Performance evaluations are performed using the isolated (up) and federated model
(bottom) of the first vessel using data from the first (left) and the third vessel (right). The
bottom-right plot shows that federated solutions can enable isolated vessels to predict the
health state of the engines of other vessels.
aviation and maritime.
Federated models presented promising results in the manufacturing sector
by using unidimensional label synchronization when datasets parties differ in
feature and sample space. Particularly, the problem adopted was configured
for predicting defective products to optimize the quality control process in
different product lines. These models have improved the prediction performance
compared to those built with data from isolated production lines and have
produced competitive results when compared to a data-centralized approach.
For instance, the model of the third production line L3 achieved an accuracy
of 0.825±0.011 in a data-isolated approach and 0.82 ±0.04 in a data-centralized
approach, with the accuracy increasing by nearly 4% using a federated approach.
Similar results emerged in the remaining production lines L0−L2.
Federated Learning with time-sharing label synchronization, inspired by
federated solutions addressing non-i.i.d. data, has enabled predictive models
from different airlines to estimate the RUL of turbofan units operating
under various conditions, specifically across different flight classes. The RUL
estimation for turbofan units operating in short, medium, and long flights is
performed by single AI models constructed using FedMulLabSync. Because
— 156 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
federated models incorporate knowledge from both local samples and similar
samples from other parties, they can perform comparably to local monitoring
systems under similar operating conditions. For instance, the federated model
for the airline operating in flight class 3 reduced the RUL estimation error from
21.75±5.9 to 10.2 ±2.1 when tested with data from the three available flight
classes. The results of federated models are similar to those achieved with
data-centralized approaches. However, when more precise models are required
for turbofans operating within a single flight class, data-isolated approaches
appear to be more stable.
The similarity-based label synchronization strategy enabled vessels to
collaborate in predicting motor engine anomalies, particularly in alerting about
cylinder scuffing. This problem involved reconstructing sensor signals using an
encoder-decoder architecture, requiring a collaborative approach to analyze the
similarity of labels in a multidimensional space.
The similarity-based approach simplifies the comparison of multiple features
to a single value by focusing on the reconstruction error of a sample with
multiple features. When evaluating the classification problem of scuffing alerts in
multiple cylinders, federated models more accurately predicted anomalies under
the operating conditions of the participating parties. This highlighted a clear
advantage of FedMulLabSync, as models from data-isolated approaches could
only predict anomalies under local conditions, while a data-centralized model
performed poorly due to the non-i.i.d. nature of the data from different fuel
modes. For instance, the model from the data-isolated approach of the second
vessel had an F1-score of 0.22 when evaluated with data from all vessels, while
the federated model for this vessel achieved an F1-score of 0.45. Despite the
performance gains of federated models, current experiments suggest that they
might sacrifice prediction capabilities on local data in favor of better performance
under the operating conditions of external parties.
6.7 Summary
This chapter presents two label synchronization strategies for FedMulLabSync:
time-sharing and similarity-based synchronization. This federated solution
is compatible with various data partitioning settings and supports both
classification and regression problems, thereby encompassing a broader range of
use cases than its predecessor, FedLabSync, introduced in the previous chapter.
While the synchronization strategies contribute to addressing research goal
RG3, the description of FedMulLabSync itself also fulfills research goal RG3.
Collaborative problems in different industrial areas beyond aviation are
explained in this chapter. Demonstrating the capabilities of FedMulLabSync in
covering classification and regression problems inside and outside Prognostics
and Health Management. Two collaborative problems inside this discipline
are covered: collaborative prognostics and prognosis. The first one is related
to implementing federated Condition-Based Maintenance strategies, while the
second one is related to conducting federated predictive maintenance via RUL
estimation.
The RUL estimation is extended to experiments with data from turbofan
— 157 —

CHAPTER 6. MULDIMENSIONAL LABEL SYNCHRONIZATION
engines with operating conditions captured from a real jet. On the other
hand, the CBM problem refers to detecting anomalies in maritime engines
from Winterthur Gas & Diesel Lda (WinGD). Particularly, this last one is
performed after analyzing the reconstruction error of sensor signals, which
requires configuring multidimensional labels. Both problems are performed by
configuring a horizontal data partitioning approach in which all parties’ datasets
share the same feature scape.
To evaluate the capabilities of FedMulLabSync in handling hybrid data
partitioning approaches, where parties’ datasets differ in feature and sample
space, we adopted a problem from industrial manufacturing. Specifically, the
task of classifying defective and acceptable products that aim to improve the
quality control process in Bosch production lines. These lines differ in the
number of stations and, consequently, in the number of features, resulting in a
naturally hybrid data partitioning scenario. Since this problem involves binary
classification, knowledge has been exchanged among parties via unidimensional
label synchronization. This strategy has helped improve prediction performance
on individual production lines.
— 158 —

7. Conclusions and Future Works
T
his thesis has introduced Federated Multidimensional Label
Synchronization (FedMulLabSync), a model-agnostic federated learning
solution designed to enhance the predictive performance of models from
different parties, even when they exhibit discrepancies in their input data
spaces. Throughout the development of this solution, primarily in the
context of machines health condition monitoring, FedMulLabSync has made
significant contributions to field of Prognostics and Health Management ( PHM).
These advancements hold particular importance for industries with strict safety
requirements, such as aviation and shipping, where reliable condition monitoring
is crucial.
Through strategic adopting of maintenance strategies based on Remaining
Useful Life ( RUL) estimation, the thesis primarily contributes to aviation
industry by leveraging run-to-failure data from aircraft systems, made available
through active participation in the Horizon2020 ReMAP project. While basic
Artificial Intelligence ( AI) techniques proved insufficient for identifying health
degradation patterns from condition monitoring data of Boeing 747 cooling units
to estimate RUL, this thesis contributes by describing a PHM pipeline that
guides the construction of robust data-driven models.
The PHM pipeline integrates physics-based methodology to construct health
indicators from sensor data in both time and frequency domains, feeding these
to AI-based RUL estimators. Although the limited run-to-failure examples for
cooling units constrained data-decentralized approaches, synthetic data from the
C-MAPSS turbofan simulator enabled the exploration of collaborative models.
Various neural network architectures, including multilayer perceptrons,
functional multilayer perceptrons, and inception-based deep neural network,
were utilized to refine RUL predictions across centralized and federated data
settings. Coupled with a federated evaluation framework, these models enabled
a feasibility analysis of collaborative solutions, constraining the performance
comparisons of models built using isolated, centralized and federated data.
The evaluation addressed key challenges like data partitioning approaches and
non-independent and non-identically distributed data ( non-i.i.d.) data.
FedMulLabSync leverages multiple knowledge aggregation strategies,
synchronizing similar samples via their labels during federated optimization.
While parameter aggregation and neural matching are typically limited to
horizontal data partitioning, experimenting with these methods was essential
for tackling non-i.i.d. data challenges. By analyzing sample similarity across
multiple dimensions and implementing time-sharing and similarity-based label
synchronization, FedMulLabSync has evolved into a versatile federated learning
— 159 —

CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS AND FUTURE WORKS
solution, adaptable to both supervised and unsupervised learning tasks,
including classification and regression.
In practical applications, this versatility has been demonstrated across various
industries. In maritime industry, a collaborative prognostics setup allowed
for the enhancement of isolated vessel anomaly detection by classifying the
severity of sensor signal reconstruction deviations through unsupervised learning
with encoder-decoder neural networks. In manufacturing, failure detection
accuracy on Bosch production lines was improved through a collaborative
approach using supervised learning. These real-world applications showcase
the adaptability and robustness of FedMulLabSync, underlining its potential to
facilitate predictive maintenance solutions across diverse sectors, where accurate
condition monitoring is paramount.
Future works
Despite the promising outcomes demonstrated by FedMulLabSync across various
machine learning tasks and data partitioning settings, significant research areas
have been identified within the scope of prognostics and health management and
federated learning:
• Acquiring suitable run-to-failure datasets from real machines to
develop reliable RUL predictors. Although synthetic data from virtual
machines has aided in creating advanced AI solutions for health condition
predictions, there is growing interest in using real machine data to
complement and reduce reliance on synthetic data.
• Enhancing AI models with explainable features and predictive
uncertainty to support decision-making. Within the PHM scope, the
integration of AI-based and statistical methodologies has been growing,
leveraging their strengths in learning from large datasets while providing
predictive confidence.
• Assessing multiple FL challenges simultaneously to address
varied federated problem-solving. While this thesis primarily focused
on hybrid data partitioning and non-i.i.d. data for practical reasons,
federated solutions must tackle a broader range of challenges. These
include data heterogeneity, security vulnerabilities, potential biases from
non-representative local data, and communication and computation
overhead.

References
Other specific publications:
• Llasag Rosero, R., Silva, C., Ribeiro, B. “Federated approaches for
Remaining Useful Life prognosis” . 26th Portuguese Conference on Pattern
Recognition (RECPAD), 2020, Poster 47.
To quicken access to the content of the aforementioned publications, they are
categorized based on two criteria: the monitored machine and the configured
data partitioning. Considering the monitored equipment, publications can be
categorized as follows:
• Cooling units: J2 and C4.
• Turbofan engines: J1, J3, C5, C3, and C1.
• Maritime engines: C5.
Experiments using data from the last equipment were useful for evaluating
the feasibility of the proposed solution FedMulLabSync in collaborative problems
beyond the aviation industry. Access to data from maritime engines was granted
by the Winterthur Gas & Diesel Lda (WinGD) in collaboration with the ZHA W
University of Applied Sciences (Switzerland). Finally, considering the data
partitioning configured, the publications above can be categorized as follows:
• Monitoring systems from multiple parties not differing in monitoring
sensors: J1, J2, C4, C2 and C1.
• Monitoring systems from multiple parties differing in monitoring sensors:
J2 and C1.
